delayed indirect spreading
in
[
12
]
. As before, recall from Section
3.1
the definitions of
T_{loc}
,
J_{k}
,
\mathscr{C}_{t,i}
,
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{t,i}
,
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, the
\mathcal{S}
events, and the
\mathcal{W}_{i}(t)
events pertaining to a given star
i\in\mathscr{S}
, together with the definition of
[t,T]
- infection. As before recall the constants
\tilde{c}_{\lambda}
,
C_{\ref{survivalvertex}}
and
0<\mathfrak{c}_{0}<1
appearing in
Proposition
4
, and assume that
C_{\ref{survivalvertex}}>C_{\ref{theoslow}}
and
c_{\ref{theoslow}}\leq\tilde{c}_{\lambda}
. Further, assume that
\mathcal{A}_{0}
holds and defin e the sequences
\{\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{\mathscr{S}_{m}^{\prime}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
as in Section
3.2
. Once again, the main goal is to show that there are small constants
c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}},c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}^{\prime}>0
to be fixed later, with
c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}
independent of
a
,
\lambda
and
N
, and
c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}^{\prime}
independent of
N
such that

(28)

\mathbb{P}\big(|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m+1}|>c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN\,\big|\,|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|>c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN\big)\,\geq\,1-e^{-c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}^{\prime}N},

which together with a union bound gives slow extinction. Following the same reasoning as in the previous section we obtain

\mathbb{P}\Big(|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m+1}|>\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m}\Big)\,\geq\,\big(1-e^{-c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}N/10}\big){\mathsf{1}}_{\{|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|>c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN\}},

and (
28
) is automatically satisfied in the case
|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|\geq 4c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN
. Henceforth we may assume that
c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN<|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|\leq 4c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN
(once again choosing
c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}\leq\frac{1}{16}
). It is at this point that the proof differs from the previous one. Indeed, while in the case of
direct
spreading the proof relied on showing that most stars update in a ‘normal’ fashion, in this case the main idea is to show that the local survival of stars is sufficiently ‘synchronous’, meaning that during a fraction of the time we will always observe sufficiently many infected stars that can transmit their infections to connectors in
\mathscr{C}^{1}
. We begin by introducing a set of indices

\mathcal{J}:=5\mathbb{N}\cap[\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m,\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}(m+\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$})]

which are used to define time intervals of the form
[k,k+5]
with
k\in\mathcal{J}
. Using these indices we now define a new sequence of i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables
\bar{U}(i,k)
with
i\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{m}
and
k\in\mathcal{J}
such that

(29)

\bar{U}(i,k)=1\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\mathcal{R}^{i}\cap[k,k+5]=\emptyset,

and use these random variables to define the set of
synchronous
stars
\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}
as

\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}\,:=\,\Big\{i\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{m}\colon\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}\bar{U}(i,k)>\mbox{$\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}}{60e^{5}}$}\Big\}.

The definition of
\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}
given here is similar to the one introduced in Definition 5 in
[
12
]
. The main idea is that intervals
[k,k+5]
with sufficiently many stars satisfying
\bar{U}(i,k)=1
serve as time windows in which connectors can become infected. These connectors, however, need to be able to hold the infection in order to further infect more vertices. We thus define for every
k\in\mathcal{J}
the set

\mathscr{C}^{1}_{k}\,:=\,\left\{j\in\mathscr{C}^{1}\colon\mathcal{U}^{j}\cap[k+3,k+4]\neq\emptyset,\,(\mathcal{U}^{j}\cup\mathcal{R}^{j})\cap[k+4,k+5]=\emptyset\right\}

of
stable
connectors, which neither update nor recover throughout
[k+4,k+5]
but updated before. We claim that for any fixed
k\in\mathcal{J}
, a stable connector
j\in\mathscr{C}_{k}^{1}
is infected throughout
[k+4.5,k+5]
as soon as there is some
i\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{m}
for which:

(i)

\mathcal{R}^{i}\cap[k,k+5]=\emptyset
,

(ii)

i
is a neighbour of
j
at time
k+4
, and

(iii)

\mathcal{I}_{0}^{ij}\cap[k+4,k+4.5]\neq\emptyset
.

Indeed, suppose that
(i)
holds; because
i\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{m}
, by definition it must be
[\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m,\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}(m+1)]
-infected and hence it is either the case that there is an updating event
u\in[k,k+4]
at which
\mathcal{S}_{u}
holds so
i
is infected, or there are no updating events in this interval, in which case at the last updating event
u
we had
\mathcal{S}_{u}^{long}
and hence
\mathcal{W}_{i}(u+l)
holds for some
u+l\in[k,k+3]
which again means that
i
is infected at some time in
[k,k+4]
. Either way, because
\mathcal{R}^{i}\cap[k,k+5]=\emptyset
the infection of
i
is sustained throughout
[k+4,k+5]
and hence it follows directly from
(ii)
,
(iii)
that there is a valid infection event between
i
and
j
in
[k+4,k+4.5]
. By definition of
\mathscr{C}^{1}_{k}
,
j
does not recover throughout
[k+4,k+5]
, thus proving the claim.

Observe that a given
i\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{m}
belongs to
\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}
with probability at least
1-e^{-0.3\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}/20e^{5}}\geq\frac{1}{8}
, where the bound follows by choosing
C_{\ref{survivalvertex}}
large (depending on
\mathfrak{c}_{0}
). From this bound and Proposition
4
(fixing
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2}
) we thus obtain that

\mathbb{P}\big(i\in\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}\,\big|\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m}\big)\,\geq\,\frac{1}{16}

on the event
\{\bar{X}_{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m}^{i}(i)=1\}
and hence a large deviation argument gives

(30)

\mathbb{P}\big(|\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}|\geq\mbox{$\frac{c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}}{48}$}aN\,\big|\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m}\big)\,\geq\,\left(1-e^{-c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}N/80}\right){\mathsf{1}}_{\{|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|>c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN\}},

which is already a bound of the right form for slow extinction so we may as well assume that

|\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}|\geq\mbox{$\frac{c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}}{48}$}aN
as soon as
|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|>c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN
. Similarly, observe that since the connectors have index at least
N/2
we can safely say that each
j\in\mathscr{C}^{1}
belongs to
\mathscr{C}^{1}_{k}
independently with some probability
\varphi
depending on
\kappa_{0}
alone. Hence it follows from a large deviation argument that

(31)

\mathbb{P}(|\mathscr{C}_{k}^{1}|\geq\varphi N/3)\,\geq\,1-e^{-0.3\varphi N}

which is again of the right form for slow extinction so we again assume that
|\mathscr{C}_{k}^{1}|\geq\varphi N/3
for all
k\in\mathcal{J}
. Write
\mathcal{B}
for the event
\{|\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}|\geq\mbox{$\frac{c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}}{48}$}aN\}\cap\{(|\mathscr{C}_{k}^{1}|\geq\varphi N/3),\;\forall k\in\mathcal{J}\}
in which there are many synchronous stars to spread the infection, and stable connectors to receive it.
Define now the index set

\mathcal{K}:=\left\{k\in\mathcal{J}\colon\big|\{i\in\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m},\bar{U}(i,k)=1\}\big|\geq\mbox{$\frac{|\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}|}{12e^{5}}$}\right\},

which corresponds to the set of intervals at which we can find enough stars in
\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}
that do not recover. Using the previous claim and the definitions of
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{K}
we deduce that there is some constant
c
independent of
\lambda
,
a
and
N
such that

(32)

\mathbb{P}\big(|\{j\in\mathscr{C}^{1}_{k}\colon X_{k+4.5}(j)=1\}|\geq\mbox{$\frac{c\varphi\lambda ap(a,1)N}{9}$}\,\big|\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m},\mathcal{B},k\in\mathcal{K}\big)\,\geq\,1-e^{-0.1\varphi c\lambda ap(a,1)N}

on the event
\{|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|>c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN\}
. Indeed, for any
j\in\mathscr{C}^{1}_{k}
and
i\in\mathscr{S}_{m}^{s}
satisfying
\mathcal{R}^{i}\cap[k,k+5]=\emptyset
the probability of satisfying
(ii)
and
(iii)
is equal to

\tfrac{p(a,1)}{N}(1-e^{-\lambda/2})\,\geq\,\tfrac{\lambda p(a,1)}{4N}

because by definition
j
updated during
[k+3,k+4]
. Since
\mathcal{B}
holds there are many synchronous stars, and hence using the definition of
\mathcal{K}
we conclude from a large deviation argument and the previous claim that any given
j\in\mathscr{C}^{1}_{k}
is infected at time
k+4.5
with probability at least

1-\left(1-\tfrac{\lambda p(a,1)}{4N}\right)^{\frac{c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}}{600e^{5}}aN}\,\geq\,1-\exp\left(-\tfrac{c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}\lambda ap(a,1)}{2400e^{5}}\right)\,\geq\,c\lambda ap(a,1),

where
c=1/4800e^{5}
. Hence (
32
) follows from the bound on
|\mathscr{C}_{k}^{1}|
and yet another large deviation argument.

So far we have seen that for any given
k\in\mathcal{K}
with high probability there are of order
\lambda ap(a,1)N
infected connectors at time
k+4.5
. However, in order to spread the infection we need to consider all these sources of infections at different times
k\in\mathcal{K}
simultaneously. For that purpose we introduce the set
\mathscr{P}_{m}
of pairs
(k,j)
as follows

\mathscr{P}_{m}\,:=\,\left\{(k,j)\colon k\in\mathcal{K},\,j\in\mathscr{C}^{1}_{k},\,X_{k+4.5}(j)=1\right\}.

To lower bound the number of synchronous times in
\mathcal{K}
we use a double count to deduce

\mbox{$\frac{|\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}|\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}}{60e^{5}}$}\leq\sum_{i\in\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}\bar{U}(i,k)\leq|\mathcal{K}||\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}|+(\mbox{$\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}}{10}$}-|\mathcal{K}|)\mbox{$\frac{|\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}|}{12e^{5}}$},

where the left inequality follows from the definition of

\mathscr{S}^{s}_{m}
, and the right one from the fact that there are at most
\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}}{10}
intervals in
[\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m,\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}(m+\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$})]
, and the definition of
\mathcal{K}
. We conclude that
|\mathcal{K}|\geq\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}/240e^{5}
. Putting this together with (
30
), (
31
), and (
32
) gives

(33)

\mathbb{P}\big(|\mathscr{P}_{m}|\geq\mbox{$\frac{c\varphi\lambda ap(a,1)\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}N}{720e^{5}}$}\,\big|\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m}\big)\,\geq\,1-e^{-c_{\eqref{enoughpairs}}N},

for some
c_{\eqref{enoughpairs}}>0
independent of
N
, on the event
\{|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|>c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN\}
. In order to conclude (
28
) we need to show that stars in
\mathscr{S}\setminus\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}
have a sufficiently large chance of getting infected by the sources of infections represented by the pairs time-connector in
\mathscr{P}_{m}
. Fix
i\in\mathscr{S}\setminus\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}
and observe that in order for
i
to get infected in
[\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m,\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}(m+\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$})]
it is enough to find
(k,j)\in\mathscr{P}_{m}
such that

(i)

j
is a neighbour of
i
at time
k+4
, and

(ii)

\mathcal{I}_{0}^{i,j}\cap[k+4.5,k+5]\neq\emptyset
.

Indeed, by definition of
\mathscr{C}^{1}_{k}
the connector
j
does not recover not update throughout
[k+4.5,k+5]
so any infection towards
i
in this interval is valid. Once again the probability of satisfying these conditions for a given pair
(k,j)
is at least

\frac{\lambda p(a,1)}{5N}
and since the state of the edges
\{i,j\}
at times
k+4.5
are independent across the
j
and
k
(since by definition the connector updated in the interval
[k+3,k+4]
), a large deviation argument yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists t\in[\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m,\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}(m+\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$})]\mbox{ with }X_{t}(i)=1\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m},\,|\mathscr{P}_{m}|\geq\mbox{$\frac{c\varphi\lambda ap(a,1)\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}N}{720e^{5}}$}\,\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\,1-\left(1-\tfrac{\lambda p(a,1)}{4N}\right)^{\mbox{$\frac{c\varphi\lambda ap(a,1)\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}N}{720e^{5}}$}}\,\geq 1-\exp\left(\mbox{$\frac{c\varphi\lambda^{2}ap^{2}(a,1)\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}}{7000e^{5}}$}\right)

on the event
\{|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|>c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN\ \mbox{ and }i\in\mathscr{S}\setminus\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}\}
. From the hypothesis of the Theorem we know that
\lambda^{2}ap^{2}(a,1)T_{loc}>M_{(iv)}
, and the constants
\mathfrak{c}_{0}
,
c
and
\varphi
do not depend on
a
,
\lambda

or
N
, so taking
M_{(iv)}
large enough we finally obtain

P\Big(\exists t\in[\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m,\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}(m+\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$})],\;X_{t}(i)=1\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m},\,|\mathscr{P}_{m}|\geq\mbox{$\frac{c\varphi\lambda ap(a,1)\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}N}{720e^{5}}$}\Big)\,\geq\,\frac{2}{3}

on the event
\{|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|>c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN\mbox{ and }i\in\mathscr{S}\setminus\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}\}
. From this point onwards the proof is the same as the one in the previous section; defining
\tau_{i}
as the first time in
[\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m,\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}(m+\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$})]
that
X_{\tau_{i}}^{i}(i)=1
we flip a coin to ask whether
i
is
[\tau_{i},\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}(m+1)]
-infected which in particular implies that
i
is infected at time
\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}(m+1)
and hence
i\in\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m+1}
. Using (
24
) with
t=\tau_{i}
we conclude that

\mathbb{P}\big(i\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}_{m+1}\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m},\,|\mathscr{P}_{m}|\geq\mbox{$\frac{c\varphi\lambda ap(a,1)\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}N}{720e^{5}}$}\big)\,\geq\,\tfrac{1}{36}\cdot{\mathsf{1}}_{\{c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN<|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|\,\mbox{ and }i\in\mathscr{S}\setminus\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}\}.}

Finally, on the event
\{c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN<|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|\leq 4c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN\}
we have
|\mathscr{S}\setminus\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|\geq\frac{aN}{4}
and it follows from a large deviation bound together with (
33
) that

\mathbb{P}\big(|\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}_{m+1}|\geq\mbox{$\frac{aN}{320}$}\,\big|\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}m}\big)\,\geq\,\big(1-e^{-c^{\prime}N}\big){\mathsf{1}}_{\{c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN<|\tilde{\mathscr{S}}_{m}|\leq 4c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}aN\}}

for some
c^{\prime}>0
independent of
N
. The result then follows by taking
c_{\eqref{recursivevertex2}}<\mbox{$\frac{1}{320}$}
. The corresponding lower bound for the metastable density follows the exact same proof as in the previous section so we omit it.

3.4.
Slow extinction and optimal strategies

In this section we prove Theorem
1

in the case
\eta<0
, that is, we show how to use the results from the previous sections to deduce slow extinction and find an upper bound for the metastability exponent
for both the factor and preferential attachment kernels. More precisely,
for each kernel and survival strategy we describe the parameters
\eta,\tau
for which the strategy is available, with the union of these parameter sets constituting the region of slow extinction. As for any strategy the metastable density is bounded from lower by
\lambda ap(a,1)
, which is increasing in
a
, we only need to obtain the maximal value of
a
for which the strategy succeeds; this gives the best possible lower bound for the strategy and comparing all strategies we find the optimal strategy and the corresponding upper bound on the metastability exponent. In all cases, the maximal
a
is a power of
\lambda
and hence the condition
a\leq e^{{1}/{\lambda}}
appearing in Theorem
2
is automatically satisfied so we disregard it throughout this section.

3.4.1.
The factor kernel

Recall that for the factor kernel
p(a,a)=a^{-2\gamma}
and
p(a,1)=a^{-\gamma}
which we use to analyse the survival mechanisms separately:

•

Quick direct spreading:
The condition ensuring the mechanism works is that
\lambda a^{1-2\gamma}>M_{(i)}
, which is possible if
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
with
a
of order
a_{qds}=\lambda^{\frac{1}{2\gamma-1}+o(1)}
.

•

Quick indirect spreading:
The condition ensuring the mechanism works is that
\lambda^{2}a^{1-2\gamma}>M_{(ii)}
, which is possible if
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
with
a
of order
a_{qis}=\lambda^{\frac{2}{2\gamma-1}+o(1)}
.

•

Delayed direct spreading:
For this mechanism two conditions need to be met,

(34)

\lambda^{2}a^{-\gamma}\geq C_{\ref{theoslow}}\log(\tfrac{1}{a\lambda})\quad\text{and}\quad(1\wedge\lambda a^{\gamma\eta})a^{1-3\gamma}\lambda^{2}>M_{(iii)}.

The mechanism is available as soon as
\gamma>\frac{1}{3}
.
The metastability exponent analysis needs to be handled by dividing it into different subcases.

–

Suppose the first condition in (
34
) is the most restrictive one. Maximizing
a
subject to this condition gives
a
of order
a_{ls}=\lambda^{{2}/{\gamma}+o(1)}
.
Here the actual spreading mechanism does not affect the exponent.
To check that the former succeeds we still require the second condition in (
34
) to hold. Replacing
a_{ls}
in this condition gives

(1\wedge\lambda^{1+2\eta-o(1)})\lambda^{\frac{2}{\gamma}-4-o(1)}=\lambda^{(0\vee(1+2\eta))+\frac{2}{\gamma}-4-o(1)}>M_{(iii)}

We get the restrictions
\gamma\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
\gamma\geq\frac{2}{3-2\eta}
.

–

Suppose now that the second condition in (
34
) is the most restrictive one while at the same time
\lambda a^{\gamma\eta}\leq 1
holds. The maximal
a
is of order

a_{dds}=\lambda^{\frac{3}{3\gamma-1-\gamma\eta}+o(1)}

Since we now require that both
\lambda^{2}a^{-\gamma}\geq C_{\ref{theoslow}}
and
\lambda a^{\gamma\eta}\leq 1
hold, substituting the expression for
a_{dds}
gives
the restrictions
\gamma\leq\frac{2}{3-2\eta}
and
\gamma>\frac{1}{3+2\eta}
.

–

Suppose that the second condition in (
34
) is the most restrictive one while
\lambda a^{\gamma\eta}\geq 1
holds, which is the case when depletion occurs. The maximal
a
in the condition
\lambda^{2}a^{1-3\gamma}>M_{(iii)}
is of order

a_{ddds}=\lambda^{\frac{2}{3\gamma-1}+o(1)}

We now require
\lambda^{2}a^{-\gamma}\geq C_{\ref{theoslow}}
and
\lambda a^{\gamma\eta}\geq 1
. Substituting the expression for
a_{ddds}
gives
the restrictions
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{3+2\eta}
.

•

Delayed indirect spreading:
As in the previous mechanism there are two conditions to be met, which after performing the same simplification as before are of the form

(35)

\lambda^{2}a^{-\gamma}\geq C_{\ref{theoslow}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lambda^{4}a^{1-3\gamma+\gamma\eta}\geq M_{(iv)}.

In this case the condition under which slow extinction holds is
\frac{1}{3-\eta}<\gamma
and for the metastability exponent we again treat separate scenarios:

–

Suppose that the first condition in (
35
) is the most restrictive one, so that the maximal
a
is of order
a_{ls}
. Substituting this in the second condition of (
35
) gives
the restriction
\gamma\geq\frac{1}{1-\eta}
.
Recall that delayed concurrent spreading is possible if both the direct and indirect spreading mechanisms succeed, which is the case if

\gamma\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
\gamma\geq\frac{1}{1-\eta}
.

–

Suppose now that the second condition in (
35
) is the most restrictive one, so that the maximal
a
is of order

a_{dis}=\lambda^{\frac{4}{3\gamma-1-\gamma\eta}+o(1)}

Substituting
a_{dis}
in the first condition of (
35
) gives
the restriction
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta}
.

For each one of the densities above we compute the corresponding lower bound for the metastable density
\lambda ap(a,1)=\lambda a^{1-\gamma}
and express both the result and the restrictions on the parameters in terms of
\eta
and
\tau=\frac{1}{\gamma}+1
. The information is summarized in Table
1
. The only thing left to do in order to conclude the first part of Theorem
1
is to compute which is the largest bound for the metastable density (or equivalent, which is the smallest exponent) in the region of slow extinction. This is an elementary exercise.

Table 1.
Density and restrictions per mechanism for the factor kernel

Mechanism

Density

Region

Quick direct spreading

\lambda^{\frac{1}{3-\tau}+o(1)}

R_{1}:=\{\tau<3\}

Quick indirect spreading

\lambda^{\frac{\tau-1}{3-\tau}+o(1)}

R_{1}=\{\tau<3\}

Local survival

\lambda^{2\tau-3+o(1)}

R_{2}:=\{\tau\leq(\tfrac{5}{2}-\eta)\wedge 3\}\cup\{\tau\leq 2-\eta\}

Delayed direct spreading

\lambda^{\frac{2\tau-2-\eta}{4-\tau-\eta}+o(1)}

R_{3}:=\{\tfrac{5}{2}-\eta<\tau\leq 4+2\eta\}

Delayed depleted direct spreading

\lambda^{\frac{\tau}{4-\tau}+o(1)}

R_{4}:=\{(4+2\eta)\vee 3\leq\tau<4\}

Delayed indirect spreading

\lambda^{\frac{3\tau-4-\eta}{4-\tau-\eta}+o(1)}

R_{5}:=\{2-\eta\leq\tau<4-\eta\}

3.4.2.
The preferential attachment kernel

Recall that for the preferential attachment kernel
p(a,a)=a^{-1}
and
p(a,1)=a^{-\gamma}
. Again we analyse the survival mechanisms separately.

•

Quick direct spreading:
In this case the condition ensuring the mechanism is
\lambda>M_{(i)}
, which fails for
\lambda
small for any choice of the parameters.

•

Quick indirect spreading:
In this case the condition ensuring the mechanism is
\lambda^{2}a^{1-2\gamma}>M_{(ii)}
which was already analysed for the factor kernel. It follows that the mechanism holds as soon as
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
and the maximal
a
is of order
a_{qis}
as before.

•

Delayed direct spreading:
For this mechanism the simplified conditions are

(36)

\lambda^{2}a^{-\gamma}\geq C_{\ref{theoslow}}\quad\text{and}\quad(1\wedge\lambda a^{\gamma\eta})\lambda^{2}a^{-\gamma}>M_{(iii)}.

Surprisingly, for this kernel the mechanism is available for all values
\gamma
and
\eta
, and we analyse the metastability exponent by considering two cases.

–

Suppose that the first condition in (
36
) is the most restrictive one, which shows that the maximal
a
is of order
a_{ls}
. Substituting
a_{ls}
into the second condition in (
36
) gives the restriction
\eta\leq-\frac{1}{2}
.

–

Suppose now that the second condition in (
36
) is the most restrictive one and further assume that
\lambda a^{\gamma\eta}\leq 1
since otherwise we fall into the previous case. Maximizing
a
in the condition
\lambda^{3}a^{-\gamma+\gamma\eta}>M_{(iii)}
gives the order

a_{dds}=\lambda^{\frac{3}{\gamma-\gamma\eta}+o(1)}

Observe that the condition
\lambda^{2}a^{-\gamma}\geq C_{\ref{theoslow}}
is trivially satisfied, while replacing
a_{dis}
into the assumption gives the restriction
\eta\geq-\frac{1}{2}
.

•

Delayed indirect spreading:
Since the kernel appears in the conditions only through
p(a,1)=a^{-\gamma}
the analysis of this mechanism is the same as for the factor kernel and hence we have that the mechanism holds as soon as
\frac{1}{3-\eta}<\gamma
.

–

If
\gamma\geq\frac{1}{1-\eta}
then the maximal
a
is of order
a_{ls}=\lambda^{\frac{2}{\gamma}+o(1)}
. Hence delayed concurrent spreading is possible if
\eta\leq-\frac{1}{2}
and
\gamma\geq\frac{1}{1-\eta}
.

–

If
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta}
then the maximal
a
is of order
a_{dis}=\lambda^{\frac{4}{3\gamma-1-\gamma\eta}+o(1)}
.

As in the previous section we compute the corresponding lower bound for the metastable density
\lambda ap(a,1)=\lambda a^{1-\gamma}
for each mechanism and express both the result and the restrictions on the parameters in terms of
\eta
and
\tau=\frac{1}{\gamma}+1
, which are summarized in Table
2
.

Table 2.
Densities and restrictions per mechanism

Mechanism

Density

Region

Quick indirect spreading

\lambda^{\frac{\tau-1}{3-\tau}+o(1)}

R_{1}:=\{\tau\leq 3\}

Local survival

\lambda^{2\tau-3+o(1)}

R_{2}:=\{\tau\leq 2-\eta\}\cup\{\eta\leq-\frac{1}{2}\}

Delayed direct spreading

\lambda^{\frac{3\tau-5-\eta}{1-\eta}+o(1)}

R_{3}:=\{\eta\geq-\frac{1}{2}\}

Delayed indirect spreading

\lambda^{\frac{3\tau-4-\eta}{4-\tau-\eta}+o(1)}

R_{4}:=\{2-\eta\leq\tau<4-\eta\}

We observe that among the delayed mechanisms the density associated to local survival is the largest and hence dominates in the domain
R_{2}
where it is feasible.
It is easy to calculate that delayed indirect spreading dominates
delayed direct spreading if
\tau\leq\frac{8}{3}+\frac{\eta}{3}
and quick indirect spreading only prevails if no other strategy is available. This completes the proof of Theorem
1
in the case
\eta<0
.

3.5.
Fast extinction phase

Recall the distinction between fast extinction, where the expected extinction time grows as a power of
N
, and ultra-fast extinction, where it grows subpolynomially.
Both for the factor kernel and the preferential attachment kernel ultra-fast extinction occurs if
\eta\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
\tau>3
, which follows directly from Theorem 2 in
[
12
]
, which also gives a polynomial upper bound for the extinction time expectation in the fast extinction regimes for
\eta\geq 0
. For
\eta<0
an appropriate upper bound will follow from Theorem
3
in the next section. In order to conclude that no ultra-fast extinction occurs unless

\eta\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
\tau>3
, we need a lower bound for the expected extinction time, which we provide here in the case
\eta<0
. The case
\eta\geq 0
follows from analogous ideas together with the results found in Sections 4.3 and 4.4 in
[
12
]
.

Lemma 2
.

Let
p
be any kernel satisfying (
1
) and
\eta<0
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
there are constants
\tilde{c}_{\lambda}
and
c_{\ref{lastlemma}}
such that for any
\lambda<\tilde{c}_{\lambda}
and all
N
large,

\mathbb{P}\big(T_{\rm{ext}}\,\geq\,\mbox{$\frac{\lambda c_{\ref{lastlemma}}N^{\gamma(1-\eta)}}{\log N}$}\,\big|\,X_{0}\equiv 1\big)=1-o(1)

Remark 11
.

Observe that the exponent given does not necessarily match the one from Theorem
3
below. Finding the exact exponent is an interesting question which could shed light onto the behaviour in the fast extinction regime. We do not pursue this here.

Proof.

Fix
i=\frac{1}{N}
which is the most powerful vertex in the network, and observe that it is initially infected since
X_{0}\equiv 1
. For this choice of
a
we have from (
1
) that
p(a,1)\geq\mathfrak{c}_{1}a^{-\gamma}=\mathfrak{c}_{1}N^{\gamma}
and
\kappa(a)=\kappa_{0}N^{\gamma\eta}
it then follows from Proposition
4
together with Remark
10
that for any
\varepsilon>0
there is a choice of
\tilde{c}_{\lambda}
and
\mathfrak{c}_{0}
such that

\mathbb{P}(i\text{ is }[0,\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}]\text{-infected}\,\big|\,X_{0}\equiv 1,\mathfrak{F})\,\geq\,1-\varepsilon,

on the event
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Now, in this particular case

T_{loc}=\frac{\lambda p(a,1)}{\kappa(a)\log(p(a,1))}=\frac{c\lambda N^{\gamma(1-\eta)}}{\log(N)},

for some constant
c
. Taking
c_{\ref{lastlemma}}=c\mathfrak{c}_{0}
and observing that from (
8
) we already have that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{0})>1-e^{-c_{\eqref{localstable}}N}
, the result then follows from the observation that by time
\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}
the vertex
i
is infected and hence
T_{ext}>\mathfrak{c}_{0}T_{loc}
.
∎

4.
Upper bounds

4.1.
Upper bound by local approximation

Proposition 8 of
[
13
]
can be easily adapted to our situation and yields the following result.

Proposition 5
.

Suppose
\eta\leq 0
. There is a constant
C_{\eqref{static_upperdens}}>0
such that, for all
\lambda>0
, the upper metastable density
\rho^{+}(\lambda)
satisfies

(37)

\rho^{+}(\lambda)\leq\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{ll}C_{\eqref{static_upperdens}}\lambda^{\frac{1}{3-\tau}}&\!\!\mbox{in the case of the factor kernel with }\tau\leq\frac{5}{2},\\[5.69054pt]
C_{\eqref{static_upperdens}}\lambda^{2\tau-3}&\!\!\mbox{in the case of either}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mbox{the preferential attachment kernel,}\\
\mbox{the factor kernel with }\tau>\frac{5}{2}.\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

This gives the right upper bound appearing in Theorem
1
only in the regions
R_{2}
introduced in Section
3.4
for both kernels. The remaining regions are covered in next section.

4.2.
Upper bound by the supermartingale technique

We complete the proof of Theorem
1
by showing the following two propositions.

Proposition 6
.

Suppose
\eta<0
. There is a constant
C_{\ref{supermartingale_upperbound}}>0

such that the following holds:

(a)

For the factor kernel,

(i)

if
\tau>4-\eta
, there is fast extinction;

(ii)

if
\frac{5}{2}-\eta\leq\tau<4+2\eta
, the upper metastable density satisfies

\rho^{+}(\lambda)\leq C_{\ref{supermartingale_upperbound}}\lambda^{\frac{2\tau-2-\eta}{4-\eta-\tau}}

(iii)

If
3\wedge(4+2\eta)\leq\tau\leq 4+\eta
, the upper metastable density satisfies

\rho^{+}(\lambda)\leq C_{\ref{supermartingale_upperbound}}\lambda^{\frac{\tau}{4-\tau}}

(iv)

If
(2-\eta)\wedge(4+2\eta)\leq\tau<4-\eta
, the upper metastable density satisfies

\rho^{+}(\lambda)\leq C_{\ref{supermartingale_upperbound}}\lambda^{\frac{3\tau-2-\eta}{4-\tau-\eta}}

(b)

For the preferential attachment kernel and
-\frac{1}{2}<\eta<0
the upper metastable density satisfies:

\rho^{+}(\lambda)\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}C_{\ref{supermartingale_upperbound}}\lambda^{\frac{3\tau-4-\eta}{4-\eta-\tau}}&\mbox{if }2-\eta\leq\tau\leq\frac{8}{3}+\frac{\eta}{3},\\
C_{\ref{supermartingale_upperbound}}\lambda^{\frac{3\tau-5-\eta}{1-\eta}}&\mbox{if }\tau\geq\frac{8}{3}+\frac{\eta}{3}.\end{array}\right.!

Proposition 7
.

Suppose
\eta\geq 1/2
and
\tau>3
. Then for the factor or preferential attachment kernel, there is ultra-fast extinction.

As in
[
12
, Theorem 2]
or
[
13
, Theorem 5]
the basic approach for the proof of Propositions
6
and
7
is to use a function
s\colon(0,1]\to[1,\infty)
associated with the model and a functional inequality satisfied by
s
to define a supermartingale, which in first approximation associates the score
s(i/N)
to each infected vertex
i
. The optional stopping theorem gives an upper bound on the time when the total score hits zero. The main difficulties in this approach are:

•

Choosing an appropriate functional inequality.
There is no ready-made recipe, although the inequalities we get, namely (
41
), (
42
), and (
48
)–(
50
), are directly inspired by understanding of the local survival and spreading mechanisms studied in Section
3
.

•

Defining the supermartingale.
This is hard as the underlying Markov process of network evolution and infection has a large and complex state space with a sophisticated transition structure depending on the network geometry. To deal with this we introduce an exploration process which only partially reveals the network structure depending on the infection paths. Again, we are guided by our understanding of the survival mechanisms.

Theorem
3
below allows us to prove Proposition
7
and to treat the case of the factor kernel in Proposition
6
(a),(i) and (ii), and of the preferential attachment kernel in Proposition
6
(b).
Theorem
4
is considerably more difficult to prove as it introduces a new ‘hybrid’ approach where we treat low-degree vertices locally as in a static network and include this treatment in our global supermartingale approach. The combination of local and global ideas is the main technical innovation of this paper. Theorem
4
allows us to treat the case of the factor kernel in Proposition
6
(a)-(iii) and (iv).

Definition 3
.

Define
\pi\colon(0,1)\to(0,\infty)
and the time-scale function
T^{\rm{loc}}\colon(0,1)\rightarrow[1,\infty)
as

(38)

\displaystyle\pi(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{p(x,y)}{\kappa(x)+\kappa(y)}\,\mathrm{d}y,

(39)

\displaystyle T^{\rm{loc}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max\left\{8,\frac{8}{3\kappa(x)},16\lambda^{2}\frac{\pi(x)}{\kappa(x)}\right\}.

For given
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, the time
T^{\rm{loc}}_{j}:=T^{\rm{loc}}(j/N)
should be interpreted as an upper bound for the time during which the infection can survive locally around
j
using only infections of its neighbours and direct reinfection of
j
from these neighbours,
but the precise definition of
T^{\rm{loc}}
comes from the statements and proofs of Theorems
3
and
4
below.
The definition of
T^{\rm{loc}}
is of course to be compared with the definition of
T_{\rm{loc}}
by (
6
). In many cases we have
\pi(x)\asymp p(x,1)\asymp x^{-\gamma}
all of the same order, so for strong vertices we will typically have
T^{\rm{loc}}_{j}\asymp\lambda^{2}(j/N)^{-\gamma}\kappa(j/N)^{-1}\asymp T_{\rm{loc}}(j/N,\lambda)
all of the same order, which is an indication that in these cases
T^{\rm{loc}}
is an accurate upper bound.

In Theorem
3
below, we will say a vertex
j
is
quick
if it updates at rate
\kappa(j/N)\geq\lambda
and
slow
if it updates at rate
\kappa(j/N)<\lambda
. When
\eta\geq 0
, we assume for simplicity
\lambda\leq\kappa_{0}
so all vertices are quick. When
\eta<0
, a vertex is slow if
j/N<a_{\rm{slow}}
, where
a_{\rm{slow}}
is defined as

(40)

a_{\rm{slow}}=\left(\frac{\lambda}{\kappa_{0}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\gamma\eta}}.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
and suppose there exists some
a=a(\lambda)\geq 0
and some non-increasing function
s:[a,1]\rightarrow[1,\infty)
, or
s:(0,1]\rightarrow[1,\infty)
if
a=0
, such that for all
x>a
, we have

(41)

7T^{\rm{loc}}(x)\int_{0}^{1}\lambda p(x,y)s(y\vee a)\,\mathrm{d}y\leq s(x)

if
\eta\geq 0
or
x\geq a_{\rm{slow}}
, or

(42)

7\ T^{\rm{loc}}(x)\left(\int_{0}^{a_{\rm{slow}}}(\kappa(x)\vee\kappa(y))p(x,y)s(y\vee a)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{a_{\rm{slow}}}^{1}\lambda p(x,y)s(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\right)\leq s(x)

if
\eta<0
and
x<a_{\rm{slow}}
.

(1)

If
a=0
, then the expected extinction time is at most linear in
N
and in particular there is fast extinction. More precisely, writing
(H)_{\delta}
for the hypothesis that
T^{\rm{loc}}s^{-\delta}
is a bounded function, we have

(a)

(H)_{1}
is a consequence of (
41
) and (
42
).

(b)

If
(H)_{\delta}
is satisfied for some
\delta\in(0,1]
, then there is
\omega=\omega(\lambda,\delta)
such that for all
N
,

(43)

\mathbb{E}\big[T_{\rm ext}\big]\;\leq\;\omega N^{\delta}.

Combining with (a) we have that
\mathbb{E}[T_{\rm ext}]
is always at most linear in
N
.

(c)

(H)_{0}
is satisfied exactly when
\eta\geq 1/2
, and in that case there is ultra-fast extinction. More precisely, there is
\omega=\omega(\lambda,\delta)
such that for all
N
,

\mathbb{E}\big[T_{\rm ext}\big]\;\leq\;\omega\log N.

(2)

If
a>0
then there exists
\omega=\omega(a,\lambda)>0

such that, for all large
N
and all
t\geq 0
,

(44)

I_{N}(t)\;\leq\;a+\frac{7}{6s(a)}\int_{a}^{1}s(y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\frac{\omega}{t}+\frac{1}{N}.

In particular, if there is metastability, then the upper metastable density satisfies

(45)

\rho^{+}(\lambda)\leq a(\lambda)+\frac{7}{6s(a(\lambda))}\int_{a(\lambda)}^{1}s(y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Note that Theorem 2 in
[
12
]
is similar to Theorem
3
here, but it holds only for
\eta\geq 0
, and always requires a condition similar to (
41
) (with only a different multiplicative constant in the left-hand side of the inequality), irrespectively of whether the vertex is quick or slow. Inequality (
41
) would actually be the natural inequality to look at if we were considering a model of infection without any underlying graph, where an infected vertex
i
could infect any other vertex
j
with the ‘temporal mean rate’
\lambda p_{ij}\leq\lambda p(i/N,j/N)/N
, and could recover at rate
1/(7T^{\rm{loc}}_{i})
instead of one. It is remarkable that our model can in this sense be upper bounded by a model without any underlying graph, or more precisely where the only manifestation of the underlying geometry lies in the introduction of this ‘local survival time’
T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}
at each vertex. The fact that Theorem 2 in
[
12
]
only requires a condition like (
41
) is also an indication that this theorem takes into account adequately the degradation and regeneration effects discussed in the introduction. In a sense, our Theorem
3
generalizes Theorem 2 of
[
12
]
with the following additional ingredients:

(i)

The local time
T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}
is appropriately extended to negative values of
\eta
and thus takes into account the weaker degradation effect for slow evolutions.

(ii)

The depletion effect is now taken into account by requiring only Inequality (
42
) to be satisfied for slow vertices, instead of Inequality (
41
).

Theorem
3
is quite strong but still has a weakness. For weak vertices with typical degree smaller than
\lambda^{-2}
, we might still have
T^{\rm{loc}}
large, which does not reflect well the fact that reinfections should be rare and the local survival effect should be nonexistent.

Theorem
4
below provides a special treatment for these weak vertices. It requires to first distinguish weak and strong vertices, and then distinguish strong quick and strong slow vertices. More precisely, we define

(46)

\displaystyle a_{\rm{str}}

\displaystyle:=\sup\Big\{x\in(0,1],\colon\int_{0}^{1}p(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y>\tfrac{1}{10\lambda^{2}}\Big\},

(47)

\displaystyle a_{\rm{stsl}}

\displaystyle:=a_{\rm{str}}\wedge a_{\rm{slow}},

and say a vertex
j
is
strong slow
if
j/N<a_{\rm{stsl}}
,
strong quick
if
a_{\rm{stsl}}\leq j/N<a_{\rm{str}}
and
weak
if
a_{\rm{str}}\leq j/N
. Note there is no strong quick vertex if
a_{\rm{stsl}}=a_{\rm{str}}
.

Theorem 4
.

There exists a large constant
\mathfrak{c}
such that the following holds: Let
\eta\leq 0
and suppose
there exists some
a=a(\lambda)>0
and some non-increasing function
s:[a,1]\rightarrow[1,\infty)
such that the following master inequality is satisfied: for all
x>a
,

(48)

\mathfrak{c}\ \int_{0}^{1}\lambda p(x,y)s(y\vee a)\,\mathrm{d}y\leq s(x)

if
x\geq a_{\rm{str}}
, or

(49)

\mathfrak{c}\ T^{\rm{loc}}(x)\int_{0}^{1}\lambda\ p(x,y)s(y\vee a)\,\mathrm{d}y\leq s(x)

if
a_{\rm{stsl}}\leq x<a_{\rm{str}}
, or

(50)

\mathfrak{c}\ T^{\rm{loc}}(x)\left(\int_{0}^{a_{\rm{stsl}}}(\kappa(x)\vee\kappa(y))p(x,y)s(y\vee a)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{a_{\rm{stsl}}}^{1}\lambda p(x,y)s(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\right)\leq s(x)

if
x<a_{\rm{stsl}}
. Suppose also the following technical assumptions are satisfied:

(H1)

\displaystyle s(a_{\rm{str}})\int_{y<a_{\rm{str}}}p(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\leq s(x)\quad\text{ for all $x>a_{\rm{str}}$ and }

(H2)

there exists some
\alpha>0
and
c_{\eqref{cond:H2}}>0
not depending on
\lambda
, such that

(51)

\displaystyle s(a_{\rm{str}})

\displaystyle\leq c_{\eqref{cond:H2}}\lambda^{-3+\alpha},

(52)

\displaystyle s(a_{\rm{str}})

\displaystyle\leq c_{\eqref{cond:H2}}\lambda^{-1+\alpha}\inf\big\{\tfrac{s(x)}{T^{\rm{loc}}(x)}\colon x<a_{\rm{str}}\big\}.

Suppose finally the functions
a^{-1}
and
s(a)
have polynomial growth
at 0, in the sense that they are bounded by
\lambda^{-\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}
when
\lambda\to 0
for some
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}>0
. Then, there exists
\omega=\omega(\lambda)>0
and
\omega^{\prime}=\omega^{\prime}(\lambda)>0
such that, for all
N
and all
t\geq 0
, we have, for small
\lambda
,

(53)

I_{N}(t)\;\leq\;2a+\frac{4}{3s(a)}\int_{a}^{1}s(y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\frac{\omega}{t}+\frac{\omega^{\prime}}{N}.

If there is metastability, we get that the upper metastable density satisfies, for small
\lambda>0
,

(54)

\rho^{+}(\lambda)\leq 2a(\lambda)+\frac{4}{3s(a(\lambda))}\int_{a(\lambda)}^{1}s(y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

We postpone the proof of these theorems to Section
5
, and check here how they can be used to deduce Propositions
6
and
7
. In order to use Theorems
3
and
4
, we have to determine a level
a
and a function
s
satisfying the hypotheses of the theorems and providing the required upper bounds. The way we do this is led by two complementary principles. First, a purely analytic approach, when the conditions required by Theorems
3
and
4
lead to optimal or natural choices. Second, the comparison of the approach with the lower bounds. In the lower bounds, we were seeking for the largest possible threshold
a
such that the stars
\mathscr{S}:=\{1,\ldots,\lfloor aN\rfloor\}
are typically infected in the metastable state. By contrast, in this upper bound section we seek for the smallest possible threshold
a
such that the contact process shows a subcritical behaviour while infecting only vertices in
\{\lfloor aN\rfloor+1,\ldots,N\}
. We thus expect that the
a
we use in this section to be larger than the
a
used in the lower bounds section,
and our aim is to make them of the same order.

To avoid cluttered notation, we henceforth assume
\beta=1
in the definition of the kernels. Recall that
\tau\in(2,\infty)
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
are linked by
\tau=1+1/\gamma
.
To shorten computations, we write
f\lesssim g
if the positive functions
f
and
g
of
\lambda
satisfy that
f/g
is bounded when
\lambda\to 0
, and similarly
f\gtrsim g
or
f\asymp g
.
We will use repeatedly that

\int_{a}^{b}y^{r-1}\mathrm{d}y\asymp a^{r}+b^{r}
when the parameter
r
is different from 0. Of course, when the sign of
r
is known, only one of the two terms
a^{r}
or
b^{r}
has to be kept. For simplicity we disregard in our computations the cases involving a parameter
r=0
. These cases would induce a logarithmic factor coming from the integration of the harmonic function, but this logarithmic factor never concerns the leading term of the quantities we are estimating and can therefore be omitted.

Application of Theorem
3
to the preferential attachment kernel.

When applying Theorem
3
,
we actually check (
41
) for all
x
irrespectively of whether
x\geq a_{\rm{slow}}
or
x<a_{\rm{slow}}
, so as to ease computations
1
1

1

Checking instead the less demanding Condition (
42
) for slow vertices would actually provide slightly better upper bounds, but only in regimes where our optimal upper bound requires Theorem
4
anyway.
.
Recall that Proposition
6
assumes
\eta\in(-\frac{1}{2},0)
, and under this hypothesis, we have for
c_{3}=\mbox{$\frac{1}{1-\gamma}$}+\mbox{$\frac{1}{\gamma+\gamma\eta}$}
and
y\in(0,1]
,

\pi(y)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{p(y,z)}{\kappa(y)+\kappa(z)}\mathrm{d}z\leq c_{3}y^{-\gamma}.

Introducing
f(x)=s(x)/T^{\rm{loc}}(x)
Inequality (
41
) is equivalent to

\Delta_{a}(f)(x)\leq f(x)
for all
x\geq a,

where we have defined the functional
\Delta_{a}
by

\Delta_{a}(f)(x):=8\lambda\int_{0}^{1}p(x,y)f(y\vee a)T^{\rm{loc}}(y\vee a)\,\mathrm{d}y.

The assumption
\eta<0
yields

T^{\rm{loc}}(y)\asymp y^{\gamma\eta}+\lambda^{2}y^{-\gamma+\gamma\eta}.

In our pursuit to match the dominant strategies in Section
3.4.2
we now suppose
a(\lambda)\lesssim\lambda^{2/\gamma}\approx a_{ls}
(where
a_{ls}
was introduced in Section
3.4.1
). With this assumption, when
y=a
the last estimate simplifies to

T^{\rm{loc}}(a)\asymp\lambda^{2}a^{-\gamma+\gamma\eta}.

In case of the preferential attachment kernel we get, for
x\geq a
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{a}(f)(x)\asymp

\displaystyle\quad\lambda x^{\gamma-1}\int_{0}^{a}a^{-\gamma}f(a)\;\lambda^{2}a^{-\gamma+\gamma\eta}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+\ \lambda x^{\gamma-1}\int_{a}^{x}y^{-\gamma}f(y)(y^{\gamma\eta}+\lambda^{2}y^{-\gamma+\gamma\eta})\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+\ \lambda x^{-\gamma}\int_{x}^{1}y^{\gamma-1}f(y)(y^{\gamma\eta}+\lambda^{2}y^{-\gamma+\gamma\eta})\mathrm{d}y.

Searching for a monomial
f(x)=x^{\alpha}
with
\alpha<0
, and assuming for simplicity that none of the four exponents
1-\gamma+\alpha+\gamma\eta
,
1-2\gamma+\alpha+\gamma\eta
,
\gamma+\alpha+\gamma\eta
, or
\alpha+\gamma\eta
, equals 0, we get

\Delta_{a}(f)(x)\sim\lambda x^{\alpha+\gamma\eta}+\lambda^{3}x^{\alpha-\gamma+\gamma\eta}+\lambda^{3}x^{\gamma-1}a^{\alpha+1-2\gamma+\gamma\eta}+\lambda x^{-\gamma}.

For
\Delta_{a}(f)(x)\leq f(x)
to hold for all
x\geq a,
we need

(i)
\lambda\lesssim a^{-\gamma\eta},

(ii)
\lambda^{3}\lesssim a^{\gamma(1-\eta)},

(iii)
\lambda^{3}\lesssim a^{2\gamma-1-\alpha-\gamma\eta},

(iv)
\lambda\lesssim a^{\alpha+\gamma}
,

(v)
\lambda^{4}\lesssim a^{3\gamma-1-\gamma\eta}.

Here (v) is just a consequence of (iii) and (iv). (i), (ii) and (v) do not depend on
\alpha
, and prevent us from choosing
a
too small. We now consider the different cases.

(1) The case
-\frac{1}{2}<\eta\leq 0
and
\tau>\frac{8}{3}+\frac{\eta}{3}
.

This is the delayed direct spreading phase, where
(i) and (v) are less restrictive than (ii), leading naturally to the choice (with
r
some constant to be defined later)

a(\lambda)=r\lambda^{\mbox{$\frac{3}{\gamma(1-\eta)}$}}.

(iii) requires
\alpha\geq\gamma-1
, while (iv) requires
\alpha\geq\frac{\gamma}{3}(\eta-2)
which is less restrictive under our assumptions, so we choose
\alpha=\gamma-1
, as (
45
) will then give the best possible upper bound for the upper metastable density. It is now a simple verification that choosing
r
large enough indeed ensures (
41
) is satisfied for small
\lambda>0
. Then (
45
) yields

\rho^{+}(\lambda)\lesssim\frac{1}{s(a(\lambda))}\lesssim\frac{1}{a^{\gamma-1}\lambda^{2}a^{-\gamma+\gamma\eta}}\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{3-2\gamma-\gamma\eta}{\gamma-\gamma\eta}}=\lambda^{\frac{3\tau-5-\eta}{1-\eta}}.

(2) The case
-\frac{1}{2}<\eta\leq 0
and
2-\eta\leq\tau\leq\frac{8}{3}+\frac{\eta}{3}
.

This is the delayed indirect spreading phase, where (i) and (ii) are less restrictive than (v), leading naturally to the choice (with
r
some constant to be defined later)

a(\lambda)=r\lambda^{\mbox{$\frac{4}{\gamma(3-\eta)-1}$}},

(iii) and (iv) is more restrictive than (iii), thus leading to the choice

\alpha=-\frac{\gamma+\gamma\eta+1}{4}.

Choosing
r
large we see that (
41
) is satisfied for small
\lambda>0
. Furthermore

a\lesssim\lambda^{2/\gamma}
holds as
\gamma<\frac{1}{1-\eta}.

Finally, (
45
) yields

\rho^{+}(\lambda)\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{3-\gamma-\gamma\eta}{3\gamma-\gamma\eta-1}}=\lambda^{\frac{3\tau-4-\eta}{4-\eta-\tau}}.

Application of Theorem
3
to the factor kernel.

As discussed previously, for the factor kernel we use Theorem
3
to prove the upper bounds in Proposition
6
only in the cases (a),(i) and (ii), so we we either assume
\frac{5}{2}-\eta\leq\tau<4+2\eta
or
4-\eta<\tau
. As in the previous analysis we check for Condition (
41
).
Replacing
p(x,y)=x^{-\gamma}y^{-\gamma}
, the left side of this inequality
factorizes as
D_{a}T^{\rm{loc}}(x)x^{-\gamma}
, where
D_{a}
does not depend on
x
and is given by

D_{a}:=8\lambda\int_{0}^{1}y^{-\gamma}s(a\vee y)\mathrm{d}y.

It is thus natural to choose the scoring function
s(x)=T^{\rm{loc}}(x)x^{-\gamma},
so that (
41
) becomes equivalent to
D_{a}\leq 1
.
From our assumptions we have
2-\eta<\tau
, so that

\pi(x)=O(x^{-\gamma})
and we get

(55)

D_{a}\lesssim\lambda+\lambda a^{1-2\gamma+\gamma\eta}+\lambda^{3}a^{1-3\gamma+\gamma\eta},

assuming for simplicity that the exponents
1-2\gamma+\gamma\eta
and
1-3\gamma+\gamma\eta
are nonzero. To discuss which values of
a
can make
D_{a}
smaller than 1, we consider different cases.

(1) The case
\tau>4-\eta
.

In this case the two exponents on the right-hand side of (
55
) are positive so we have
D_{0}\lesssim c\lambda
with finite
c
, and in particular (
41
) is satisfied for
a=0
and
\lambda\leq 1/c
, yielding fast extinction. Moreover, the reader might check that the hypothesis
(H)_{\delta}
is satisfied for
\delta\geq\frac{1-\eta}{2-\eta}
. It follows that, for
\lambda\leq 1/c
, the expected extinction time is at most
\omega N^{\frac{1-\eta}{2-\eta}},

for some
\omega=\omega(\lambda)<\infty
.

(2) The case
\tau=4-\eta
.

We get
D_{a}\sim\lambda+\lambda^{3}\log(1/a)
and so

D_{a}\leq 1
for

a(\lambda)=e^{-r\lambda^{-3}},

for well-chosen
r
and
\lambda
small. Thus we get an exponentially small upper bound for the upper metastable density.

(3) The case
\frac{5}{2}-\eta\leq\tau\leq 4+2\eta
.

In this region the largest term of (
55
) is
\lambda^{3}a^{1-3\gamma+\gamma\eta}
so we get
D_{a}\leq 1
for small
\lambda
by taking
a(\lambda)=r\lambda^{\frac{3}{3\gamma-1-\gamma\eta}},

for a well-chosen
r
. (
45
) then gives us the upper bound for the upper metastable density as

\displaystyle\rho^{+}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle a(\lambda)+\frac{1}{s(a(\lambda))}\int_{a(\lambda)}^{1}s(y)\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\frac{1}{s(a(\lambda))}\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{2-\gamma\eta}{3\gamma-1-\gamma\eta}}=\lambda^{\frac{2\tau-2-\eta}{4-\tau-\eta}}.

Notice that this bound is available in the full region
\frac{5}{2}-\eta\leq\tau<4-\eta
, yet it does not match the lower bounds obtained in Section
3.4.1
when
4+2\eta\leq\tau<4-\eta
. In the next section we use Theorem
4
to obtain the matching upper bounds we are missing here.

Application of Theorem
4
to the factor kernel

We consider the vertex updating model for the factor kernel
p(x,y)=x^{-\gamma}y^{-\gamma}
in the region delimited by the inequalities
\tau>3
,
\tau>4+2\eta
,
\tau>2-\eta
and
\tau<4-\eta
. The inequality
\tau>2-\eta
implies
\pi(x)\asymp x^{-\gamma}
. We also have
a_{\rm{str}}\asymp\lambda^{2/\gamma}
. For
\eta>-1/2
, we have
a_{\rm{stsl}}\asymp\lambda^{1/-\gamma\eta}<a_{\rm{str}}
while for
\eta<-1/2
, we have
a_{\rm{stsl}}=a_{\rm{str}}\asymp\lambda^{2/\gamma}
. We now search for some
a<a_{\rm{stsl}}
and function
s
satisfying Conditions (
48
), (
49
) and (
50
) as well as the Hypotheses
(H1)
and
(H2)
.
Let us introduce
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
and
A_{3}
the following integrals over the function
s
:

A_{1}=\int_{0}^{a_{\rm{stsl}}}\lambda y^{-\gamma}s(y\vee a)dy,\,\,A_{2}=\int_{0}^{a_{\rm{stsl}}}\kappa(y)y^{-\gamma}s(y\vee a)dy,\,\,A_{3}=\int_{a_{\rm{stsl}}}^{1}\lambda y^{-\gamma}s(y)dy.

Recalling the form of the factor kernel
p(x,y)=x^{-\gamma}y^{-\gamma}
and bounding the max
\kappa(x)\wedge\kappa(y)
by the sum, we see that the inequalities (
48
), (
49
) and (
50
) are guaranteed by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}(A_{1}+A_{3})x^{-\gamma}\leq s(x),

\displaystyle\qquad\mbox{ for }x\geq a_{\rm{str}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}(A_{1}+A_{3})x^{-\gamma}T^{\rm{loc}}(x)\leq s(x),

\displaystyle\qquad\mbox{ for }a_{\rm{stsl}}\leq x<a_{\rm{str}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}\left(\frac{\kappa(x)}{\lambda}A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3}\right)x^{-\gamma}T^{\rm{loc}}(x)\leq s(x),

\displaystyle\qquad\mbox{ for }x<a_{\rm{stsl}}.

A natural choice for the scoring function
s
is then given by

s(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x^{-\gamma}&\quad\mbox{ for }x\geq a_{\rm{str}}\\
x^{-\gamma}T^{\rm{loc}}(x)&\quad\mbox{ for }a_{\rm{stsl}}\leq x<a_{\rm{str}},\\
\frac{\kappa(x)}{\lambda}x^{-\gamma}T^{\rm{loc}}(x)+\rho x^{-\gamma}T^{\rm{loc}}(x)&\quad\mbox{ for }x<a_{\rm{stsl}},\end{array}\right.

leading naturally to the requests

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathfrak{c}(A_{1}+A_{3})\leq 1,\\
\mathfrak{c}(A_{2}+A_{3})\leq\rho,\end{array}\right.

which in turn are implied by the three requests

A_{1}\leq\frac{1}{2\mathfrak{c}},\quad A_{2}\leq\frac{\rho}{2\mathfrak{c}},\quad A_{3}\leq\frac{1\wedge\rho}{2\mathfrak{c}}.

Before computing these terms, we check that the hypotheses
(H1)
and
(H2)
are satisfied with this choice of scoring function. The hypothesis
(H1)
is equivalent to

{a_{\rm{str}}}^{-\gamma}\int_{0}^{a_{\rm{str}}}x^{-\gamma}y^{-\gamma}\mathrm{d}y\leq x^{-\gamma}, \qquad\mbox{ for }x>a_{\rm{str}},

which is in turn equivalent to
a_{\rm{str}}^{1-2\gamma}/(1-\gamma)\leq 1
, and is satisfied for small
\lambda
as we lie in the phase
\tau>3
or equivalently
\gamma<1/2
. Furthermore, we have

s(a_{\rm{str}})=a_{\rm{str}}^{-\gamma}\asymp\lambda^{-2},

so the first part of the hypothesis
(H2)
is satisfied, with
\alpha=1
. We will check the second part of the hypothesis
(H2)
later on, while we now bound the terms
A_{1}
to
A_{3}
, up to multiplicative constants depending only on
\gamma
and
\eta
. First, observe that since
a_{\rm{stsl}}\leq a_{\rm{str}}
we have that
T^{\rm{loc}}(x)\asymp\lambda^{2}x^{-\gamma+\gamma\eta}
, for
x<a_{\rm{stsl}}
, and hence by our choice of
s
and the definition of
a_{\rm{stsl}}
,

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle\asymp\lambda a^{1-\gamma}s(a)+\rho\int_{a}^{a_{\rm{stsl}}}\lambda^{3}y^{-3\gamma+\gamma\eta}dy+\int_{a}^{a_{\rm{stsl}}}\lambda^{2}y^{-3\gamma}dy

\displaystyle\asymp\rho\lambda^{3}a^{1-3\gamma+\gamma\eta}+\lambda^{2}a^{1-3\gamma}+\lambda^{2}a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-3\gamma},

using that
1-3\gamma+\gamma\eta=(\tau-4+\eta)\gamma<0
for
\tau<4-\eta
, and assuming for simplicity
1-3\gamma\neq 0
.

When
\eta\geq-1/2
, we have
a_{\rm{stsl}}\asymp\lambda^{-1/\gamma\eta}
and therefore we get that

\lambda^{2}a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-3\gamma}\asymp\lambda^{\frac{\tau-4-2\eta}{-\eta}}
, and this term tends to 0 as
\lambda
tends to 0 as
\tau>4+2\eta
. When
\eta<-1/2
, we have
a_{\rm{stsl}}\asymp\lambda^{2/\gamma}
and thus the term

\lambda^{2}a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-3\gamma}\asymp\lambda^{2\tau-6}
also goes to 0 as
\tau>3
. Thus, to guarantee the inequality
A_{1}\leq 1/2\mathfrak{c}
for small
\lambda
, it suffices to satisfy the following requests:

(56)

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}a^{1-3\gamma}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 1,

(57)

\displaystyle\lambda^{3}a^{1-3\gamma+\gamma\eta}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 1/\rho,

with an abuse of notation, as for these requests to make sense, the involved multiplicative constants should be specified. In other words, the request
\lesssim 1
should be understood as
\leq\mathfrak{c}(\gamma,\eta)
, where
\mathfrak{c}(\gamma,\eta)>0
is some constant which may change from one inequality to the other, and which could be made explicit if necessary.

Similarly, we obtain

A_{2}\asymp\rho(\lambda^{2}a^{1-3\gamma}+\lambda^{2}a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-3\gamma})+(\lambda a^{1-3\gamma-\gamma\eta}+\lambda a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-3\gamma-\gamma\eta}),

leading to the requests (
56
) and (
57
) again, as well as the additional requests

(58)

\lambda a^{1-3\gamma-\gamma\eta}+\lambda a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-3\gamma-\gamma\eta}\lesssim\rho.

To bound
A_{3}
, we first treat the case
\eta<-1/2
for which
A_{3}=\lambda\int_{a_{\rm{stsl}}}^{1}y^{-2\gamma}\mathrm{d}y\asymp\lambda
as we have
\tau>3
or equivalently
\gamma<1/2
. In the case
\eta>-1/2
, we obtain

\displaystyle A_{3}

\displaystyle\asymp\lambda+(1+\rho)\lambda^{3}a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-3\gamma+\gamma\eta}

\displaystyle\asymp\lambda+(1+\rho)\lambda a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-3\gamma-\gamma\eta},

using the value of
a_{\rm{stsl}}
in that case. Assuming further
\rho\leq 1
, the original request for
A_{3}
now reads

\lambda+\lambda a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-3\gamma-\gamma\eta}\lesssim\rho

where the bound on
\lambda a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-3\gamma-\gamma\eta}
already follows from (
58
) and thus we only require

(59)

\lambda\lesssim\rho.

Thus far we have several requests, namely, (
56
), (
57
), (
58
), and (
59
). In order to make these inequalities independent of
\rho
we combine (
59
) with inequality (
57
), obtaining

(60)

\lambda^{4}a^{1-3\gamma+\gamma\eta}\lesssim 1,

and also combine inequalities (
57
) and (
58
), which gives

\displaystyle\lambda^{4}a^{2-6\gamma}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

\displaystyle\lambda^{4}a^{1-3\gamma+\gamma\eta}a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-3\gamma-\gamma\eta}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1.

These last two inequalities are redundant with (
56
) and (
60
); the first of these requests amounts to (
56
), while the second is less restrictive than either (
56
) or (
60
) depending on the sign of
1-3\gamma-\gamma\eta
(using the fact that
a\leq a_{\rm{stsl}}
).

We are thus left with the two main requests (
56
) and (
60
). The other requests only yield restictions on the choice of
\rho
, which we should check are compatible with
\rho\leq 1
. Which of (
56
) or (
60
) is the most demanding depends on whether
\tau
is larger or smaller than
4+\eta
, and we now treat these two cases.

•

\tau<4+\eta
. In that case the most restrictive request is (
56
), leading to the choice

a\asymp\lambda^{\frac{2}{3\gamma-1}}.

Moreover,
\rho
should satisfy

\lambda\lesssim\rho\lesssim\lambda^{-3}a^{-1+3\gamma+\gamma\eta}\asymp\lambda^{\frac{\tau-4-2\eta}{4-\tau}}\asymp\lambda^{1-2\frac{4+\eta-\tau}{4-\tau}}.

In particular, the choice
\rho\asymp\lambda^{1-2\frac{4+\eta-\tau}{4-\tau}}
allows to satisfy the second part of
(H2)
with
\alpha=2\frac{4-\tau+\eta}{4-\tau}\in(0,2).

Pushing further the computations we get

\frac{1}{s(a)}\int_{a}^{1}s(y)\mathrm{d}y\asymp\frac{1}{s(a(\lambda))}\asymp\lambda^{\frac{\tau}{4-\tau}}.

We thus obtain an upper bound for the metastable density as

\rho^{+}(\lambda)\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{\tau}{4-\tau}}.

•

\tau\geq 4+\eta
. In that case the most restrictive request is (
60
), leading to the choice

a\asymp\lambda^{\frac{4}{3\gamma-1-\gamma\eta}}

In that case we have
\rho\asymp\lambda
, which does not allow to satisfy the hypothesis
(H2)
for
\alpha>0
. The first term in the definition of
s(x)
gives another bound, but this also does not always allows to satisfy
(H2)
. However, we can modify slightly the definition of
s(x)
so that
(H2)
is satisfied, by adding the term
\lambda^{1-\alpha}a_{\rm{str}}^{-\gamma}T^{\rm{loc}}(x)
when
x<a_{\rm{str}}
,
for some
\alpha>0
, which makes
(H2)
automatically satisfied. This new term changes the values of the integrals by adding new terms
A^{\prime}_{1}
,
A^{\prime}_{2}
and
A^{\prime}_{3}
to the previous ones, however the new inequalities that we request remain almost unchanged

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathfrak{c}((A_{1}+A^{\prime}_{1})+(A_{3}+A_{3}^{\prime}))\leq 1\\
\mathfrak{c}((A_{2}+A_{2}^{\prime})+(A_{3}+A^{\prime}_{3}))\leq\lambda,\end{array}\right.

since it is enough to satisfy the inequalities for the previous score function (because the new one is bigger).
We thus obtain the additional requests
A^{\prime}_{1}\lesssim 1
and
A^{\prime}_{2},A^{\prime}_{3}\lesssim\lambda
. Computing the integrals yields

\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle\asymp\lambda^{4-\alpha}a_{\rm{str}}^{-\gamma}\left(a^{1-2\gamma+\gamma\eta}+a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-2\gamma+\gamma\eta}\right)

\displaystyle\asymp\lambda^{2-\alpha}\left(a^{1-2\gamma+\gamma\eta}+a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-2\gamma+\gamma\eta}\right).

By the value of
a
, the first term is
\lambda
to the power
\mbox{$\frac{2(\tau-2+\eta)}{4-\tau-\eta}$}-\alpha
, leading to the condition
\alpha<\mbox{$\frac{2(\tau-2+\eta)}{4-\tau-\eta}$}
. For the second term, we split the analysis between the case
\eta\geq-1/2
, with
a_{\rm{stsl}}\asymp\lambda^{-1/{\gamma\eta}}
, leading to
\alpha<\mbox{$\frac{\tau-3-\eta}{-\eta}$}
, and the case
\eta<-1/2
, with
a_{\rm{str}}\asymp\lambda^{2/\gamma}
, leading to the request
\alpha<2(\tau-2+\eta)
.
Similarly, we compute

A^{\prime}_{2}\asymp\lambda^{3-\alpha}a_{\rm{str}}^{-\gamma}a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-2\gamma}\asymp\lambda^{1-\alpha}a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-2\gamma}.

Splitting again the analysis in the two cases
\eta\geq-1/2
and
\eta<-1/2
, we see that the request
A^{\prime}_{2}\lesssim\lambda
is guaranteed by asking
\alpha<\mbox{$\frac{\tau-3}{-\eta}$}
in the first case, and
\alpha<2(\tau-3)
in the second case.

Finally, the term
A^{\prime}_{3}
is present only in the case
\eta>-1/2
, where
a_{\rm{str}}>a_{\rm{stsl}}\asymp\lambda^{1/-\gamma\eta}
, and we then have

A^{\prime}_{3}\asymp\lambda^{2-\alpha}a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-2\gamma+\gamma\eta}\asymp\lambda^{1-\alpha}a_{\rm{stsl}}^{1-2\gamma}\asymp A^{\prime}_{2},

thus not leading to any further request. There is now no difficulty choosing
\alpha>0
small satisfying all the requests, and pushing further the computations we then get

\frac{1}{s(a)}\int_{a}^{1}s(y)\mathrm{d}y\asymp\frac{1}{s(a(\lambda))}\asymp\lambda^{\frac{3\tau-4-\eta}{4-\tau-\eta}}.

We thus obtain an upper bound for the metastable density as

\rho^{+}(\lambda)\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{3\tau-4-\eta}{4-\tau-\eta}}.

5.
Proof of Theorems
3
and
4

5.1.
Proof of Theorem
3

We now provide the proof of Theorem
3
, using settings and notations that will be reusable in the proof of Theorem
4
. In particular, we rewrite (
41
) as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}\ T^{\rm{loc}}(x)\int_{0}^{1}\lambda p(x,y)s(y\vee a)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq s(x)

with of course
\mathfrak{c}=7
, and similarly for (
42
).
We suppose
a(\lambda)
and
s(x)
are given satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem
3
. When
a>0
, we extend
s
to
(0,1]
by
s(x)=s(x\vee a)
. The theorem also involves other functions defined on
(0,1]
, namely
\kappa
,
\pi
and
T^{\rm{loc}}
.
We use the subscript notation when considering the corresponding functions defined on
\{1,\ldots,N\}
, so

\kappa_{j}=\kappa(j/N)
,
\pi_{j}=\pi(j/N)
,
T^{\rm{loc}}_{j}=T^{\rm{loc}}(j/N)
, and
s_{j}=s(j/N)
. Recall the notation
p_{i,j}=\mbox{$\frac{1}{N}$}{p(i/N,j/N)}\wedge 1
.

The fact that
(H)_{1}
follows from (
41
) and (
42
)
(when
a=0
) is immediate as then, using the monotonicity of
p
,

\frac{s(x)}{T^{\rm{loc}}(x)}\geq\mathfrak{c}\int_{0}^{1}(\lambda\wedge\kappa(y))p(1,y)s(y\vee a)\,\mathrm{d}y>0.

Moreover,
(H)_{0}
is satisfied when
\eta\geq 1/2
since

\frac{\pi(x)}{\kappa(x)}\leq\frac{1}{\kappa(x)^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}p(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2}}{\kappa_{0}^{2}}x^{-\gamma+2\gamma\eta},

where the second inequality follows from (
1
). Using again (
1
) we deduce that for
\eta<1/2
it cannot hold since

\frac{\pi(x)}{\kappa(x)}\geq\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{1}x^{-\gamma}}{2\kappa(x)\big(\kappa(x)+\min(\kappa(y),y\in[1/2,1])\big)}.

If
a>0
we call the vertices
j<aN
stars. The proof attaches a score to a configuration that behaves as a supermartingale as long as no star gets infected. We stop the process when infecting a star, thus before any edge between stars can be used to transmit the infection. It is convenient and clearer to consider in the following that there are simply no edges between stars, for example by redefining
p_{ij}=0
when
i
and
j
are stars.

Recall the definition of slow vertices just before Theorem
3
, and in particular the definition of
a_{\rm{slow}}
in (
40
). In Theorem
3
, the request (
42
) seems to indicate that the infection propagates between slow vertices at rate

\kappa(x)\vee\kappa(y)
instead of
\lambda
, which amounts to take into account the depletion effect, and is a significant difficulty in this theorem. We say an edge
\{i,j\}
(not necessarily in
G
) is slow if
i
and
j
are both slow vertices, otherwise we refer to the edge as quick. So a slow edge updates at a rate bounded by
2\lambda
, and a quick edge at rate at least
\lambda
. In the following, we suppose
a<a_{\rm{slow}}
. When this does not hold, the proof still works and is actually easier as there are no slow edges.

The proof relies on the following ideas, introducing a modification of the entire process (which alters the underlying network, its dynamics and the evolution of the infection) which constitutes a stochastic upper bound:

•

For the new underlying network keep the original set of vertices
\{1,\ldots,N\}
, maintaining their previous classification as either slow or quick, as well as the definition of stars. We also maintain all quick edges, consisting of edges with at least one quick vertex as an endpoint.

•

As for the remaining edges (between slow vertices), we replace them with their directed versions so for each pair
i,j
of slow vertices we consider
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
as possible edges of the network (even at the same time) instead of
\{i,j\}
.

•

Each vertex
i
still updates at rate
\kappa_{i}
, however updatings affect the edges differently according to their type. At this point it is enough to keep in mind that updatings maintain the role of losing the local neighbourhood of vertices.

•

The new process is still a contact process at its core, where each vertex is classified as either healthy or infected, and can be infected, recover, and be reinfected. Vertices recover at rate
1
independently from one another as in the original process, however the rate at which the infection is transmitted along each edge is more intricate.

•

A new addition to the process is that we also subclassify infected vertices as
saturated
and
unsaturated
. The reader might think of a vertex as saturated whenever it is surrounded by many neighbours (or maybe just powerful ones), likely to reinfect it after its recovery.
For this reason for saturated vertices we just negate recoveries altogether, and hence these become beacons of infection that
cannot recover
. In order for saturated vertices to disappear they have to first update and observe a more favourable environment to become unsaturated, and thus be available for recovery. The different conditions that result in saturation will be given gradually as we explain below the evolution of the process.

•

We begin by describing first the rules affecting slow vertices and slow (directed) edges, see Figure
2
for an example. By definition, for the original process the subnetwork of slow vertices evolves at a slower pace than the contact process and hence is seen as almost static by the infection, which explores it as it spreads. We adopt the point of view of the infection and thus classify slow edges as either
unrevealed
,
present
or,
absent
(all mutually exclusive). A fourth label,
saturated
, will be helpful when dealing with saturated vertices, but we leave the intuition behind this label for later. Slow edges can change their labels through updates or infections of slow vertices:

–

Every slow vertex
i
updates at rate
\kappa_{i}
, and upon updating a two-step mechanism occurs:

*

First, all slow edges
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
become unrevealed regardless of their previous status.

*

Next, if
i
is infected at the time of the update, we
resample
each edge
(i,j)
, declaring it
present
or
absent
by tossing a coin with probability
p_{i,j}
, independently from all other edges. We do the same for each edge
(j,i)
for which
j
is infected at the time of the update.

–

If a slow vertex
i
becomes infected, we
resample
each unrevealed edge
(i,j)
leaving
i
in the same manner as before. Observe that this procedure does not affect already revealed edges .

Figure 2.
Left: Four slow vertices, of which the top and right ones are infected. Unrevealed edges are represented as dotted, present as solid, and absent are not drawn. In the picture the edges leaving the infected vertices, marked as shaded, have been revealed. Centre: The vertex on the right has recovered but no updatings have occurred so the information about edges is kept. Right: The vertex on the right has updated making all the edges leaving it unrevealed. Observe that the edge going from the top vertex to the right one also updates since the top vertex is infected, but it is immediately revealed again, flipping a coin to decide its status which in this case turns to be absent.

•

Slow vertices recover at rate
1
(unless saturated) and transmit the infection through present edges at rate
\lambda
(regardless of the state of the receiving vertex). If an infection is sent through the present edge
(i,j)
, then

–

both vertices
i
and
j
become instantly
saturated
;

–

the edges
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
become
saturated
, regardless of their previous state.

See Figure
3
for an example.
The reason why this infection leads to saturation is that for a slow vertex
i
we have
T^{loc}(i/N)\geq 8/3\kappa_{i}\geq 8/3\lambda
, which fits the intuition that both
i
and
j
are likely to have enough time to infect each other if the edge
(i,j)
is present. Contrary to the intuition given for vertices, the saturation of an edge means that it becomes useless for the infection, since we know that both vertices are saturated and hence constantly infected. It will be important later that when referring to present edges we are not including saturated ones for this same reason.

i
i
i
j
j
j
If
\displaystyle j
gets infected by a third
vertex, we reveal the edge
\displaystyle(j,i)
,
which in this case is present.
An infection occured along
\displaystyle(j,i)
.
Both vertices and both edges are
now saturated. Only an update of
\displaystyle i
or
\displaystyle j
will change this situation.
saturation
If
\displaystyle i
becomes infected and
\displaystyle j
is not,
we reveal the edge
\displaystyle(i,j)
, which in
this case is absent. We keep the
edge
\displaystyle(j,i)
unrevealed.

Figure 3.
Dynamics of the slow edges
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
.

•

For any given slow vertex
i
define
N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}(t)
as the number of present edges leaving
i
at time
t
. If at any given time it satisfies

N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}(t)\geq\frac{3\kappa_{i}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}{8\lambda},

then we declare vertex
i
to be
saturated
.
Observe that when a vertex is healthy, the number of present edges leaving it can only decrease so at any time in which the condition is met,
i
must be necessarily infected. We remind the reader that a saturated vertex can only stop being saturated by updating, so dropping
N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}(t)
below
\frac{3\kappa_{i}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}{8\lambda}
by the updating of its neighbours is irrelevant. Intuitively, this condition entails that
i
is anyway very likely to quickly transmit an infection to another slow vertex, thus leading to saturation by the previous rule.

•

When a slow vertex
i
updates while infected, we sample freshly the slow edges leaving it and thus observe a totally new value for
N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}(t)
.
Irrespectively of whether
i
was saturated or unsaturated before the update, the vertex
i
will be saturated after the update only if the new value of

N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}(t)
is too large, however this happens with probability bounded, using the Markov inequality, by

(61)

\frac{8\lambda}{3\kappa_{i}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}\sum_{j\leq a_{\rm{slow}}N}p_{ij}\leq\frac{1}{6\lambda\pi_{i}}\int_{0}^{a_{\rm{slow}}}p(i/N,z)\,\mathrm{d}z\leq\frac{1}{3},

where the first inequality comes from the definition of
T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}
and the second from that of
\pi_{i}
, together with the fact that the updating rate of a slow edge is bounded by
2\lambda
.

•

We now describe the rules affecting quick edges, for which we use the wait-and-see coupling introduced in
[
12
, Section 5.1]
where each such edge is treated as a possible channel for the infection, irrespectively of whether the edge belongs to the original network or not. Instead of thinking of quick edges as absent or present we classify them as
clogged
or
clear
. A clogged edge
\{i,j\}
transmits the infection at the reduced rate
\lambda p_{i,j}
(as long as either
i
or
j
is infected), and if this happens the edge is cleared. A
clear
edge then transmits the infection at the regular rate
\lambda
. Note that if the vertices
i
and
j
are infected and the edge is clogged, we still count an infection along the edge at rate
\lambda p_{i,j}
, with the only effect of clearing the edge. The reduced rate associated to clogged edges reflects the fact that the edge is likely to be absent from the original network. We refer to
[
12
, Section 5.1]
to justify that we get an upper bound for the original process. Observe that only quick edges can be clogged or clear whereas only slow edges can be unrevealed, present, absent or saturated.
