Infection rate along an edge, depending on its state.

•

Recall that quick edges can be adjacent to both slow and quick vertices
i
, the latter also updating at rate
\kappa_{i}
. When a vertex
i
updates, all quick edges
\{i,j\}
become clogged irrespectively of their previous status.

•

For every quick edge
\{i,j\}
define
R_{ij}=\lambda/(\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j})
and for any vertex
i
(quick or slow) define

R_{i}(t):=\sum_{\{i,j\}\text{ clear}}R_{ij},

which is an upper bound for the probability that one of the clear edges incident to
i
transmits an infection to
i
before being updated. If at any given time, vertex
i
satisfies
R_{i}(t)\geq 1/4
, then we declare it to be
saturated
. Observe that quick edges can only become clear through the spread of an infection, thus at the time in which
R_{i}(t)\geq 1/4
vertex
i
is necessarily infected. Commonly, the number of infected neighbours of
i
is a fraction of
R_{i}
and hence a vertex, which is saturated due to this condition, would be very likely reinfected after a recovery.

•

At time
t=0
the initial configuration of the process is constructed as follows:

–

First, declare the set of vertices that are infected. Also, set each quick edge as clogged and each slow edge as unrevealed.

–

Next, update each slow vertex through the two-step mechanism described above. This reveals all slow edges leaving slow infected vertices, while maintaining all other slow edges unrevealed.

The fact that this new process is a stochastic upper bound of the original contact process can be proved similarly to what was done in
[
12
, Section 5.1]
, together with Lemma
3
below, and observing that saturated vertices cannot recover, and whenever a slow edge
(i,j)
is used to spread the infection, both
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
become saturated, and thus can be thought as belonging to the network.

Lemma 3
.

Suppose
p\in[0,1]
,
G=(V,E)
is a finite graph, and
e\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(V)\backslash E
an edge not present in the graph.
Let
G^{e,p}
and
\overrightarrow{G}^{e,p}
be two random graphs such that:

•

G^{e,p}
is obtained from
G
by adding edge
e
with probability
p
.

•

\overrightarrow{G}^{e,p}
is obtained from
G
by adding independently
2
2

2

The lemma stays true without this independence property, and this assumption is not used in the proof.
the directed edge
\overrightarrow{e}
as well as its reverse edge
\overleftarrow{e}
with probability
p
.

Writing
G^{e}=(V,E\cup\{e\})
, we thus have
\mathbb{P}(G^{e,p}=G^{e})=p
. The contact process on
G^{e,p}
is then stochastically dominated by the contact process on

\overrightarrow{G}^{e,p}
where, the first time an infection is transmitted along edge
\overrightarrow{e}
or
\overleftarrow{e}
, the graph is instantly replaced by
G^{e}
.

Proof.

Consider the contact process on
G^{e}
, but at the first time the edge
e
is used to transmit the infection, with probability
1-p
the infection and the edge
e
are deleted. We get the contact process on
G^{e,p}
(with the presence of
e
in the graph not being revealed at once).
If the infection (in
G^{e}
) was transmitted in the direction of
\overrightarrow{e}
(resp.
\overleftarrow{e}
), we couple the choice of keeping
e
in
G^{e,p}
with that of keeping
\overrightarrow{e}
(resp.
\overleftarrow{e}
) in
\overrightarrow{G}^{e,p}
. Then, the first infection using
e
in
G^{e,p}
(if
G^{e,p}=G^{e}
) is an infection in
\overrightarrow{G}^{e,p}
. The lemma follows.
∎

We are now ready to define the score associated to a given configuration of the new process: Let
s
be the function given in the hypothesis of the theorem and define

(62)

t(x)=\frac{s(x)}{r\kappa(x)T^{\rm{loc}}(x)},

as well as
t_{j}=t(j/N)
, with the constant
r
being defined
3
3

3

The proof of Theorem
4
will use again the same notation but with a different value for
r
, which is why we do not use that
r=3/2
up until the end of the proof.
by
r=3/2
. Since
T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}\leq 8
and
T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}\leq\frac{8}{3\kappa_{i}}
we obtain the following bounds which will be used extensively throughout this section:

(63)

t_{j}\leq\frac{3s_{j}}{8r}=\frac{s_{j}}{4},\quad\text{ and }\quad\kappa_{i}t_{i}\leq\frac{T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}\kappa_{i}t_{i}}{8}=\frac{s_{i}}{8r}.

Define

m_{j}(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}s_{j}+t_{j}&\mbox{if }j\mbox{ is \color[rgb]{1,0,1} infected saturated},\\
s_{j}+4R_{j}(t)t_{j}&\mbox{if }j\mbox{ is \color[rgb]{1,0,0} infected unsaturated},\\
2R_{j}(t)(s_{j}+t_{j})&\mbox{if }j\mbox{ is \color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13} healthy}.\end{array}\right.

The score associated to the configuration is then

(64)

M_{t}:=\sum_{i}m_{i}(t)+{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sum_{(i,j)\text{ present edge}}s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}},

where
u_{j}=t_{j}/3
. We refer to the first term on the right-hand side of (
64
) as the
vertex score
, whereas we refer to the second term as the
edge score
. We now discuss the idea behind this score and some computations involved:

•

Each vertex
j
is associated to a score
m_{j}(t)
that depends on its current state (healthy, infected unsaturated, infected saturated), on the quick edges
\{j,k\}
and on the slow edges
(j,k)
leaving
j
. Each present slow edge
(i,j)
entering
j
is also associated to a score, that reflects the fact that
i
is likely to later infect
j
. No score is associated to other slow or quick edges and in particular no score is associated to saturated edges.

•

The score
m_{j}(t)
associated to an infected vertex
j
consists of a base score
s_{j}
(reflecting the infective potential of the vertex) plus a term
4R_{j}(t)t_{j}
which scales linearly with
R_{j}
and thus with the number of clear edges connected to it. We cap this score at saturation.

•

The score
m_{j}(t)
associated to a healthy vertex
j
also scales with
R_{j}
, and thus on the estimated probability that
j
could be later (re)infected by one of these edges.

•

When revealing the neighbourhood of a slow vertex (whether it is a result of an infection or an update), the
average
increase of the edge score is bounded by

(65)

\sum_{j<a_{\rm{slow}}N}p_{ij}(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j})\leq\frac{4}{3}\frac{1}{\kappa_{i}}\sum_{j<a_{\rm{slow}}N}(\kappa_{i}\vee\kappa_{j})p_{ij}s_{j}\leq\frac{4}{3}\frac{s_{i}}{\mathfrak{c}\kappa_{i}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}=\frac{4r}{\mathfrak{c}}u_{i}\leq u_{i},\hskip-22.76228pt

where we have used that
s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}\leq s_{j}+\mbox{$\frac{4}{3}$}t_{j}\leq\mbox{$\frac{4}{3}$}s_{j}
, the assumptions on the function
s
, the definition of
t
, and our choice
\mathfrak{c}>4r=6
.

•

Note that when the present directed slow edge
(i,j)
transmits an infection to
j
, the score
s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}
associated to it, is itself an upper bound for the new score
m_{j}(t)=s_{j}+t_{j}
assigned to
j
(that is now infected and saturated) plus the average increase in score due to revealing the directed slow edges leaving
j
(since it is bounded by
u_{j}
from the previous argument).
Simultaneously, the edge
(i,j)
becomes saturated, with no score associated to it anymore. Hence, this infection does not increase the total score in average, except possibly for the score associated to
i
, which will then also be saturated. It is especially for this feature that we have introduced
u_{j}
and associated the score
s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}
to the present edge
(i,j)
.
In a sense, associating the score
s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}
to a present slow edge
(i,j)
also amounts to considering that revealing the edge
(i,j)
is “as bad” as using it to transmit the infection.

•

We now summarize the previously described conditions entailing saturation:

(i)

i
is a slow vertex, and since its last update it has infected another slow vertex or has been infected by another slow vertex.

(ii)

R_{i}(t)\geq 1/4
,

(iii)

i
is a slow vertex and
N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}(t)\geq\frac{3\kappa_{i}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}{8\lambda}
.

Recall also that a vertex can only stop being saturated by updating, so saturation is maintained even if
R_{i}(t)
or
N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}
drop below their respective values due to the updating of other vertices.

Let
T_{\rm{hit}}
be the first infection time of a star in
[1,\ldots,\lfloor aN\rfloor]
. Our aim is to show that
M_{t\wedge T_{\rm{hit}}}
is a supermartingale. To do so we fix
t>0
, and bound the expected infinitesimal change of the score

(66)

\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[M_{t+\mathrm{d}t}-M_{t}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}]

uniformly over all configurations in
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
where stars have not yet been infected. Loosely speaking, if
\mathrm{d}t
is sufficiently small, then aside from some term tending to zero with
\mathrm{d}t
, the difference
M_{t+\mathrm{d}t}-M_{t}
comes from a single configuration-changing event. The possible events are infections, recoveries and updates, whose effect on the configuration vary depending on the type of vertex/edge and its state. Since the score is a sum of individual scores, the expression (
66
) becomes a large sum of terms associated to different vertices/edges affected by these events. In order to facilitate the analysis of this sum, we group these terms into
contributions
which we control below. The reader can easily check that these contributions indeed exhaust the changes of
M_{t}
under all possible events without double counting.

\mathbf{(i)\ Negative\ Contributions}.

\bullet

Recoveries of infected unsaturated vertices.
The recovery of an infected unsaturated vertex
i
(whether fast or slow) holds at rate one and induces a change of score

m_{i}(t+\mathrm{d}t)-m_{i}(t)=2R_{i}(t)(s_{i}+t_{i})-(s_{i}+4R_{i}(t)t_{i})\leq(2R_{i}(t)-1)s_{i}\leq-\frac{s_{i}}{2}

since the fact that
i
is unsaturated implies that
R_{i}\leq\frac{1}{4}
. The total contribution to the expected infinitesimal change is thus upper bounded by

(67)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}-\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}\frac{s_{i}}{2}.}

\bullet

Unrevealing of present slow edges.
In this contribution we group the terms which appear due to the effect of
unrevealing
previously present edges as a result of the updating of slow vertices. For any given present directed slow edge
(i,j)
,
the update of one of its endvertices occurs at rate
\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j}
, and has probability at least
2/3
(actually tending to
1
with
N
) to stay unrevealed after the update. This yields a contribution

(68)

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}-\sum_{(i,j)\text{ present}}\frac{2}{3}(\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j})(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}).}

\bullet

Updates of infected saturated vertices.
In this contribution we group the change in score of infected saturated vertices as a result of their own updating, together with the increase in score that occurs as a result of revealing their new neighbourhood. Each saturated vertex
i
updates at rate
\kappa_{i}
, and after its update we have
R_{i}(t+\mathrm{d}t)=0
so if
i
is quick, then

m_{i}(t+\mathrm{d}t)-m_{i}(t)=s_{i}-(t_{i}+s_{i})=-t_{i}.

If
i
is slow, on the other hand, we also need to take into account the effect of the update on the slow edges:

–

During the first step of the two-step mechanism all slow edges leaving
i
become unrevealed, thus dropping
N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}(t+\mathrm{d}t)
to zero.

–

During the second step it could happen that
i
reveals a large value of
N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}(t+\mathrm{d}t)
, thus becoming saturated again, so
m_{i}(t+\mathrm{d}t)-m_{i}(t)=0
. However, from (
61
) the probability of this event is bounded by
1/3
.

–

Also during the second step, a new neighbourhood of
i
is revealed, which induces a change in the edge score that is in average upper bounded by
u_{i}=t_{i}/3
due to (
65
).

Adding both effects we obtain that for a slow vertex
i
the average change in score is upper bounded by
\frac{2}{3}(-t_{i})+\frac{t_{i}}{3}=-\frac{t_{i}}{3}
, and since this bound also works for quick vertices, we deduce that the total contribution to (
66
) is at most

(69)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}-\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{saturated}}}\frac{1}{3}{\kappa_{i}t_{i}}}.

\bullet

Updates of clear edges adjacent to at least one healthy vertex.
In this contribution we count the change in score
m_{i}(t+\mathrm{d}t)-m_{i}(t)
for every healthy vertex
i
due to the decrease of
R_{i}
after either its own update or the update of one of its neighbours. Observe that the previous contribution addressed the change in score for saturated vertices, which are infected, and hence we are not double counting. For each healthy vertex
i
, its vertex score is

m_{i}(t)=2R_{i}(t)(s_{i}+t_{i})=2\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{j\sim i}{\{i,j\} \text{ clear edge}}}\frac{\lambda}{\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j}}(s_{i}+t_{i})

This score can decrease by
2R_{i}(t)(s_{i}+t_{i})
after its own update (which occurs at rate
\kappa_{i}
), or can decrease by
2\frac{\lambda}{\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j}}(s_{i}+t_{i})
after the update of its neighbour
j
(which occurs at rate
\kappa_{j}
). This yields a contribution

(70)

\sum_{i\text{ healthy}}\bigg[-2R_{i}(t)\kappa_{i}(s_{i}+t_{i})-2\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{j\sim i}{\{i,j\} \text{ clear}}}\frac{\lambda\kappa_{j}}{\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j}}(s_{i}+t_{i})\bigg]={\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}-\sum_{i\text{ healthy}}2\lambda N^{\rm{cl}}_{i}(t)(s_{i}+t_{i}),}

where
N^{\rm{cl}}_{i}(t)
is the number of clear edges incident to
i
.

\bullet

Remaining negative contributions
. There are a few negative contributions we are neglecting in our count, such as the the decrease of
R_{i}
after an infected unsaturated
vertex
i
updates, or after one of its neighbours does. The reason behind neglecting such terms is that these are smaller in magnitude than the ones already considered, and are not necessary to counteract the positive contributions we count below.

We now come to all the positive contributions, coming from the infections and from the updating of the infected vertices (which may reveal a less favourable environment). We will systematically express these positive terms so as to have them easily compared with and compensated by the negative ones.

\mathbf{(ii)\ Positive\ contributions}.

\bullet

Revealing the neighbourhood of infected unsaturated vertices.
In this contribution we group the terms coming from revealing the neighbourhood of infected unsaturated (slow) vertices after their own update. Observe that this leaves out the effect of revealing new neighbours as a result of the updating of said neighbours; this will be addressed in another contribution. Similarly as for saturated vertices, we count both the increase of the edge scores of the newly revealed present edges, and the one coming from the possibility of having
N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}
too large after the update. This gives a positive contribution bounded by

(71)

\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}\frac{2}{3}\kappa_{i}t_{i}\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}\frac{2}{3}\ \frac{s_{i}}{8r}}.

\bullet

Infections along clear edges
. The infection of a healthy vertex
i
through clear edges holds at rate
\lambda N^{\rm{cl}}_{i}(t)
and implies an increase of its vertex score of at most

m_{i}(t+\mathrm{d}t)-m_{i}(t)=(s_{i}+4R_{i}(t)t_{i})-2R_{i}(t)(s_{i}+t_{i})=s_{i}+2R_{i}(t)(t_{i}-s_{i})\leq s_{i}

Observe that if
i
is slow, the infection of
i
has the added effect of revealing its neighbourhood, thus increasing the edge score. Again, due to (
65
) this increase is bounded in average by
u_{i}\leq t_{i}
, so grouping together this term and the previous one we obtain a contribution bounded by

(72)

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}\sum_{i\text{ healthy}}\lambda N^{\rm{cl}}_{i}(t)(s_{i}+t_{i})}.

\bullet

Infections along present edges
. Each present slow edge
(i,j)
with
i
infected
transmits the infection at rate
\lambda
. This infection has the effect of making
i
,
j
and
(i,j)
saturated, while also possibly revealing the neighbours of
j
. The saturation of
j
implies a maximal increase in edge score of
s_{j}+t_{j}
while the new edges revealed imply an average increase in score of
u_{j}
. Both contributions cancel out with the saturation of
(i,j)
which imply a decrease in score of
s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}
. It follows that we only have to consider the increase in score given by the saturation of
i
, which is null if
i
was saturated, and is bounded by
t_{i}
otherwise (since
i
was already infected). It follows that this contribution is bounded by

(73)

\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}\lambda N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}(t)t_{i}\leq\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}\frac{3}{8}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}\kappa_{i}t_{i}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}3\ \frac{s_{i}}{8r}},

where in the first inequality we have used the bound
N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}\leq\frac{3T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}\kappa_{i}}{8\lambda}
which holds for unsaturated vertices.

\bullet

Infections along clogged edges and revealing of slow edges
(i,j)
by the update of
j
.

In this contribution we cluster the terms coming from two different effects, since the bounds obtained from each effect can be naturally merged into one. Consider first any infected vertex
i
. For any clogged edge
\{i,j\}
, we have transmission of an infection from
i
to
j
with intensity
\lambda p_{i,j}
. This increases:

(I.1)

The vertex score of
j
, which is bounded by
s_{j}+t_{j}
.

(I.2)

The edge score associated to the possible revealing of the neighbourhood of
j
(if
j
slow). By (
65
) the average increase of this score is bounded by
u_{j}
.

(I.3)

The vertex score of
i
by the increase of
R_{i}(t)
. This increase in score is equal to

\frac{4\lambda}{\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j}}t_{i}
if
i
is unsaturated.

Suppose now that
i
is an infected slow vertex (so its neighbourhood has been revealed) and fix any absent slow edge
(i,j)
. This edge becomes present by the update of
j
with intensity
\kappa_{j}p_{i,j}
(it can also become present by the update of
i
, but it was already taken into account). This has the effect of:

(II.1)

Increasing the edge score by
s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}
due to the new present edge.

(II.2)

Possibly making
i
saturated if
N^{\rm{pr}}_{i}(t)
becomes too large by this additional present edge. Since
i
was already infected, the score increase is at most
t_{i}
.

In order to deal with all these contributions suppose first that
i
is infected and quick. Grouping
(I.1)
and
(I.2)
together gives a contribution bounded by

\sum_{j}\lambda p_{i,j}(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j})\leq\frac{4}{3}\sum_{j}\lambda p_{i,j}s_{j}\leq\frac{4}{3}\frac{s_{i}}{\mathfrak{c}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}

where in the last inequality we have used (
41
). Suppose now that
i
is infected and slow. In that case the contributions
(I.1)
and
(I.2)
only arise for neighbours
j
that are quick, while for slow neighbours we can count the contribution coming from
(II.1)
:

\displaystyle\sum_{j\text{ quick}}\lambda p_{i,j}(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j})+\sum_{j\text{ slow}}\kappa_{j}p_{i,j}(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}(\lambda\wedge\kappa_{j})p_{i,j}(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{3}\sum_{j}(\lambda\wedge\kappa_{j})p_{i,j}s_{j}\leq\frac{4}{3}\frac{s_{i}}{\mathfrak{c}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}},

where this time we have used (
42
). We conclude then that from
(I.1)
,
(I.2)
and
(II.1)
we obtain the bound

\sum_{i\text{ infected}}\frac{4}{3}\frac{s_{i}}{\mathfrak{c}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}

It will be convenient to split this sum, counting separately over saturated and unsaturated vertices. Using the bounds
T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}\geq 8
and
T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}\geq\frac{8}{3\kappa_{i}}
we obtain

(74)

\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}\frac{4}{3}\frac{s_{i}}{\mathfrak{c}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}\frac{4}{3\mathfrak{c}}\frac{s_{i}}{8}}

and

(75)

\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{saturated}}}\frac{4}{3}\frac{s_{i}}{\mathfrak{c}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{saturated}}}\frac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}\kappa_{i}t_{i}}

Regarding
(I.3)
we first assume that
i
is quick and unsaturated so the total contribution coming from clogged edges incident to
i
is at most

\sum_{j}\lambda p_{i,j}\frac{4\lambda}{\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j}}t_{i}=4\lambda^{2}t_{i}\pi_{i}\leq\frac{T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}{4}\kappa_{i}t_{i}=\frac{s_{i}}{4r}

where in the inequality we have used that
T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}\geq 16\frac{\lambda^{2}\pi_{i}}{\kappa_{i}}
. Suppose now that
i
is slow. In this case we can use the fact that for any slow vertex
j
we have
\kappa_{j}\leq\lambda
to bound the contribution coming from
(II.2)
by

\sum_{j\text{ slow}}\kappa_{j}p_{i,j}t_{i}\leq\sum_{j\text{ slow}}\lambda p_{i,j}t_{i}\leq\sum_{j\text{ slow}}\lambda p_{i,j}t_{i}\frac{2\lambda}{\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j}}

Since vertices connected to
i
through clogged edges are necessarily quick we can thus add the terms coming from
(I.3)
and
(II.2)
(associated to
i
) together to obtain

\sum_{j\text{ quick}}\lambda p_{i,j}\frac{4\lambda}{\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j}}t_{i}+\sum_{j\text{ slow}}\kappa_{j}p_{i,j}t_{i}\leq\sum_{j}\frac{4\lambda^{2}}{\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j}}t_{i}p_{i,j}\leq\frac{s_{i}}{4r}

which is the same bound obtained for quick vertices, and hence we conclude the upper bound for the total contribution of terms coming from
(I.3)
and
(II.2)

(76)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}\frac{s_{i}}{4r}}

We can now group all the different contributions to the expected infinitesimal change. We begin by grouping (
67
), (
71
), (
73
), (
74
) and (
76
) together, so we arrive at

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}-\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}\left(4r-\frac{17}{3}-\frac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}\right)\frac{s_{i}}{8r}}=-\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}\frac{1}{21}\frac{s_{i}}{8r}\leq-\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}\frac{\kappa_{i}t_{i}}{21},

where in the equality we have injected the values
r=3/2
and
\mathfrak{c}=7
, and in the inequality we have used (
63
). Grouping together (
69
) and (
75
) yields the upper bound

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}-\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{saturated}}}\left(\frac{1}{3}-\frac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}\right)\kappa_{i}t_{i}=-\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ infected}}{\text{saturated}}}\frac{\kappa_{i}t_{i}}{21}.}

Next, we group together (
70
) and (
72
), which gives

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}-\sum_{i\text{ healthy}}\lambda N^{\rm{cl}}_{i}(t)(s_{i}+t_{i}).}

Using the basic observation
R_{i}(t)\leq N^{\rm{cl}}_{i}(t)
, we finally obtain that the average infinitesimal increase

\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[M_{t+\mathrm{d}t}-M_{t}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}]
is upper bounded by

(77)

-\sum_{i\text{ infected}}\frac{\kappa_{i}t_{i}}{21}-\sum_{i\text{ healthy}}\frac{\lambda}{2}m_{i}(t)-\sum_{(i,j)\text{ {present}}}\frac{2}{3}(\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j})(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}),

and since this quantity is negative we conclude that
M_{t\wedge T_{\rm{hit}}}
is a supermartingale.

To finish the proof of the theorem, we first consider the case
a>0
, and go back to the original contact process
X
, for which we can use self-duality to get

(78)

I_{N}(t)\leq\frac{\lceil aN\rceil}{N}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=\lceil aN\rceil+1}^{N}\mathbb{P}_{i}(t<T_{\rm{ext}}^{X}),

where
\mathbb{P}_{i}
stands for the law of the infection process with initially only vertex
i
infected, and
T_{\rm{ext}}^{X}
stands for its extinction time.
Since
X
is stochastically bounded from above by the new process up until the hitting time
T_{\rm{hit}}
, we know that on the event
T_{\rm{ext}}<T_{\rm{hit}}
, where
T_{\rm{ext}}
is the extinction time of the new process, we have
T_{\rm{ext}}^{X}\leq T_{\rm{ext}}
. Letting
T=T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge T_{\rm{ext}}
, we thus have

(79)

\mathbb{P}_{i}(t<T_{\rm{ext}}^{X})\leq\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{hit}}<T_{\rm{ext}})+\mathbb{P}_{i}(t<T).

To control the term
\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{hit}}<T_{\rm{ext}})
we use that
(M_{t\wedge T})
is a supermartingale to obtain

\mathbb{E}_{i}[M_{0}]\geq\mathbb{E}_{i}[M_{T}]=\mathbb{E}_{i}[M_{T_{\rm{hit}}}{\mathsf{1}}_{T_{\rm{hit}}<T_{\rm{ext}}}]+\mathbb{E}_{i}[M_{T_{\rm{ext}}}{\mathsf{1}}_{T_{\rm{hit}}>T_{\rm{ext}}}]=\mathbb{E}_{i}[M_{T_{\rm{hit}}}{\mathsf{1}}_{T_{\rm{hit}}<T_{\rm{ext}}}],

since
M_{T_{\rm{ext}}}\geq 0
. Now, at
T_{\rm{hit}}
we know that there is at least one star infected, so
M_{T_{\rm{hit}}}\geq s(a)
. On the other hand, under the assumption that at time
t=0
only
i
is infected, the total score of the configuration comes from its vertex score, and the initially revealed slow edges
(i,j)
(if
i
slow). From (
61
) and (
65
) we can thus bound

\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{hit}}<T_{\rm{ext}})\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{i}[M_{0}]}{s(a)}\leq\frac{s_{i}+\frac{t_{i}}{3}+u_{i}}{s(a)}\leq\frac{7}{6}\frac{s_{i}}{s(a)}.

To control the term
\mathbb{P}_{i}(t<T)
we observe that on
t<T
there is at least one vertex
j>aN
infected and hence from (
77
) we deduce that

\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[M_{t+\mathrm{d}t}-M_{t}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}]\leq-r

for some
r>0
independent of
i
,
t
and
N
(but which might depend on
a
and
\lambda
). Hence
M_{t\wedge T}+r(t\wedge T)
is a supermartingale, and in particular
r\mathbb{E}_{i}[T]\leq\mathbb{E}_{i}[M_{T}+rT]\leq\mathbb{E}_{i}[M_{0}]
. We infer that

\mathbb{P}_{i}(t<T)\leq\frac{1}{t}\mathbb{E}_{i}[T]\leq\frac{1}{rt}\mathbb{E}_{i}[M_{0}]\leq\frac{7s_{i}}{6rt}.

Finally, replacing the bounds found in (
79
) and using said bounds in (
78
) together with a simple sum-integral comparison yields

I_{N}(t)\leq a+\frac{1}{N}+\frac{7}{6s(a)}\int s(y)dy+\frac{7}{6rt}\int s(y)dy,

and hence we conclude (
44
).

In the case
a=0
with
(H)_{\delta}
satisfied for some
\delta\in[0,1]
, there is
C=C(a,\lambda)
independent of
N
such that
T^{\rm{loc}}s^{-\delta}\leq C
. We can use this to further improve (
77
) by observing that

\kappa_{i}t_{i}\geq\frac{1}{C}\kappa_{i}t_{i}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}s_{i}^{-\delta}=\frac{s_{i}^{1-\delta}}{Cr}\geq\frac{(s_{i}+t_{i}+u_{i})^{1-\delta}}{C^{\prime}},

for some
C^{\prime}
. It follows from (
77
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[M_{t+\mathrm{d}t}-M_{t}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}]\leq-\sum_{i\text{ infected}}\frac{\kappa_{i}t_{i}}{21}-\sum_{i\text{ healthy}}\frac{\lambda}{2}m_{i}(t)-\sum_{(i,j)\text{ present}}\frac{2}{3}\kappa_{j}t_{j}

\displaystyle\leq-\sum_{i\text{ infected}}\frac{(s_{i}+t_{i})^{1-\delta}}{21C^{\prime}}-\sum_{i\text{ healthy}}\frac{\lambda}{2}m_{i}(t)^{1-\delta}-\sum_{(i,j)\text{ present}}\frac{2}{3C^{\prime}}(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j})^{1-\delta}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{C^{\prime\prime}}M_{t}^{1-\delta},

where in the last inequality we used the concavity of the function
f(x)=x^{1-\delta}
.

In particular, if
\delta>0
we conclude that
M_{t\wedge T_{\rm{ext}}}^{\delta}+\frac{\delta}{C^{\prime\prime}}(t\wedge T_{\rm{ext}})
is a supermartingale and so

\mathbb{E}_{i}[T_{\rm{ext}}]=\mathbb{E}_{i}\left[T_{\rm{ext}}+\frac{C^{\prime\prime}}{\delta}M^{\delta}{T_{\rm{ext}}}\right]\leq\frac{C^{\prime\prime}}{r\delta}\mathbb{E}[M_{0}^{\delta}]\leq\frac{C^{\prime\prime}}{r\delta}\mathbb{E}[M_{0}]^{\delta}\leq\frac{C^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{7}{6}\int s(y)dy\right)^{\delta}}{\delta}N^{\delta}.

We get that (
43
) holds. When
\delta=0
we use that
\log(1+M_{t\wedge T_{\rm{ext}}})+r(t\wedge T_{\rm{ext}})/2
is a supermartingale to deduce the upper bound
\mathbb{E}[T_{\rm ext}]=O(\log N)
, so (
1c
) holds. For more details, we refer here to the similar computations made in the appendix of
[
13
]
.

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
4

The general strategy of the proof is the same as in the proof of Theorem
3
: We introduce a stochastic upper bound for the infection process and then associate a score with each configuration. We then prove that the scoring function, recording the evolution in time of the score, defines a supermartingale. We maintain the name
M(t)
for this scoring function, to emphasise the fact that several computations from the previous proof will be reused here.
However, our upper bound is much closer to the actual process and the analysis of the supermartingale therefore more complex. In particular, the scores depend on a finer classification of vertices allowing us to separate local and global features of the infection process in the analysis.

As before, the threshold
a
provided in the statement of the theorem defines stars
i<aN
and non-star vertices
i\geq aN
. We also recall the thresholds

a_{\rm{slow}}=(\lambda/\kappa_{0})^{-\frac{1}{\gamma\eta}}\quad\text{and}\quad a_{\rm{stsl}}=a_{\rm{str}}\wedge a_{\rm{slow}},

where
a_{\rm{str}}
was defined by (
46
) and which, by (
1
), is of order
\lambda^{2/\gamma}
.
Using these thresholds we introduce the new classification of the vertices, dividing them into
strong slow
,
strong quick
, and
weak
, as shown in Table
4
below. Without loss of generality we
suppose
a<a_{\rm{stsl}}
, as this is the case featuring the largest variety in the classification for nonstar vertices. This also implies that star vertices are strong slow vertices.

To
compare the current classification of vertices to the one used in the proof of Theorem
3
we consider separately the cases
\eta<-1/2
and
\eta\geq-1/2
. In the first case we have
a_{\rm{stsl}}\ll a_{\rm{str}}
, and hence the
strong slow vertices
coincide with slow vertices, while the quick vertices are subdivided into
strong quick
and
weak
. On the other hand, if
\eta\geq-1/2
, we have
a_{\rm{stsl}}=a_{\rm{str}}
, so
strong quick
vertices do not exist. In that case,
strong slow vertices
are still slow, but now weak vertices can be slow, too. Although weak vertices may exhibit qualitatively distinct behaviours, our methods allow us to work with these vertices without the need to differentiate between the quick and the slow ones.

Compared to Theorem
3
, the new classification introduced here allows for a finer control of the behaviour of the infection, combining a local and global element of the analysis. This can be observed when comparing the inequalities within the statements of both theorems: while inequalities (
41
) and (
42
) have analogous counterparts (
49
) and (
50
), in Theorem
4
we also find inequality (
48
) for weak vertices. Most noticeably, there is no
term
T^{\rm{loc}}
in (
48
), which is in line with the idea that local survival does not apply to weak vertices, and hence imposing this inequality follows closer the expected behaviour of the infection on weak vertices. With the new classification comes a small trade-off: a slight decrease in precision concerning the constants within the statement. Unlike in Theorem
3
, where the constant
\mathfrak{c}
was explicit, here we need it to be sufficiently large for the proof to work.

The stochastic upper bound introduced here has several differences with the one introduced in the proof of Theorem
3
(although many similarities as well), and to motivate its definition we point out the following observations:

•

Providing an upper bound for
I_{N}(t)
translates by (
78
) into bounding from above the extinction probability
of the contact process starting from a single infected non-star vertex. The infection should not be able to spread and survive for too long, unless it infects a star on its way. Hence we should not observe vertices with unusually large degree, short cycles, or other structures that are rare in the local limit of the (static) network. If we do, we stop the process. Part of the proof consists in showing that by that time the infection has already either died out or infected a star.

•

The stochastic upper bound for
(\mathscr{G}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({N}})}}_{t},X_{t})
is an infection process (akin to the contact process) running on top of an environment modelled as a growing oriented network
(G_{t})_{t\geq 0}
representing the environment seen by the infection. The growth of the network
is following the oriented infection paths
using updating rules similar to those in
\mathscr{G}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({N}})}}_{t}
, with one significant deviation from the previous model: connections are never destroyed but merely blocked for the infection. Hence the growing network serves not only to carry the infection process, but also to keep track of all the vertices and connections that have been revealed in the past. This information
can be used for the
global analysis
in a similar way as in the previous section.

•

The growing network is set up to have a tree structure,
which we exploit in order to control the behaviour of the infection on weak vertices. For these vertices, the scoring function
M(t)
is defined for the
local analysis
similarly as in
[
16
]
, where the authors study the contact process on static trees with degrees bounded by
1/(8\lambda^{2})
. Note that weak vertices have expected degree less than
1/(10\lambda^{2})
, and thus are quite likely to have degree bounded by
1/(8\lambda^{2})
. At the early stage of the infection, we will typically never visit a weak vertex with a degree higher than this threshold.

•

For our methods to work we need to control the degree of weak vertices, which, as seen in Table
5
can be slow. In particular, it is not viable to use the clogged/clear scheme introduced in the proof of Theorem
3
, where vertices steadily increase their degree until the next updating event. Instead, we are forced to reveal more of the network structure, which implies several complications. But this network structure will also imply simplifications in our computations as compared to the previous section.

The stochastic upper bound

We now define more precisely the upper bound consisting of an infection process running on top of a growing oriented network
(G_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. Despite the complexity of the process it will always be clear from the construction that up to the time when the process is stopped it represents a stochastic upper bound on the infection.

Let
\mathcal{E}
the set of all oriented edges of the vertex set
\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
excluding loops. The growing network is initialized at time
t=0
as some
G_{0}=(V_{0},E_{0})
to be described later, with vertex set
V_{0}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,N\}
and
E_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
. The network then evolves by connecting to new vertices from this set through edges in
\mathcal{E}
. Together with the evolution of the network
(G_{t})_{t\geq 0}
we also keep track of an accompanying process
(\mathfrak{A}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
of
available edges
, where
\mathfrak{A}_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{E}\setminus E_{t}
at each time
t
consists of all the oriented edges that can be used for the growth of the network. Each edge can only be used once and in only one of the two possible orientations in the construction of
G_{t}
, so the set of available edges decreases over time and the set inequality
\mathfrak{A}_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{E}\setminus E_{t}
is, in general, strict because as the network grows, several unused edges will be discarded.

We maintain the classification of vertices in
\{1,\ldots,N\}
distinguishing between strong slow, strong quick, and weak. Vertices can be either
healthy
or
infected
, and strong vertices (both quick and slow) can also be
saturated
, which is a state analogous to the one defined in the proof of Theorem
3
. We also classify each oriented edge
(i,j)
as either

•

weak
, if
j
is weak,

•

strong slow
if both
i
and
j
are strong slow,

•

or
strong quick
if
j
is strong (either quick or slow) but
(i,j)
is not strong slow,

see Table
6
. Observe that in general it is the head of the oriented edge
(i,j)
which determines its classification, except for strong slow edges. Also note that in the case
\eta\geq-1/2
, strong quick edges only occur if the head is strong slow and the tail is weak. In that case the term “strong” is a bit awkward, but, as will become clear below, it points towards the idea that the infection uses such an edge to discover a new strong vertex.

Besides their classification, edges also have a
state
which depends on their type and whether they belong to the network at a given time:

•

Edges that belong to
G_{t}
are either
present
or
absent
, regardless of their classification. The reader might think of this in the same way as in the original dynamic network, where present edges are used for the spread of the infection, while absent edges are not.

•

Strong slow edges that belong to
G_{t}
(and only these edges) can also become saturated, which is a state analogous to the one defined for slow edges in the proof of Theorem
3
. Again, the intuition is that saturated edges connect saturated vertices, and hence become useless (we provide the exact conditions for saturation later).

•

Available edges that are strong quick are

clogged
, which, as in Theorem
3
, can be thought of as constantly available channels for the infection, traversed at a much smaller rate.

•

Available edges that are either weak or strong slow are

unseen
, which is a state that reflects the fact that “these connections have not occurred so far”.

[formula]
[formula]

Strong slow

Strong quick

Weak

Strong slow

unseen/absent/

present/saturated

unseen/absent/present

Strong quick

Weak

clogged/absent/present

Table 6.
The possible states of an edge
(i,j)
. Edges are classified
strong slow
in the green,
strong quick
in the blue and
weak
in the yellow field.

The network
G_{t}
represents the graph structure that has been observed by the infection by time
t
. In this sense, the reader may find it useful to think of
G_{t}
as a window in which we (somewhat closely) track the structure belonging to
\mathscr{G}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({N}})}}_{t}
, and hence it makes intuitive sense for this window to grow over time, representing the growth of the region “explored” by the infection. This intuition may contrast with the intuition about the
actual
structure in
\mathscr{G}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({N}})}}_{t}
, where connections are destroyed over time. We reconcile both intuitions by pointing out that while
G_{t}
grows, most of its edges are labeled
absent
over time. From this point onwards, all comments about the growth of the network refer to
G_{t}
.

We now define the following operations:

(1)

Let
(i,j)
in
\mathfrak{A}_{t}
be an available edge at time
t
. We say that we
incorporate
the edge into the network, by:

•

Adding the vertex
j
to
G_{t}
. If
j
was already in
G_{t}
, we ignore this step.

•

Adding the edge
(i,j)
to
G_{t}
, changing its state to
present
.

•

Removing both
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
from
\mathfrak{A}_{t}
.

At times we abuse notation and say that we incorporate a vertex
j
. This amounts to saying that we incorporate the edge
(i,j)
if
i
is clear from context.

(2)

Let
i
be a vertex in
G_{t}
. We say that we
reveal new neighbours of
i
by:

•

Tossing an independent coin with probability
p_{i,j}
for each
unseen
edge
(i,j)
.

•

Incorporating to the network each edge whose toss turned up heads

We frequently specify some subset of the neighbours of
i
to reveal. This means that we only toss coins for the edges
(i,j)
with
j
belonging to the specified subset.

(3)

Let
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
. We say that we
reveal
i
by:

•

Tossing an independent coin with probability
p_{j,i}
for each
unseen
edge
(j,i)
,
j\in G_{t}
.

•

Incorporating to the network each edge whose toss turned up heads.

As in the previous operation, we might specify that
i
is revealed
to a particular subset of vertices
, which implies that we only toss coins for the edges
(j,i)
with
j
belonging to the specified subset.

Figure 4.
a)
An example where the vertex set of
G_{t}
is
\{1,2,3\}
, while
4
,
5
, and
6
have not been added to the network yet. Vertex
3
reveals new neighbours so we require to sample all
unseen
available edges leaving
3
. In this case, this includes the edge
(3,2)
, even though
2
already belongs to
G_{t}
. We also exclude the edge
(3,4)
which is
clogged
, represented through a squiggly arrow.
b)
After sampling all edges, only edge
(3,5)
is incorporated to the network, so in particular the new vertex set of
G_{t}
is
\{1,2,3,5\}
.

Figure 5.
c)
In the picture we see the network from Figure
4
where now it is vertex
4
that is being revealed. This requires to sample all
unseen
available edges entering
4
from vertices in
G_{t}
. Again, this excludes edge
(3,4)
which is
clogged
.
d)
After sampling all edges, both
(1,4)
and
(2,4)
are incorporated to the network. The creation of a cycle then stops the process.

The following observations are key about the newly defined operations:

(a)

Incorporating an edge means that the network grows by adding the edge (with the corresponding endpoint) to its structure, and since edges can only be incorporated once it must become unavailable afterwards along with its inverse.

(b)

When revealing new neighbours of a vertex
i
, we only reveal
unseen
edges, thus leaving out strong quick edges. The reason behind this is that in the new process, strong slow and weak edges behave similarly to the edges in the original dynamic network
\mathscr{G}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({N}})}}_{t}
, while strong quick edges behave similarly to the quick edges in the previous proof, see Figure
4
for an example.

(c)

Typically, when we reveal new neighbours of
i\in G_{t}
, these will not be in
G_{t}
and when we reveal a vertex
i
to a vertex
j\in G_{t}
, we have
i\notin G_{t}
. It is still possible that neighbours
i,j\in G_{t}
are revealed (and the tree structure is broken), see for example Figure
5
, but this is an unlikely and undesirable scenario upon which we stop the process, as we will explain later.

(d)

The classification of edges is such that strong quick vertices can never be revealed, and strong slow ones can only be revealed to strong slow vertices in
G_{t}
.

We say a vertex
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
has been
visited
by time
t
, if it has become infected or saturated at least once by that time. Since
G_{t}
represents the part of the environment observed by the infection by time
t
, visited vertices always belong to the network. The reader might think of non-visited vertices in
G_{t}
as places yet to be explored by the infection.

Using the new definitions, we can now begin to describe the evolution of the process and the scoring associated with the configurations it can reach.

Evolution of the process within
G_{t}

Setting aside the growth of the network, the rules followed by the process are analogous to the original contact process on
\mathscr{G}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({N}})}}_{t}
:

Rule (1):

Each vertex
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
updates at rate
\kappa_{i}
. If
i
belongs to
G_{t^{-}}
, then (among other effects we describe later) all edges
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
in
G_{t^{-}}
, whether previously present, absent, or saturated, are resampled as in
\mathscr{G}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({N}})}}_{t}
: for each such edge, we flip a coin with probability
p_{i,j}
and label
present
all the ones for which the coin turned up heads. We label
absent
all the rest.

Rule (2):

Within
(G_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the infection evolves as a regular contact process with infection rate
\lambda
(except for saturated vertices which we cover later). The edges used to spread the infection must always be
present
(not absent or saturated) and can be used regardless of their orientation.

Note that within
G_{t}
all edges and vertices are treated the same, regardless of their type, and oriented edges are treated as if they were unoriented. The classification of vertices has more impact on the network growth than on the changes and infections occurring within it.

Evolution of the process by inherited rules

Several of the evolution rules and scoring functions are adaptations of what was defined in the previous section. Before giving these rules we provide a brief (and incomplete) summary of the process described in Section
5
:

•

Vertices were divided into slow and quick, where each vertex
i
updated at rate
\kappa_{i}
.

•

When a slow vertex
i
updated, it lost its set of slow neighbours (which became
unrevealed
). However, if at that time
i
was infected, we sampled a new set of slow neighbours. Also, while
i
was infected (or saturated), every time another slow vertex
j
updated, its connection to
i
was resampled.

•

When a slow vertex
i
transitioned from
healthy
to
infected
or
saturated
, we added new slow neighbours among the ones that were
unrevealed
at that time.

•

Edges
\{i,j\}
with at least one quick vertex were classified as either
clogged
or
clear
. Clogged edges transmitted the infection at rate
\lambda p_{i,j}
, and at the infection event, the edge was cleared. Clear edges transmitted the infection at rate
\lambda
, and upon updating, become clogged again.

We refer the reader to Section
5.1
for the intuition behind this construction. We adapt these rules to the current scenario, with strong slow and strong quick vertices playing the role of slow and quick vertices, respectively:

Rule (3):

A strong slow vertex
i\in G_{t}
reveals new strong slow neighbours if either,

•

it updates while infected or saturated (
i
must be
visited
at time
t^{-}
), or

•

it transitions from
healthy
to
infected
or
saturated
.

Rule (4):

When a strong slow vertex
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
updates at a time
t
, it is revealed to the strong slow vertices in
G_{t}
that are infected.

Rule (5):

An infected vertex
i
transmits the infection through the clogged edges of the form
(i,j)
at rate
\lambda p_{i,j}
. When an infection of this sort takes place,
j
becomes infected,
(i,j)
is incorporated to the network and
j
becomes instantly infected (and thus visited), see for example Figure
6
.

Figure 6.
a)
In the picture
G_{t}
consists of the four vertices joined through solid arrows, with a single vertex infected, depicted as grey. Each vertex is classified as either weak (W), strong slow (Ss), or strong quick (Sq), and solid arrows marked by an
X
are
absent
. The infected vertex can infect its strong slow neighbour (but not the weak one) or transmit the infection through one of the
clogged
edges, depicted as squiggly arrows, at rate
\lambda p_{i,j}
.
b)
The infection is transmitted through the present edge. The newly infected strong slow vertex reveals new strong slow neighbours, which results in the incorporation of a new edge to the network.

A small difference between the rules given here, and the description of the process in Section
5
is that strong quick edges belonging to
G_{t}
do not become clogged again upon updating, and instead evolve as any other edge. In that sense, the current process is closer to the original dynamic network
\mathscr{G}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({N}})}}_{t}
, because it has “better memory”.

Evolution of the process at weak vertices and edges

As pointed out after the presentation of Theorem
3
at the beginning of Section
4.2
, the main improvement of Theorem
4
over Theorem
3
is related to the treatment of weak vertices. In this treatment, we need to control the degrees of these vertices at all times, which forces us to endow weak vertices and edges with a set of rules that closely resembles those in the original network
\mathscr{G}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({N}})}}_{t}
:

Rule (6):

When a visited vertex
i\in G_{t}
updates, it reveals new weak neighbours. This also occurs when
i
first becomes visited.

Rule (7):

When a weak vertex
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
updates, we reveal it to all visited vertices in
G_{t}
.

Note that Rule
(7)
introduces a steady growth of the network, while Rule
(6)
introduces jumps to this growth. Also new weak edges are only appended to visited vertices, since the structure around non-visited vertices has not yet been observed by the infection.

All the rules described so far act either simultaneously, or following a sequential intuitive ordering. For example, see Figure
7
, when a visited weak vertex within
G_{t}
updates, it resamples the state of the connections within
G_{t}
(following Rule
(1)
), reveals new weak neighbours (Rule
(6)
), and reveals itself to non-neighbouring vertices in
G_{t}
(Rule
(7)
), all at the same time.

Figure 7.
a)
The visited weak vertex (identified with a wide border) updates, so we require to resample its connection to the strong vertex at the top left, and both reveal new weak neighbours, and be revealed to the vertices in the network.
b)
After resampling its connection to the strong vertex, the edge becomes absent. Also, two new edges are incorporated to the network.

As a further example, when a vertex
i\in G_{t}
infects some
j\notin G_{t}
through a clogged edge,
j
is added to the network and becomes visited. Consequently, according to Rule
(6)
, it reveals new weak neighbours. If
j
is a strong slow vertex, then according to Rule
(3)
, it also reveals new strong slow neighbours. Observe that we have not provided the mechanisms involved in the saturation of vertices and edges. As some of the conditions for saturation require us to describe certain elements of the scoring function we will postpone it.

Starting and stopping the process

As pointed out before, the goal of the theorem is to upper bound the extinction probability
\mathbb{P}_{i_{0}}(t<T_{\rm{ext}})
of the contact process starting from a single infected non-star vertex. We fix such a vertex
i_{0}
and initialize
G_{0}
as follows:

•

Initially, we consider all possible oriented edges
(i,j)
in
\mathcal{E}
as
available
.

•

We start by adding
i_{0}
to the network and infecting it, thus declaring it
visited
.

•

Following Rules
(3)
and
(6)
, we reveal new weak and (possibly) strong slow neighbours of
i_{0}
, thereby defining
G_{0}
as well as
\mathfrak{A}_{0}
.

As mentioned at the beginning of this section, for our methods to work, we require the network to satisfy a few conditions that are likely to hold for some time. If one or more of these conditions are not met, we stop the process. Let
s
be the function in the statement of Theorem
4
, and introduce the stopping times:

T_{\rm{ext}}

the extinction time of the infection process,

T_{\rm{hit}}

the smallest time
t
such that

M(t)\geq s(a)
,

T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}

the smallest time
t
such that either
G_{t}
contains an (undirected) cycle, or an edge
(i,j)\in G_{t^{-}}
is resampled as
present
(following Rule
(1)
),

T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{deg}}

the smallest time
t
such that a weak vertex
i
is connected to at least
1/(8\lambda^{2})
weak vertices in
G_{t}
through present edges.

We also advance that there is an additional stopping time
T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{conn}}
, which is far more technical and will be defined in the next section, see (
104
),
since it requires several elements which will be introduced when defining the scoring function. The process stops at time
T
, which is defined as

T=T_{\rm{bad}}\wedge T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge T_{\rm{ext}},\qquad\text{ with }\qquad T_{\rm{bad}}=T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{deg}}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{conn}}.

The intuition behind the stopping time
T
is that we run the process until either the network stops behaving nicely (
T_{\rm{bad}}
), the infection goes extinct (
T_{\rm{ext}}
), or the score becomes too large (
T_{\rm{hit}}
). As will be seen later, the latter condition includes the critical event in which the infection reaches a star, or a star vertex is incorporated to
G_{t}
. In particular
G_{t}
contains only nonstar vertices at times
t<T_{\rm{hit}}
.
Moreover, at time
t<T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}
, the network
G_{t}
consists of a tree in which all edges point outwards from the root, which is the starting infected vertex
i_{0}
. Once again, we stress that the orientation of the edges is used only in the construction of
G_{t}
and the scoring, not for the propagation of the epidemics inside
G_{t}
.

At this point it is worth mentioning that once a vertex
i
becomes visited, we begin tracking its weak neighbourhood (following Rules
(6)
and
(7)
). After an update of
i
, with large probability all edges incident to
i
in
G_{t}
become absent. Thus, if
i
was healthy at the time of the update, it becomes inaccessible for the infection, never to be reached again. One might question whether tracking the weak neighbours of
i
could prematurely halt the process by reintroducing an absent edge or creating a loop in
G_{t}
. However, as we demonstrate in the last section, even in such cases, the infection is more likely to reach a star or extinction before such events occur.

Score attached to weak vertices

We begin describing the score given to configurations as long as the network behaves nicely, that is, for all
t<T_{\rm{bad}}
. The general score
M(t)
is divided as

M(t)=M_{\rm{strong}}(t)+M_{\rm{weak}}(t),

with
M_{\rm{strong}}(t)
and
M_{\rm{weak}}(t)
the scores attached to strong and weak vertices. In order to define
M_{\rm{weak}}(t)
we use that locally around a weak vertex the graph has a tree structure and methods from
[
16
]
developed for the contact process on static trees can be adapted to our situation.
We begin by recalling
[
16
]
, where the contact process was studied on a tree
\mathfrak{t}
with degrees bounded by
1/(8\lambda^{2})
. In this work the authors associate a scoring
W^{\rm{static}}_{j}(t)
to each vertex
j
, defined as

W^{\rm{static}}_{j}(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathfrak{t}\\
i\text{ infected}\end{subarray}}(2\lambda)^{d_{\mathfrak{t}}(i,j)},

where
d_{\mathfrak{t}}
is the graph distance within
\mathfrak{t}
. In order to prove that this scoring is a supermartingale, it is shown that each
W^{\rm{static}}_{j}(t)
has average infinitesimal increase bounded by
-W^{\rm{static}}_{j}(t)/4
. It follows that for any choice of nonnegative weights
w_{j}
on
\mathfrak{t}
, the process

(80)

\sum_{j\in\mathfrak{t}}w_{j}W^{\rm{static}}_{j}(t)=\sum_{i,j\in\mathfrak{t}}(2\lambda)^{d_{\mathfrak{t}}(i,j)}w_{j}{\bf{1}}_{i\text{ infected at time }t}

is a non-negative supermartingale. Observe that the process in (
80
), being a double sum, can be written as

\sum_{j\in\mathfrak{t}}w_{j}W^{\rm{static}}_{j}(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathfrak{t}\\
i\text{ infected}\end{subarray}}M^{\rm{static}}_{i}(t)

where, for each
i\in\mathfrak{t}
,

(81)

M^{\rm{static}}_{i}(t)=\sum_{j\in\mathfrak{t}}(2\lambda)^{d_{\mathfrak{t}}(i,j)}w_{j}

serves as a dual scoring function. Maybe surprisingly, we can use the same functions in the context of evolving graphs with only minor modifications.

Let
t\geq 0
and
i\in G_{t}
a weak vertex. We define
\mathfrak{t}(t)\subseteq G_{t}
as the subgraph induced by the weak vertices, where we only keep the present edges (which for all purposes will be taken unoriented). The resulting network corresponds to a collection of trees with maximum degree at most
1/(8\lambda^{2})
, since we assume that
t<T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{deg}}
, where we define the usual graph distance
d_{\mathfrak{t}}(i,j)
, with the convention
d(i,j)=\infty
if
i
and
j
are not connected.

Observe that vertices in
\mathfrak{t}
that are not labelled
visited
, have been revealed as neighbours of visited vertices, but have not revealed their own neighbourhoods yet. These vertices, which we call
pending leaves
, prevent us from choosing
w_{j}=s(j/N)
in (
80
), since upon being infected they would reveal new neighbours (following Rule
(6)
) and thus increase the score in average. To prevent this, we replace
w_{j}
with a slightly different scoring whose role is to overestimate the possible scoring obtained after revealing a new part of the structure. Let
\mathfrak{c}\geq 4
, which will be further specified later, and assume that
s
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
4
for this value of
\mathfrak{c}
.

We define the function

\tilde{s}_{i}:=\begin{cases}s_{i}&\mbox{ if }i\text{ is visited,}\\
\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{c}-2}s_{i}&\mbox{ if }i\text{ is a pending leaf,}\end{cases}

where
s_{i}=s(i/N)
. From the constraint
\mathfrak{c}\geq 4
, we thus have
s_{i}<\tilde{s}_{i}\leq 2s_{i}
. We now define for each
j
the individual scoring
M_{j}
similarly to (
81
) as

(82)

M_{i}(t)=\sum_{j\in\mathfrak{t}}(2\lambda)^{d(i,j)}\tilde{s}_{j}

under the assumption that
\lambda<1/2
. Observe that from this assumption and the choice
d(i,j)=\infty
for non-connected vertices, the sum can be restricted to the connected component of
i
. We thus define

M_{\rm{weak}}(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathfrak{t}\\
i\text{ infected}\end{subarray}}M_{i}(t).

Evolution of the process by saturation of vertices and edges

As in the process introduced in the previous section, vertex saturation is a state akin to being infected, but in which vertices cannot recover. When a vertex becomes saturated, it remains in this state, acting as a source of infection, until it updates. Even though the intuition is the same as in the proof of Theorem
3
, the conditions for saturation differ, and not all vertices can become saturated. We also maintain the intuition for edge saturation, which occurs when an edge connects two saturated vertices, allowing the use of the edge for infection transmission to be ignored. Before defining the exact conditions for saturation we introduce more notation.

Recall the definition of
\mathfrak{t}
as introduced in the previous subsection and let
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
be an arbitrary subset of
\mathfrak{t}
. We write

(83)

M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}(t):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\\
k\text{ infected}\end{subarray}}M_{k}(t)

to refer to the total scoring of
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
. Define also
\mathfrak{t}_{j}
as the connected component of
j
in
\mathfrak{t}
(for
j\in G_{t}
weak). With this notation, for any strong vertex
i\in G_{t}
, define
R_{ij}(t)
, for
j\in G_{t}
, as:

R_{ij}(t):=\begin{cases}\;\;0&\mbox{ if }j\mbox{ is strong slow,}\\[0.0pt]
\frac{\lambda}{\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j}}&\mbox{ if }j\mbox{ is strong quick,}\\[4.30554pt]
\frac{16\lambda M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}}(t)}{s_{j}}&\mbox{ if }j\mbox{ is weak.}\end{cases}

Using these functions define

(84)

R_{i}(t):=R_{i}^{\rm{strong}}(t)+R_{i}^{\rm{weak}}(t):=\sum_{j\,\rm{strong}\,\sim i}R_{ij}(t)+\sum_{j\,\rm{weak}\,\sim i}R_{ij}(t)

where
j\sim i
means, as usual, that
i
and
j
are neighbours in
G_{t}
(regardless of orientation). Finally, for a strong slow vertex
i\in G_{t}
, let
N^{\rm{ss}}_{i}
be the number of neighbours of
i
in the network that are strong slow. We can now introduce the evolution rules concerning saturated vertices and edges.

Rule (8):

A strong vertex
i\in G_{t}
is instantly declared
infected and saturated
as soon as any one of the following two conditions is met:

(i)

R_{i}(t)\geq 1/4
.

(ii)

i
is incident to a weak vertex
j
(through a present edge), and some vertex
k\in\mathfrak{t}_{j}
infects another strong vertex
i^{\prime}
.

If
i
is strong slow, we also declare it infected and saturated if

(iii)

since its last update it has infected another strong slow vertex or has been infected by another strong slow vertex,

(iv)

N^{\rm{ss}}_{i}(t)\geq\frac{3\kappa_{i}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}{8\lambda}
, where
T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}
is as in Definition
3
.

Rule (9):

Saturated vertices do not recover, and can only desaturate when updating. When a saturated vertex
i
updates, if none of the conditions introduced in Rule
(8)
hold, then the vertex is no longer saturated and becomes just infected.

Rule (10):

When a strong slow vertex
i
infects another strong slow vertex
j
, the edge between them becomes
saturated
.

The reader might wonder why, as opposed to the proof of Theorem
3
we do not need to “split” the slow strong edges within
G_{t}
. To answer this question recall that the splitting of edges was used to handle the possibility of reinfection between slow vertices, which for this process is impossible. We argue as follows: suppose that the strong slow edge
(i,j)
is incorporated to the network at some time
t
, at which time
j
must have been revealed as a (healthy) neighbour of
i
. Since the network is a tree, for
j
to become infected for the first time, it must receive the infection from
i
. At that point, both
i
and
j
become saturated (following Condition
(iii)
in Rule
(8)
). For
j
to reinfect
i
later on,
i
must be healthy, and hence must have updated at some point. However, in that case, the edge
(i,j)
would have become absent, thereby disconnecting
i
and
j
.

Observe that the definition of saturation in Theorem
3
involves conditions analogous to
(i)
,
(iii)
, and
(iv)
in Rule
(8)
. Condition
(ii)
, however, is new and might be quite astonishing with its long distance interaction, which does not even require
i
to be infected in the first place, see for example Figure
8
. It is however extremely useful, as it ensures that for a strong unsaturated vertex
i
, the weak trees
\mathfrak{t}_{j}
attached to it are not connected to any other strong vertex from which it could receive infections.

Figure 8.
In the picture,
i
is connected to the weak vertex
j
, and the vertices in
\mathfrak{t}_{j}
are depicted with a wide border. There is one infected vertex in
\mathfrak{t}_{j}
attempting to infect the strong vertex
i^{\prime}
through a
clogged
edge. If it were to succeed, then
i
would immediately become infected and saturated. Observe that the vertex at the right also could infect
i^{\prime}
, but in that case nothing would happen to
i
, because of the absent edge (with an
X
) on its path to
i
.

A first application of Condition
(ii)
is the justification of the new choice for the function
R_{i}(t)
in this proof. Observe that it is similar to the one introduced in the proof of Theorem
3
, the main difference being the introduction of the term
R_{ij}(t)=16\lambda M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}}(t)/s_{j}
for weak vertices. Recall that the role of
R_{ij}
is to provide an upper bound for the probability that the vertex
j
will later infect
i
before the connection is refreshed. With this in mind,

•

suppose that
i
is unsaturated and connected to
j
, and in what follows conditions
(i)-(iv)
are never met (since upon becoming saturated, infections from
j
to
i
are pointless). Because the event in condition
(ii)
does not occur, the tree
\mathfrak{t}_{j}
cannot be directly connected to any other strong vertex
i^{\prime}
, and hence in our interpretation of
R_{ij}
we assume that the infection is “trapped within
\mathfrak{t}_{j}
”.

•

In the next section we show that, when neglecting infections between strong and weak vertices,
M_{\rm{weak}}
is a supermartingale, and in fact,

\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}(t+\mathrm{d}t)}(t+\mathrm{d}t)-M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}(t)}(t)|\mathfrak{F}_{t}]\leq-\frac{1}{8}M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}(t)}(t),

where
\mathfrak{t}_{j}(t)
and
\mathfrak{t}_{j}(t+\mathrm{d}t)
are the connected components of
j
in
\mathfrak{t}
at times
t
and
t+\mathrm{d}t
, respectively. Using almost identical computations it can be proved that if we allow for infections between
i
and
j
, a similar upper bound is obtained if we replace
M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}(t)}(t)
by

\tilde{M}(t):=M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}(t)}(t)+{\bf{1}}_{i\text{ infected at time }t}\sum_{l\in\mathfrak{t}}(2\lambda)^{d(l,j)-1}\tilde{s}_{l}.

If we were to start the infection process at time
t
from a given configuration in which
i
is healthy, then, using the stopping time
\tilde{T}\geq t
in which either the infection dies out within
\mathfrak{t}_{j}
, or
i
becomes infected, we arrive at

\frac{s_{j}}{2\lambda}\mathbb{P}(j\text{ infects }i\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{t})\leq\mathbb{E}(\tilde{M}(\tilde{T})\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{t})\leq\tilde{M}(t)=M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}(t)}(t),

thus showing that
R_{ij}
fits the intended role.

•

When
i
first infects
j
, the value of
R_{ij}(t)
will be of order
\lambda
, and the same will be typically true when the infection propagates throughout
\mathfrak{t}_{j}
. This is an improvement over the expression
\lambda/(\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j})
used for strong quick vertices or in the proof of Theorem
3
.

Score attached to strong vertices and edges

We now address the remaining expression
M_{\rm{strong}}(t)
from the decomposition
M(t)=M_{\rm{weak}}(t)+M_{\rm{strong}}(t)
, which is analogous to the scoring introduced in the proof of Theorem
3
, so in particular we use the same notation: We recall the functions
\pi
and
T^{\rm{loc}}
introduced in Definition
3
,

\displaystyle\pi(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{p(x,y)}{\kappa(x)+\kappa(y)}\,\mathrm{d}y,

\displaystyle T^{\rm{loc}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max\left\{8,\frac{8}{3\kappa(x)},16\lambda^{2}\frac{\pi(x)}{\kappa(x)}\right\},

and take
a(\lambda)
and
s(x)
satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem
4
, that is, (
48
), (
49
), and (
50
), as well as assumptions (H1) and (H2). As in the previous proof we use the subscript notation when considering the corresponding functions defined on
\{1,\ldots,N\}
. We now define the scoring
M_{\rm{strong}}
as

(85)

M_{\rm{strong}}(t):=\sum_{i\in G_{t}\text{ strong}}m_{i}(t)+{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{(i,j)\text{ present}}{\text{strong slow}}}s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}},

with

m_{i}(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}s_{i}+t_{i}&\mbox{if }i\mbox{ is \color[rgb]{1,0,1} infected saturated},\\
s_{i}+4R_{i}(t)t_{i}&\mbox{if }i\mbox{ is \color[rgb]{1,0,0} infected unsaturated},\\
2R_{i}(t)(s_{i}+t_{i})&\mbox{if }i\mbox{ is \color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13} healthy},\end{array}\right.

and again, for some
r\geq 3/2
to be fixed later,

t_{i}:=\frac{s_{i}}{r\kappa_{i}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}},\qquad u_{i}=\frac{t_{i}}{3}.

Regarding the scoring we point out the following useful facts:

•

Since
T^{\rm{loc}}_{j}\geq\frac{8}{3\kappa_{i}}
and
T^{\rm{loc}}_{j}\geq 8
, it easily follows from the definition of
t_{i}
that inequalities

(86)

t_{i}\leq\frac{s_{i}}{4}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\kappa_{i}t_{i}\leq\frac{s_{i}}{8r}

hold as in the previous section. Similarly, as in (
61
), for any strong slow vertex
i
,

(87)

\frac{8\lambda}{3\kappa_{i}T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}\sum_{j\leq a_{\rm{stsl}}N}p_{ij}\leq\frac{1}{3},

by exploiting the fact that
a_{\rm{stsl}}\leq a_{\rm{slow}}
. As a last adaptation of previous computations, we can require that
\mathfrak{c}\geq 4r
(by fixing
\mathfrak{c}
after fixing
r
), which gives

(88)

\sum_{j<a_{\rm{stsl}}N}p_{ij}(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j})\leq u_{i},

analogously to (
65
).

•

As in the previous proof, the score related to the connection between strong slow vertices is attached to the strong slow edges rather than the vertices (because
R_{ij}=0
if both vertices are strong slow). Note that this is one of the few instances where the orientation of an edge
(i,j)\in G_{t}
does matter, since the score value depends on the endpoint
j
alone. Again, this scoring reflects the idea that
j
is very likely to get infected by
i
.

•

A star vertex
j
can be incorporated to
G_{t}
without being infected only through revealing a present strong slow edge
(i,j)
, with associated score
s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}>s(a)
. If this happens we instantly reach
T_{\rm{hit}}
.

•

The scoring of strong vertices only depend on the weak vertices through
R^{\rm{weak}}
, while the scoring of weak vertices do not depend on the strong vertices.

•

Non visited vertices have null score. This follows since the only non visited vertices that can be found in
G_{t}
are either healthy weak vertices (for which the scoring is null), or strong slow vertices with a single neighbour
j\in G_{t}
, which is also strong slow (so the score is null since
R_{ij}=0
if
j
is strong slow). A similar analysis shows that when a strong vertex
i
reveals new neighbours, it does not increase its score.

Supermartingale property of
M(t)

In this section we compute the average infinitesimal change of
M(t)
due to the evolution of the process and show that it is a nonnegative supermartingale. Before the computations we point out two important facts:

(1)

The scoring
M(t)
has only been defined at times
t
satisfying
t<T_{\rm{bad}}
, so for the scoring to be useful, we need to define what the scoring should be when reaching a configuration giving
t=T_{\rm{bad}}
. Fortunately, since
M(t)
is nonnegative, it is enough to set
M(T_{\rm{bad}})=0
without any risk of losing the supermartingale property.

(2)

We have not yet defined the stopping time
T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{conn}}
. The definition will be given at the end of this section, but we advance that it comprises the scenarios in which strong vertices are connected to too many weak trees where the infection has proliferated.

As in the proof of Theorem
3
, we show that
M(t)
is a supermartingale by fixing
t>0
, and writing the expected infinitesimal change of the score

(89)

\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[M(t+\mathrm{d}t)-M(t)|\mathfrak{F}_{t}]

as a large sum of terms associated with the effects that a single transition event of the process have on the scoring of the vertices and edges. In what follows we assume that:

•

At time
t
we have not yet stopped the process. Even though considering times
t\geq T
is harmless for the supermartingale property (because in that case
M(t+\mathrm{d}t)-M(t)=0
), it is important for our computations to obtain strictly negative upper bounds for (
89
).

•

The transition events considered preserve the tree event (that is
t+\mathrm{d}t<T_{\rm{bad}}
). Again, this is a harmless assumption since by definition
M(T_{\rm{bad}})=0
.

Analogously to the proof of Theorem
3
, we group these terms into
contributions
, many of them identical to the ones presented in the previous section. We divide these contributions according to the types of vertices involved in the events taking place. In order to avoid cluttered notation, we will drop the dependence on time for all functions for the rest of this section, provided there is no risk of confusion.

\mathbf{(i)\;\;\text{\bf Contributions from events that only involve weak vertices}}.

For any fixed
t\geq 0
let
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
be a connected component of
\mathfrak{t}
at time
t
. Our main goal will be to show that the expressions appearing in

(90)

\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[M_{\rm{weak}}(t+\mathrm{d}t)-M_{\rm{weak}}(t)|\mathfrak{F}_{t}]

as a result of events that involve vertices in
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
, and their interactions with other weak vertices, can be upper bounded by
-\frac{1}{8}M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}(t)
, where
M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}
was defined in (
83
). We first observe that because we are assuming
t+\mathrm{d}t<T_{\rm{bad}}
, we exclude the possibility of connecting vertices in
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
to other vertices in
G_{t}\setminus\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
and hence the score of the latter vertices is unaffected. On the other hand, weak vertices that are added to
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
are healthy, so their score is not counted when computing
M_{\rm{weak}}(t+\mathrm{d}t)
. Hence, for these events it is enough to upper bound

\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}(t+\mathrm{d}t)-M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}(t)|\mathfrak{F}_{t}],

where it is important to bear in mind that
M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}(t+\mathrm{d}t)
stands for the collective score at time
t+\mathrm{d}t
of the vertices that belonged to the connected component at time
t
(the connected component might have changed at time
t+\mathrm{d}t
). As with (
89
), the expression above corresponds to a large sum of terms, which we group depending on the type of event they originate from:

\bullet

Infections and recoveries within
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
.
In this contribution we bound the average infinitesimal changes in score due to the recoveries of vertices in
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
, as well as the infections between these vertices. Notice that this must take into account the possible reveal of new neighbours as a result of infecting a previously non visited vertex (following Rule
(6)
). Fix
j\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
with
j
infected, and consider the following events:

-

j
recovers. This event occurs at rate
1
, and it results in the exclusion of
M_{j}
from
M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}
(all other terms remain unchanged). It follows that this event yields a total contribution

(91)

-\sum_{i\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}\tilde{s}_{i}(2\lambda)^{d(i,j)}.

-

j
infects an already visited neighbour
j^{\prime}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
. This event results in the inclusion of
M_{j^{\prime}}
into
M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}
, while all other terms remain unchanged. Since for each
j^{\prime}\sim j
, this occurs at rate
\lambda
, the event results in the total contribution

(92)

\lambda\sum_{{\begin{subarray}{c}{j^{\prime}\sim j}\\
{j^{\prime}\text{ visited}}\end{subarray}}}\sum_{i\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}\tilde{s}_{i}(2\lambda)^{d(i,j^{\prime})}.

-

j
infects a pending leaf
j^{\prime}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
. This is the most delicate event, since it has the added effect of changing the value of
\tilde{s}_{j^{\prime}}
and connecting new vertices to
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
. We break down the changes in score as follows:

*

For every
k\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
, the change in value of
\tilde{s}_{j^{\prime}}
implies a decrease of
M_{k}
by
\frac{2}{\mathfrak{c}-2}s_{j^{\prime}}(2\lambda)^{d(k,j^{\prime})}
.

*

For every
k\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
, the connection of a vertex
l
to
j^{\prime}
implies an increase of
M_{k}
by

\tilde{s}_{l}(2\lambda)^{d(k,l)}=\frac{2\lambda\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{c}-2}s_{l}(2\lambda)^{d(k,j^{\prime})}

where we have used that
d(k,l)=d(k,j^{\prime})+1
and the fact that
l
is not visited (for the definition of
\tilde{s}_{l}
).

*

Since every weak vertex
l\notin G_{t}
becomes a neighbour of
j^{\prime}
with probability
p_{j^{\prime},l}
, it follows that the average change of
M_{k}
is upper bounded by

-\frac{2}{\mathfrak{c}-2}s_{j^{\prime}}(2\lambda)^{d(k,j^{\prime})}+\frac{2}{\mathfrak{c}-2}(2\lambda)^{d(k,j^{\prime})}\sum_{l\text{ weak}}\lambda\mathfrak{c}p_{j^{\prime},l}s_{l}\leq 0

where we have used (
48
) to deduce that
\sum\lambda\mathfrak{c}p_{j^{\prime},l}s_{l}\leq s_{j^{\prime}}
.

Since the effect of revealing new vertices decreases all the
M_{k}
in average, it follows that the change of
M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}
by infecting a pending leaf can be upper bounded by the one given by the infection of an already visited vertex. We conclude that the total contribution is bounded by

(93)

\lambda\sum_{{\begin{subarray}{c}{j^{\prime}\sim j}\\
{j^{\prime}\text{ non visited}}\end{subarray}}}\sum_{i\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}\tilde{s}_{i}(2\lambda)^{d(i,j^{\prime})}.

\bullet

Network dynamics.
In this contribution we upper bound the average changes of
M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}
that follow from the updates of weak vertices (not necessarily in
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
). Observe that on these events the infection does not spread so
M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}
does not increase by adding new terms
M_{j}
, but rather by modifying the values
M_{i}
of all currently infected vertices in
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
. Fix then any such infected
i
and observe that as long as
t+\mathrm{d}t<T_{\rm{bad}}
, the updating of another
i^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{t}
can only decrease
M_{i}
(because the connected component of
i
in
\mathfrak{t}
is reduced) and hence the only transitions of the network that may increase
M_{i}(t)
are:

*

the update of
i
, or

*

the update of an unseen vertex
k
that then connects to some visited vertex
j\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
, becoming a pending leaf.

In the first case, the resulting tree
\mathfrak{t}_{i}(t+\mathrm{d}t)
containing
i
will just consist of
i
and some pending leaves
j
. The average value of
M_{i}(t+\mathrm{d}t)
will then be

(94)

M_{i}(t+\mathrm{d}t)\leq s_{i}+\sum_{j\text{ weak}}\frac{2\lambda\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{c}-2}p_{i,j}s_{j}\leq s_{i}+\frac{2}{\mathfrak{c}-2}s_{i}=\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{c}-2}s_{i}

where in the second inequality we have used (
48
). In the second case, the unseen vertex
k
that connects to
j\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
will add to
M_{i}
the quantity
(2\lambda)^{d(i,j)+1}\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{c}-2}s_{k}
. Since the update of
i
occurs at rate
\kappa_{i}\leq\kappa_{0}
, and each
k
attaches to a visited vertex
j
with rate
\kappa_{k}p_{j,k}\leq\kappa_{0}p_{j,k}
we deduce that the average change of
M_{i}
due to these events is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{c}-2}s_{i}-M_{i}(t)\right)+\sum_{j\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}\sum_{k\text{ weak}}\kappa_{0}p_{j,k}(2\lambda)^{d(i,j)+1}\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{c}-2}s_{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{c}-2}M_{i}-M_{i}\right)+\sum_{j\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}\frac{2\kappa_{0}}{\mathfrak{c}-2}(2\lambda)^{d(i,j)}s_{j}=\frac{4\kappa_{0}}{\mathfrak{c}-2}M_{i},

where we have used that
s_{i}\leq M_{i}
(since
i
is infected), as well as (
48
), and in the equality we have used the definition of
M_{i}
. Adding all these average changes, and taking
\mathfrak{c}
sufficiently large we obtain that the total contribution given by network dynamics events is

(95)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\\
i\text{ infected}\end{subarray}}\frac{4\kappa_{0}}{\mathfrak{c}-2}M_{i}\leq\frac{1}{8}M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}.

By adding the terms in (
91
), (
92
), and (
93
), for all infected vertices
j\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
, as well as the contribution obtained in (
95
), we obtain that the expressions appearing in
\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}(t+\mathrm{d}t)-M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}(t)|\mathfrak{F}_{t}]
can be upper bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\\
j\text{ infected}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}\tilde{s}_{i}\left(\sum_{j^{\prime}\sim j}\lambda(2\lambda)^{d(i,j^{\prime})}-(2\lambda)^{d(i,j)}\right)+\frac{1}{8}M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\\
j\text{ infected}\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}\tilde{s}_{i}\left(\lambda(2\lambda)^{d(i,j)-1}+\frac{1}{8\lambda^{2}}(2\lambda)^{d(i,j)+1}-(2\lambda)^{d(i,j)}\right)+\frac{1}{8}M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\\
j\text{ infected}\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}\tilde{s}_{i}(2\lambda)^{d(i,j)}+\frac{1}{8}M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}=-\frac{1}{8}M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}},

where in the inequality we have used that for any pair of vertices
i,j\in\mathfrak{t}
, there is at most one vertex
j^{\prime}\sim j
satisfying
d(i,j^{\prime})=d(i,j)-1
, while the rest of the (at most)
\frac{1}{8\lambda^{2}}
neighbours of
j
satisfy
d(i,j^{\prime})=d(i,j)+1
.

Now that we have shown that the events involving vertices in
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
and their interactions with other weak vertices induce an average change of at most
-\frac{1}{8}M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}
in
M_{\rm{weak}}
, we can consider all events involving only weak vertices (such that
t+\mathrm{d}t<T_{\rm{bad}}
) by adding all these contributions to obtain that their contribution to (
90
) is upper bounded by

-\frac{1}{8}M_{\rm{weak}}(t).

Similarly, it can be shown that the contribution of said events to

\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}(t+\mathrm{d}t)}(t+\mathrm{d}t)-M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}(t)}(t)|\mathfrak{F}_{t}]

is upper bounded by
-\frac{1}{8}M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}(t)}(t)
. Indeed, we can repeat the exact same computations as for
M_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}}
, with the only exception of the case in which a vertex
j^{\prime}\neq j
in
\mathfrak{t}_{j}
updates, which then becomes detached from
j
. We exploit this upper bound, together with the fact that for each strong vertex
i
, its scoring depends on the weak vertices only through the terms
R_{ij}
, which depend linearly on the corresponding
M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}}
to finally conclude that the contribution of events involving only weak vertices to the average change in the total score
M(t)
is upper bounded by

(96)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-\frac{1}{8}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i\ \text{weak}}{\text{ infected}}}M_{i}(t)}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ strong infected}}{\text{ unsaturated}}}-\frac{4}{8}R_{i}^{\rm{weak}}(t)t_{i}}+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ strong}}{\text{ healthy}}}-\frac{2}{8}R_{i}^{\rm{weak}}(t)(s_{i}+t_{i})}.

\mathbf{(ii)\;\;\text{\bf Contributions from events that only involve strong vertices}}.

Observe that events only involving strong vertices do not affect
M_{\rm{weak}}
. Indeed, the scoring of each
M_{i}
depends on
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
which is unaffected by these events, while the possible revealing of new weak vertices (as neighbours of strong ones) also leave
