M_{\rm{weak}}
unaffected, since these new vertices are healthy. It follows that the events involving exclusively strong vertices only impact
M_{\rm{strong}}
.

Noting the inherited rules concerning strong vertices, the almost identical score function to the one in the proof of Theorem
3
, as well as the bonds (
86
), (
87
), and (
88
), it follows that the computations arising from these events are analogous to the ones in the previous proof, so we refrain from repeating them, although we point out that:

•

When vertices update, the already revealed edges turn absent instead of unrevealed. This does not affect our computations since we always use worst-case scenario bounds. This actually improves some of the negative terms.

•

The contributions obtained in (
70
) and (
72
), which depended on clear edges, now depend on present edges
(i,j)
where both
i
and
j
are strong, but where at least one of them is strong quick. The term
N^{\rm{cl}}_{i}(t)
appearing in both terms is now replaced by the number of all such edges incident to
i
.

•

The contribution computed in (
71
) still relates to the event in which an infected unsaturated strong slow vertex updates, thus revealing new neighbours. In that case only new strong slow neighbours affect the scoring.

•

The contribution computed in (
73
) now relates to the transmission of the infection through a strong slow edge.

Following the same computations leading to (
77
) we obtain a total contribution of

(97)

\begin{split}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}-\sum_{{i\text{ infected}}\atop{\text{ unsaturated}}}\left(4r-\tfrac{17}{3}-\tfrac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}\right)\tfrac{s_{i}}{8r}}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\sum_{{i\text{ infected}}\atop{\text{saturated}}}\left(\tfrac{1}{3}-\tfrac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}\right)\kappa_{i}t_{i}}\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}\sum_{{i\text{ healthy}}\atop{\text{strong}}}\lambda N^{\rm{sq}}_{i}(t)(s_{i}+t_{i})}-{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sum_{{(i,j)\text{ present}}\atop{\text{strong slow}}}\tfrac{2}{3}(\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j})(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j})},\end{split}

where
N^{\rm{sq}}_{i}
is the number of non strong slow present edges connecting
i
to strong vertices.

\mathbf{(iii)\;\;\text{\bf Contributions from events that involve both strong and weak vertices}}.

The remaining transitions to consider are events that follow from infections of weak vertices by strong ones, and vice-versa.
Moreover, we emphasize that the infection of a strong vertex by a weak one along a clogged edge can also lead to infection and saturation of other strong vertices by the “long distance saturation” rule.

\bullet

Infection of weak vertices by strong neighbours.
The infection of a weak vertex
j
by a neighbouring strong infected vertex
i
happens at rate
\lambda
. Following similar computations to the ones in (
94
), we deduce that
j
increases its score in average by
M_{j}(t)
(even if infecting
j
resulted in revealing new neighbours). It follows that if
i
is unsaturated, then it increases its score by at most
64\lambda M_{j}(t)t_{i}/s_{j}
. This yields the contribution

(98)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ strong infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}64\lambda^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\text{ weak}\sim i\\
j\text{ healthy}\end{subarray}}\frac{M_{j}(t)}{s_{j}}t_{i}}+{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ strong}}{\text{infected}}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j{\textrm{ weak}}\sim i\\
j\text{ healthy}\end{subarray}}\lambda M_{j}(t)}.

\bullet

Infection of a strong healthy vertex by a connected weak vertex
. The infection of a strong vertex
i
by a weak infected vertex
j
through a present edge holds at rate
\lambda
, leading to the contribution

\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ strong}}{\text{ healthy}}}\ \ \sum_{j\text{ weak infected }\sim i}\lambda(s_{i}+t_{i}).

Observing that
j
is infected, we have
M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}}\geq s_{j}
and thus
R_{ij}(t)\geq 16\lambda
, we can further bound this contribution by

(99)

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ strong}}{\text{ healthy}}}\frac{R_{i}^{\rm{weak}}(t)}{16}(s_{i}+t_{i})}.

\bullet

Infection of a strong healthy vertex by a weak one along a clogged edge
.
The infection of a strong vertex
j
by a weak infected vertex
i
holds at rate
\lambda p_{ij}
when the edge is clogged. Apart from a possible long distance effect, which is taken into account separately below, this transmission creates an average score associated to
j
and to the strong slow vertices pending to
j
(when
j
is slow) at most of

(s_{j}+t_{j})+u_{j}\leq\tfrac{4}{3}s_{j}.

Summing over
i
and
j
and using the inequality (
48
) available for the weak vertex
i
, we obtain a contribution bounded by

(100)

\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ weak}}{\text{ infected}}}\frac{4}{3\mathfrak{c}}s_{i}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ weak}}{\text{ infected}}}\frac{4}{3\mathfrak{c}}M_{i}(t)}.

\bullet

Long distance saturation.
For each strong unsaturated
i
and each weak neighbour
j
of
i
, a vertex
k\in\mathfrak{t}_{j}
may infect another strong vertex (through a clogged edge), leading to infection and saturation of
i
, and thus a score increase of at most
t_{i}
if
i
infected, or
(s_{i}+t_{i})+u_{i}
if
i
healthy (taking into account the possible revealing of strong slow vertices pending to
i
in that case).
For each such
i
and
j
, this holds at rate

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathfrak{t}_{j}\\
\text{infected}\end{subarray}}\sum_{i^{\prime}\text{ strong}}\lambda p_{k,i^{\prime}}\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathfrak{t}_{j}\\
\text{infected}\end{subarray}}\lambda\int_{x^{\prime}<a_{\rm{str}}}p(\tfrac{k}{N},x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\leq\frac{\lambda}{s(a_{\rm{str}})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathfrak{t}_{j}\\
\text{infected}\end{subarray}}s_{k}\leq\frac{\lambda M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}}}{s_{j}}=\frac{R_{ij}}{16},

where the third inequality follows from (H1) and the last inequality from the definition of
M_{\mathfrak{t}_{j}}
and
s_{j}\leq s(a_{\rm{str}})
.
Summing over the choices of
i
and
j
, we obtain a contribution bounded by

(101)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ strong infected}}{\text{unsaturated}}}\frac{1}{16}R_{i}^{\rm{weak}}(t)t_{i}}+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ strong}}{\text{ healthy}}}\frac{1}{8}R_{i}^{\rm{weak}}(t)s_{i}}.

\mathbf{(iv)\;\;\text{\bf Total contribution}}.

Grouping the contributions obtained in (
96
), (
97
), (
98
), (
99
), (
100
), and (
101
), we can upper bound the infinitesimal increase of
M
as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[\left(M(t+\mathrm{d}t)-M(t)\right)|\mathfrak{F}_{t}]

\displaystyle\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ weak}}{\text{infected}}}I_{i}(t)}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ strong}}{\text{\rm{pending}}}}I_{i}^{\rm{pend}}(t)}+\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ strong infected}}{ \text{unsaturated}}}I_{i}^{\rm{inf}}(t)}+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ strong}}{\text{healthy}}}I_{i}^{\rm{hea}}(t)}+\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{i \text{ strong infected}}{\text{saturated}}}I_{i}^{\rm{sat}}(t)},

where the expressions of the terms inside the sums are given by

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}I_{i}(t)}

\displaystyle=-\left(\tfrac{1}{8}-\tfrac{4}{3\mathfrak{c}}\right)M_{i}(t)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}I_{i}^{\rm{pend}}(t)}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{2}{3}\kappa_{i}(s_{i}+t_{i}+u_{i}).

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}I_{i}^{\rm{inf}}(t)}

\displaystyle=-\left(4r-\tfrac{17}{3}-\tfrac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}\right)\tfrac{s_{i}}{8r}+\sum_{j{\text{ weak}}\sim i}\lambda M_{j}(t)+64\lambda^{2}t_{i}\sum_{j{\text{ weak}}\sim i}\tfrac{M_{j}(t)}{s_{j}}-\tfrac{7}{16}R^{\rm{weak}}_{i}(t)t_{i},

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}I_{i}^{\rm{hea}}(t)}

\displaystyle=-\left(\lambda N^{\rm{sq}}_{i}(t)+\tfrac{1}{16}R^{\rm{weak}}_{i}(t)\right)(s_{i}+t_{i}),

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}I_{i}^{\rm{sat}}(t)}

\displaystyle=-\left(\tfrac{1}{3}-\tfrac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}\right)\kappa_{i}t_{i}+\sum_{j{\text{ weak}}\sim i}\lambda M_{j}(t).

Taking
r
and
\mathfrak{c}
large with still
\mathfrak{c}>4r,
we have that the terms
\mbox{$\frac{1}{8}$}-\mbox{$\frac{4}{3\mathfrak{c}}$}
,
\mbox{$\frac{1}{3}$}-\mbox{$\frac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}$}
and
4r-\mbox{$\frac{17}{3}$}-\mbox{$\frac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}$}
are all positive, as well as

4r-6-\mbox{$\frac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}$}
. Define the expressions,

\displaystyle J_{i}(t):=\sum_{j{\text{ weak}}\sim i}\lambda M_{j}(t),\qquad K_{i}(t):=\sum_{j{\text{ weak}}\sim i}\frac{M_{j}(t)}{s_{j}}

and note that as soon as there is some
\varepsilon>0
such that the inequalities

(102)

\displaystyle J_{i}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\tfrac{1}{3}-\tfrac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}-\varepsilon\right)\kappa_{i}t_{i},

(103)

\displaystyle K_{i}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\left(4r-6-\tfrac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}-\varepsilon\right)\tfrac{\pi_{i}}{32},

hold, then (
102
) ensures that
I_{i}^{\rm{sat}}(t)\leq-\varepsilon\kappa_{i}t_{i}
as well as

J_{i}(t)\leq\tfrac{\kappa_{i}t_{i}}{3}=\tfrac{s_{i}}{3rT^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}\leq\tfrac{1}{3}\tfrac{s_{i}}{8r},

while (
103
) ensures that

64\lambda^{2}t_{i}K_{i}(t)\leq(4r-6-\mbox{$\frac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}$}-\varepsilon)\mbox{$\frac{s_{i}}{8r}$}
and further
I_{i}^{\rm{inf}}(t)\leq-\varepsilon s_{i}/8r
, so in particular all the contributions become negative. With this in mind we define at last the missing stopping time
T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{conn}}
as

(104)

T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{conn}}=\inf\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}t\geq 0,\;\text{\eqref{LD_for_J} or~\eqref{LD_for_K}
fails to hold for at least}\\
\text{one vertex $i$ amongst the visited strong vertices}\end{array}\right\},

for some values of
r
,
\mathfrak{c}
and
\varepsilon>0
that are still not fixed, but satisfy all previously mentioned constraints. We then have:

(105)

\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}[M_{t+\mathrm{d}t}-M_{t}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}]\leq-c_{\eqref{eq:negative_infinitesimal_increase}}{\mathsf{1}}_{t<T},

for some
c_{\eqref{eq:negative_infinitesimal_increase}}>0
independent of
i_{0}
,
t
, and
N
. We therefore conclude that both
(M(t\wedge T))_{t\geq 0}
and
(M(t\wedge T)+c_{\eqref{eq:negative_infinitesimal_increase}}(t\wedge T))_{t\geq 0}
are supermartingales.

Exploiting the supermartingale

As in the previous proof, our aim is to exploit the supermartingale property to prove Theorem
4
. First, we bound
\mathbb{P}_{i}(t<T_{\rm{ext}})
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{i}(t<T_{\rm{ext}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{i}(t\leq T)+\mathbb{P}_{i}(T=T_{\rm{hit}})+\mathbb{P}_{i}(t\leq T_{ext},T<t,T\neq T_{\rm{hit}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{i}(t\leq T)+\mathbb{P}_{i}(T=T_{\rm{hit}})+\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{bad}}<T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t).

As in the previous proof,
M(0)
is not deterministic under
\mathbb{P}_{i}
but satisfies

s_{i}\leq\mathbb{E}_{i}[M(0)]\leq\frac{7}{6}s_{i}
,
so the optional stopping theorem applied to
M(\cdot\wedge T)
implies

\mathbb{P}_{i}(T=T_{\rm{hit}})\leq\frac{7s_{i}}{6s(a)}.

Moreover, with the optional stopping theorem applied to
M(\cdot\wedge T)+c_{\eqref{eq:negative_infinitesimal_increase}}(\cdot\wedge T)
, we get

\mathbb{P}_{i}(t<T)\leq\frac{1}{t}\mathbb{E}_{i}[T]\leq\frac{1}{c_{\eqref{eq:negative_infinitesimal_increase}}t}\mathbb{E}_{i}[M_{0}]\leq\frac{7s_{i}}{6c_{\eqref{eq:negative_infinitesimal_increase}}t}.

Using (
78
), we now obtain

(106)

I_{N}(t)\leq a+\tfrac{1}{N}+\tfrac{7}{6}\Big(\tfrac{1}{s(a)}+\tfrac{1}{c_{\eqref{eq:negative_infinitesimal_increase}}t}\Big)\int_{a}^{1}s(y)dy+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=\lceil aN\rceil+1}^{N}\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{bad}}<T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t).

This expression resembles (
53
), except for the last term. In Subsection
5.3
, we will prove that we can bound this term at time
t=t_{0}:=7s(a)/c_{\eqref{eq:negative_infinitesimal_increase}}
as

(107)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=\lceil aN\rceil+1}^{N}\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{bad}}<T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t_{0})\leq a+\frac{\omega^{\prime}}{N},

for some choice of
\omega^{\prime}=\omega^{\prime}(\lambda)
, and small
\lambda
.
Taking this for granted, we continue as follows:

•

For
t\leq t_{0}
, we bound
\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{bad}}<T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t)
by
\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{bad}}<T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t_{0})
to obtain

I_{N}(t)\leq 2a+\frac{7}{6s(a)}\int_{a}^{1}s(y)dy+\frac{\omega}{t}+\frac{1+\omega^{\prime}}{N},

with
\omega=\omega(\lambda)=\tfrac{7}{6c_{\eqref{eq:negative_infinitesimal_increase}}}\int_{a}^{1}s(y)\mathrm{d}y
.

•

For
t\geq t_{0}
, we use that
I_{N}
is nonincreasing to bound
I_{N}(t)
by
I_{N}(t_{0})
, which, using again (
107
) and the equality
\frac{7}{6}(\frac{1}{s(a)}+\frac{1}{c_{\eqref{eq:negative_infinitesimal_increase}}t_{0}})=\frac{4}{3s(a)}
, yields

I_{N}(t)\leq 2a+\frac{4}{3s(a)}\int_{a}^{1}s(y)dy+\frac{1+\omega^{\prime}(\lambda)}{N}.

Thus (
53
) is satisfied for all
t\geq 0
, showing Theorem
4
. It only remains to prove (
107
).

5.3.
Bound on the probability of stopping the exploration early.

We will provide a bound of the form

(108)

\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{bad}}<T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t_{0})\leq o(a(\lambda))+\frac{\omega^{\prime}(\lambda)}{N},

valid for all small
\lambda
and large
N
, and uniformly over
i\in\{\lceil aN\rceil+1,\ldots,N\}
, from which (
107
) naturally follows. Recall the definition of
t_{0}=t_{0}(\lambda)=7s(a)/c_{\eqref{eq:negative_infinitesimal_increase}}
and
T_{\rm{bad}}=T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{deg}}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{conn}}
, and introduce further the following stopping times:

•

T_{\ell}
, for
\ell\geq 1
, the time of the
\ell
-th infection, where an infection means a vertex transitioning from the state healthy to infected (and possibly saturated). Note that two infections can occur simultaneously (by the saturation rules
(8)(i)
or
(8)(ii)
), in which case we count the two infections, so we might have
T_{\ell}=T_{\ell+1}
for example. A single vertex can also be infected multiple times. In any case, we can roughly bound the number of visited vertices at times
t<T_{\ell}
by
\ell
.

•

\tilde{T}_{\rm{deg}}
, the smallest time when a visited strong vertex is connected to more than
2c_{2}a^{-\gamma}
weak vertices in
G_{t}
. Note that when incorporated to the network, a strong vertex
j
was possibly connected to a weak vertex
i
that has infected it (so that
(i,j)
has been incorporated to
G_{t}
). However, its connections to the other weak vertices follow the same dynamics as in the original stationary dynamic network (by rules (6) and (7)). It follows that at any given time
t
, we can use (
1
) to bound the average number of present weak edges
(j,k)
by
c_{2}a^{-\gamma}
. Being connected to more than
2c_{2}a^{-\gamma}
is thus an unlikely event, which will help us to control the more involved quantities
J_{j}(t)
and
K_{j}(t)
appearing in (
102
) and (
103
).

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{bad}}<T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\ell}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{deg}}\wedge\tilde{T}_{\rm{deg}}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{conn}}<T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t_{0})

\displaystyle\leq P^{\rm{infected}}+P^{\rm{tree}}+P^{\rm{deg}}\ +P^{\rm{conn}},

where the second line is obtained by looking at which of the stopping times is smallest, and the terms are defined as

\displaystyle P^{\rm{infected}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\ell}\leq T_{\rm{bad}}\wedge T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t_{0}),

\displaystyle P^{\rm{tree}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}<T_{\ell}\wedge T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t_{0}),

\displaystyle P^{\rm{deg}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{deg}}\wedge\tilde{T}_{\rm{deg}}<T_{\ell}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}\wedge T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t_{0}),

\displaystyle P^{\rm{conn}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{P}_{i}(T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{conn}}<T_{\ell}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{deg}}\wedge\tilde{T}_{\rm{deg}}\wedge T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t_{0}).

We now prove that

\displaystyle P^{\rm{tree}}\leq\tfrac{\omega^{\prime}(\lambda)}{N},

\displaystyle P^{\rm{infected}}+P^{\rm{deg}}+P^{\rm{conn}}=o(a(\lambda)),

when
\ell
is chosen as

(109)

\ell=\ell(\lambda)=\lambda^{-C_{\eqref{ineq:k_limit}}}

with
C_{\eqref{ineq:k_limit}}>0
defined below.

Bounding the total number of infections

We prove

(110)

P^{\rm{infected}}\leq\frac{2+4s(a)^{2}}{\ell}.

From now on, we fix a sufficiently large value of
C_{\eqref{ineq:k_limit}}
to ensure this is
o(a)
. This is possible as
a^{-1}
and
s(a(\lambda))
are growing polynomially in
\lambda^{-1}
.

Proof.

The idea is to associate each infection with a positive increase of value at least
1/2
for
the supermartingale
M(t\wedge T)
, and then use standard martingale theory to obtain the desired upper bound. Indeed, the infection of a weak vertex
i
implies a score increase of at least
s_{i}\geq 1
, while the infection of a strong vertex
i
implies an increase of its associated score
m_{i}
of at least
s_{i}/2\geq 1/2
. This simple idea however has to take into consideration several subtleties.

•

The infection of a strong pending vertex possibly implies a
decay of the total score
. This is due to the fact that while the vertex becomes infected (and saturated), the edge simultaneously also becomes saturated, which implies a decay of its associated score. For this reason, in this proof, we will consider a strong pending vertex as already infected when counting the number of “infections”.

•

By the previous consideration, when a strong vertex
i
reveals a strong pending leaf
j
, we count this as an infection, which we also want to associate to an increase of score. However, it might be the case that
i
was itself a strong pending leaf, that was just infected by another strong slow edge
(k,i)
. In that case, the following events occur simultaneously:

–

i
gets infected and saturated, with associated score
s_{i}+t_{i}
.

–

k
also gets saturated (if it were not before), but for simplicity we neglect this effect from this discussion.

–

The edge
(k,i)
becomes saturated, implying a score decrease of
s_{i}+t_{i}+u_{i}
. These first events together thus imply possibly a score decrease of
u_{i}
.

–

The vertex
i
has revealed the pending strong slow vertex
j
(and possibly other strong slow and weak vertices as well). The increase in score associated to this newly revealed strong slow vertex is
s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}
.

The problem is that the increase
s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j}
might be compensated by the decrease
u_{i}
, so it is not clear whether we can associate a score increase to the revelation of
j
.

In order to overcome this difficulty, we define a discrete-time supermartingale
(M^{\rm disc}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, that evolves as the jump process associated to the supermartingale
M_{t}
, except for the particular case in which a strong slow vertex
i
is infected by another strong slow vertex
k
. In that case, the discrete-time process
M^{\rm disc}
decomposes the jump
\Delta M_{t}
into several jumps as follows:

•

As a first jump of
M^{\rm disc}
, it increases due to the change in score given by the saturation of
i
,
k
, and
(k,i)
, the revelation of new weak neighbours of
i
, and an additional term
\sum_{j\text{ strong slow}}p_{ij}(s_{j}+t_{j}+u)
representing the average increase of
M_{t}
resulting from the revelation of new strong slow neighbours. Equivalently, this term can be seen as the result of assigning a temporary score
p_{i,j}(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j})
to each strong slow edge
(i,j)
, before attempting to reveal it as in Rule
(3)
.

•

For the remaining steps associated to this event, we successively attempt to reveal each strong slow edge
(i,j)

(starting from the smallest
j
or in other arbitrary order), and replace the score
p_{i,j}(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j})
by either
(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j})
or
0
, according to whether the edge was revealed or not.

Once the vertex
i
has gone through all the strong slow vertices, the two processes coincide again (via a time-change that we do not write down explicitly). This provides the construction of
(M^{\rm disc}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
. Note that:

•

When we successfully reveal a strong slow neighbour
j
, this implies a positive jump
\Delta M^{\rm disc}_{n}=(1-p_{i,j})(s_{j}+t_{j}+u_{j})\geq 1/2
, using that
p_{i,j}\leq p(a,a)/N\leq 1/2
for large
N
. Actually it is now true that any “infection” that we count in this proof is associated to an increase
\Delta M^{\rm disc}_{n}\geq 1/2
. We might also have two simultaneous infections, or reveal several strong pending vertices when a strong slow vertex updates, but in each case the total number of simultaneous “infections” is bounded by
2\Delta M^{\rm disc}_{n}
.

•

The first jump of
M^{\rm disc}
is on average equal to
\Delta M_{t}
, while the rest of the jumps have mean
0
. Doing this, we maintain the supermartingale property of the process, so
M^{\rm disc}
is indeed a supermartingale.

•

The process
M^{\rm disc}
takes nonnegative values, and it can reach a value larger than
s(a)
only at a moment corresponding to the time
T_{\rm{hit}}
when
M_{t}
also reaches such a value.

Writing
N_{\rm{hit}}:=\inf\{n\geq 0,M^{\rm disc}_{n}>s(a)\}
and
Z_{n}:=(s(a)-M^{\rm disc}_{n\wedge N_{\rm{hit}}})_{+}
, we have that
Z_{n}
is a submartingale with values in
[0,s(a)]
. The total number of infections occurring before time
T
can now be bounded (using also
T\leq T_{\rm{hit}}
), by

\sum_{n<N_{\rm{hit}}}2\Delta M^{\rm disc}_{n}{\bf{1}}_{\Delta M^{\rm disc}_{n}\geq 1/2}\leq 4\sum_{n<N_{\rm{hit}}}\left(\Delta M^{\rm disc}_{n}\right)^{2}.

Noting that we have
\Delta Z_{n}=-\Delta M^{\rm disc}_{n}
for
n<N_{\rm{hit}}
, we can further bound the expected number of infections occurring before time
T
by

4\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{n<N_{\rm{hit}}}\left(\Delta Z_{n}\right)^{2}\Big]\leq 4\mathbb{E}[Z_{\infty}^{2}-Z_{0}^{2}]\leq 4s(a)^{2}.

We also have at most two additional infections occurring at time
T
.
Then (
110
) follows by the Markov inequality. ∎

Bounding the probability of breaking the tree structure

We now prove

P^{\rm{tree}}\leq 8\ell^{2}(2\kappa_{0}t_{0}+\lambda t_{0}+\ell+1)^{2}\frac{p(a,a)^{2}}{N}=:\frac{\omega^{\prime}(\lambda)}{N}.

Proof.

Introduce, in this proof only, the notation
\tilde{T}:=T_{k}\wedge T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t_{0}
, and the quantities

\displaystyle E^{\rm{tree}}_{\rm{rep}}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\parbox{284.52756pt}{$(i,j)\in\{1,2,,\ldots,N\}^{2}$, such that $(i,j)$ becomes present at least twice before time $\tilde{T}$}\bigg\}

\displaystyle V^{\rm{tree}}_{\rm{cycle}}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\parbox{284.52756pt}{$j\in\{1,2,,\ldots,N\}$, there are $i,i^{\prime}\in\{1,2,\ldots N\}$ such that both $(i,j)$ and $(i^{\prime},j)$ become present
before time $\tilde{T}$}\bigg\}

It follows that
T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}<\tilde{T}
if and only if
|E^{\rm{tree}}_{\rm{rep}}|+|V^{\rm{tree}}_{\rm{cycle}}|\geq 1
so from the Markov inequality, we can bound

P^{\rm{tree}}\leq\mathbb{E}[|E^{\rm{tree}}_{\rm{rep}}|]+\mathbb{E}[|V^{\rm{tree}}_{\rm{cycle}}|].

The quantities in both expectations involve counting edges that become present, so we stress that in order for an edge
(i,j)
to become present at time
t<\tilde{T}
:

•

The edge
i
must be visited at time
t
.

•

It must be the case that at time
t
either:

–

(i,j)
is a strong quick edge and
i
is infecting
j
(which was previously not in the network), or

–

i
or
j
updates, or

–

i
is a strong slow neighbour which becomes saturated due to Rule
(8.iii)
.

Denote by
\tau_{i}
the time at which vertex
i
becomes visited, and
N_{\rm{visited}}
the number of visited vertices by time
\tilde{T}
. We now bound
|E^{\rm{tree}}_{\rm{rep}}|
and
|V^{\rm{tree}}_{\rm{cycle}}|
separately:

•

Repeated edges
: Start from the observation

\mathbb{E}[|E^{\rm{tree}}_{\rm{rep}}|]=\sum_{\text{ edges }(i,j)}\mathbb{P}(\tau_{i}<\tilde{T}\text{ and $(i,j)$ becomes present at least twice before time $\tilde{T}$})

and observe that, conditionally on
\tau_{i}
:

–

The number of updates of
(i,j)
in
(\tau_{i},\tilde{T}]
that result in the edge being present is bounded by an independent Poisson random variable with rate
(\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{j})(\tilde{T}-\tau_{i})p_{i,j}\leq 2\kappa_{0}t_{0}p_{i,j}
.

–

Since
\tilde{T}\leq T_{\ell}
, there are at most
\ell
infections within
[\tau_{i},\tilde{T}]
which could reveal
(i,j)
due to Rule
(8.iii)
, and at each such infection an independent coin with probability
p_{i,j}
is tossed to decide whether the edge becomes present.

–

The number of possible events in which
(i,j)
is infecting
j
through a clogged edge is bounded by a Poisson random variable with rate
\lambda t_{0}p_{i,j}
.

We thus deduce that, conditionally on
\tau_{i}
, the probability of
(i,j)
becoming present at least twice is bounded by

2(2\kappa_{0}t_{0}+\lambda t_{0}+\ell+1)^{2}p_{i,j}^{2}\leq 2(2\kappa_{0}t_{0}+\lambda t_{0}+\ell+1)^{2}\frac{p(a,a)^{2}}{N^{2}},

and as there are at most
\ell
infections before time
\tilde{T}
, it must be that
N_{\rm{visited}}\leq\ell
, so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|E^{\rm{tree}}_{\rm{rep}}|]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\text{ edges }(i,j)}\mathbb{P}(\tau_{i}<\tilde{T})2(2\kappa_{0}t_{0}+\lambda t_{0}+\ell+1)^{2}\frac{p(a,a)^{2}}{N^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{P}(\tau_{i}<\tilde{T})2(2\kappa_{0}t_{0}+\lambda t_{0}+\ell+1)^{2}\frac{p(a,a)^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle\leq 2\ell(2\kappa_{0}t_{0}+\lambda t_{0}+\ell+1)^{2}\frac{p(a,a)^{2}}{N}

•

Cycles
: This case is similar to the previous one, where we can write

\mathbb{E}[|V^{\rm{tree}}_{\rm{cycle}}|]=\sum_{i,j,l}\mathbb{P}(\tau_{i},\tau_{j}<\tilde{T},\text{ and both $(i,l)$ and $(j,l)$ become present before time $\tilde{T}$}).

Following the same line of thought as in the previous case, we deduce that, conditionally on
\tau_{i}
and
\tau_{j}
, the probability of both
(i,l)
and
(j,l)
becoming present is bounded by

4(2\kappa_{0}t_{0}+\lambda t_{0}+\ell+1)^{2}p_{i,l}p_{j,l}\leq 4(2\kappa_{0}t_{0}+\lambda t_{0}+\ell+1)^{2}\frac{p(a,a)^{2}}{N^{2}},

and hence we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|V^{\rm{tree}}_{\rm{cycle}}|]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j,l}\mathbb{P}(\tau_{i},\tau_{j}<\tilde{T})4(2\kappa_{0}t_{0}+\lambda t_{0}+\ell+1)^{2}\frac{p(a,a)^{2}}{N^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\mathbb{P}(\tau_{i},\tau_{j}<\tilde{T})4(2\kappa_{0}t_{0}+\lambda t_{0}+\ell+1)^{2}\frac{p(a,a)^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle\leq 4\ell^{2}(2\kappa_{0}t_{0}+\lambda t_{0}+\ell+1)^{2}\frac{p(a,a)^{2}}{N}.

The conclusion follows by adding both bounds.
∎

Bounding the degrees

We prove below the upper bounds

(111)

\displaystyle P^{\rm{deg}}

\displaystyle\leq\ell t_{0}e^{-c_{\eqref{Pdegree}}\lambda^{-2}},

with
c_{\eqref{Pdegree}}>0
a constant independent of
\lambda
or
N
. As
\ell
and
t_{0}
are polynomial in
\lambda
, this term is exponentially small in
\lambda
, and in particular negligible compared to
a
as
\lambda\to 0
.

Proof.

At times
t<T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}
, each vertex
j
in
G_{t}
(except
i_{0}
) is the head of one edge
(i,j)\in G_{t}
, with possibly
i
weak. Once
j
is infected, every other weak edge
(j,k)
is revealed and evolves as in the true stationary dynamic network. Therefore, conditionally given
j
is in
G_{t}
, the number
N_{j}^{\rm w}(t)
of present weak edges
(j,k)
in
G_{t}
, follows a Poisson binomial distribution, with expectation bounded by
1/10\lambda^{2}
if
j
is itself a weak vertex. Therefore, for given
t
, the probability of having
N_{j}^{\rm w}(t)\geq 1/9\lambda^{2}
is uniformly bounded by
e^{-c\lambda^{-2}}
for some constant
c>0
. Moreover, the updating rates of the vertices are upper bounded, therefore one can define a time
step
\tau
such that for any given
s
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\min\{N_{j}^{\rm w}(u)\colon s\leq u\leq s+\tau\}\geq 1/9\lambda^{2}\ \Big|\ N_{j}^{\rm w}(s)\geq 1/8\lambda^{2}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}.

It follows that we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\max\{N_{j}^{\rm w}(s)\colon s\leq t\}\geq 1/8\lambda^{2}\ \Big|\ j\in G_{t}\right)\leq\frac{2t}{\tau}e^{-c\lambda^{-2}}.

Now, we inspect the successively infected weak vertices one by one up until time
T_{\ell}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}\wedge T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t_{0}
. We find at most
\ell
such vertices, for which we can use the previous bound with
t=t_{0}
. Thus the probability of finding a weak vertex with at least
1+\lfloor 1/8\lambda^{2}\rfloor
weak neighbours is bounded by
\frac{2\ell t_{0}}{\tau}e^{-c\lambda^{-2}}
.
Similarly, for a strong vertex
j>aN
and conditionally given
j\in G_{t}
, the number of present weak edges
(j,k)
in
G_{t}
follows a Poisson binomial distribution with expectation bounded by
c_{2}a^{-\gamma}
, and we deduce that the probability of finding a strong vertex connected to more than
2c_{2}a^{-\gamma}
weak vertices is also bounded by
\frac{2\ell t_{0}}{\tau}e^{-c\lambda^{-2}}
(with a possibly different value of
c>0
). We deduce (
111
) by decreasing the value of
c>0
if necessary. ∎

Bounding the connectivities of visited strong vertices

It finally remains to bound the probability
P^{conn}
to visit a strong vertex
i
that fails to satisfy (
102
) or (
103
) by having a too large value for
J_{i}(t)
or
K_{i}(t)
. This last bound is more technical than the previous ones, and before tackling it, we first provide an upper bound for quantities that can be thought of at upper bounds for
J_{i}(t)
and
K_{i}(t)
in a fixed idealized environment.

A large deviation upper bound in an idealized environment.

Fix a strong vertex
i
, and introduce a family
(C_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}})_{n\geq 1,j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\text{ weak}}
of independent random variables, indexed by the sequences of distinct weak vertices, such that
C_{j_{1}}
is Bernoulli distributed with parameter
p_{i,j_{1}}
and, for
n\geq 2
,
C_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}
is Bernoulli distributed with parameter
p_{j_{n-1},j_{n}}
. Define

\bar{J}_{i}=\sum_{j\text{ weak}}\lambda C_{j}\bar{M}_{j},\qquad\bar{K}_{i}=\sum_{j\text{ weak}}\frac{C_{j}}{s_{j}}\bar{M}_{j},

where
\bar{M}_{j}
is defined by

\bar{M}_{j}:=2s_{j}+2\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\text{ weak}}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{n}2\lambda C_{j,j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}\right)s_{j_{n}}.

Proposition 8
.

For
\mathfrak{c}
sufficiently large, there exists a finite constant
C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}
such that the following results hold:

(1)

for any
j
weak and any
\theta\geq 0
satisfying
\theta\leq 1/(2\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}}))
and
\theta\leq s_{j}^{-1}
, we have:

\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{M}_{j}}]\leq 1+C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta s_{j}.

(2)

for any
j
weak and any
\theta\geq 0
satisfying
\theta\leq 1/(2\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}}))
, we have

\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{M}_{j}}]\leq e^{C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta s_{j}}.

(3)

for any
i
strong and any
\theta\geq 0
satisfying
\theta\leq 1/(\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}}))
, we have

\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{J}_{i}}]\leq e^{C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta\sigma_{i}}\leq e^{\frac{C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta}{\mathfrak{c}}\,\frac{s_{i}}{T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}},

where
\sigma_{i}=\lambda\sum_{j\text{weak}}p_{ij}s_{j}
.

(4)

for any
i
strong and any
\theta\geq 0
satisfying
\theta\leq 1
and
\theta\leq 1/(2\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}}))
, we have

\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{K}_{i}}]\leq e^{\frac{C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}}{2\kappa_{0}}\theta\tilde{\sigma}_{i}}\leq e^{C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta\pi_{i}},

where
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}=\sum_{j\text{weak}}p_{ij}
.

Proof of Proposition
8
.

We focus on the first point of the proposition. For
j
weak and
n\geq 0
, we introduce

\bar{M}_{j}^{(n)}:=2s_{j}+2\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\text{ weak}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}2\lambda C_{j,j_{1},\ldots,j_{l}}\right)s_{j_{k}},

so that
\bar{M}_{j}^{(0)}=2s_{j}
and
\bar{M}_{j}=\lim\bar{M}_{j}^{(n)}
. We prove recursively on
n
the existence of a constant
C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}>0
independent of
n
such that, for any
j
weak and any
\theta\geq 0
satisfying
\theta\leq 1/(2\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}}))
and
\theta\leq s_{j}^{-1}
, we have

\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{M}_{j}^{(n)}}]\leq 1+C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta s_{j}.

For
n=0
, using the convexity of the exponential function and
\theta s_{j}\leq 1
, we have

e^{\theta\bar{M}_{j}^{(0)}}=e^{2\theta s_{j}}\leq 1+(e^{2}-1)\theta s_{j},

whence the result holds if
C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\geq e^{2}-1
. For the induction step, write

\bar{M}_{j}^{(n)}=2s_{j}+\sum_{j_{1}\text{ weak}}2\lambda C_{j,j_{1}}\bar{M}_{j,j_{1}}^{(n)},

where the random variables

\bar{M}_{j,j_{1}}^{(n)}:=2s_{j_{1}}+2\sum_{k=2}^{n}\sum_{j_{2},\ldots,j_{n}}\left(\prod_{l=2}^{n}2\lambda C_{j,j_{1},\ldots,j_{l}}\right)s_{j_{k}}

are independent, independent from the variables
C_{j,j_{1}}
, and have the same laws as
\bar{M}_{j_{1}}^{(n-1)}
. Further,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{M}_{j}^{(n)}}]

\displaystyle=e^{2\theta s_{j}}\prod_{j_{1}\text{ weak}}\mathbb{E}[e^{2\lambda\theta C_{j,j_{1}}M_{j,j_{1}}^{(n)}}]=e^{2\theta s_{j}}\prod_{j_{1}\text{ weak}}\left(1+p_{jj_{1}}\left(\mathbb{E}[e^{2\lambda\theta\bar{M}_{j_{1}}^{(n-1)}}]-1\right)\right).

From the hypothesis
\theta\leq 1/(2\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}}))
, it follows
2\lambda\theta\leq 1/s(a_{\rm{str}})\leq 1/s_{j_{1}}
for all
j_{1}
weak, as well as
2\lambda\theta\leq\theta\leq 1/(2\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}}))
, since
\lambda\leq 1/2
. We can thus apply the induction hypothesis to
obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{M}_{j}^{(n)}}]

\displaystyle\leq e^{2\theta s_{j}}\prod_{j_{1}\text{ weak}}\left(1+2\lambda\theta C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}p_{jj_{1}}s_{j_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(2\theta s_{j}+2C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta\sum_{j_{1}\text{ weak}}\lambda p_{jj_{1}}s_{j_{1}}\right) \leq\exp\left(2\theta s_{j}\big(1+\frac{C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}}{\mathfrak{c}}\big)\right),

where we used (
48
).
Using now the convexity of the exponential function and
2\theta s_{j}(1+C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}/\mathfrak{c})\leq 2(1+C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}/\mathfrak{c})
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{M}_{j}^{(n)}}]\leq 1+\left(e^{2(1+C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}/\mathfrak{c})}-1\right)\ \theta s_{j}.

To perform the recurrence step, we need
e^{2(1+C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}/\mathfrak{c})}-1\leq C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}
, which we can ensure by choosing
C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}>e^{2}-1
and
\mathfrak{c}
sufficiently large. This concludes the proof of the first part of the proposition.

For the second part, we proceed exactly as above to bound
\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{M}_{j}^{(n)}}]
by the expression
\exp\left(2\theta(1+C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}/\mathfrak{c})s_{j}\right)
. This is the required upper bound, by increasing the value of
C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}
if necessary. Note also that we cannot further bound this without the hypothesis
\theta s_{j}\leq 1
.

For the third and fourth parts, the computations are similar. For
\theta\leq 1/\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}})
, which ensures
\lambda\theta\leq\min(1/2\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}}),1/s_{j})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{J}_{i}}]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j\text{ weak}}\left(1+p_{ij}\left(\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda\theta\bar{M}_{j}}]-1\right)\right)\leq e^{C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta\sigma_{i}}.

For
\theta\leq\min(1,1/(2\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}})))
, which ensures
\theta/s_{j}\leq\min(1/2\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}}),1/s_{j})
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{K}_{i}}]\leq e^{C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta\sum_{j \text{weak}}p_{ij}} \leq e^{2C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta\kappa_{0}\pi_{i}}.

∎

Corollary 5
.

Under the same conditions, choosing a value
C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}>2
for which Proposition
8
(2) holds, we also have, for any
j
weak,

(112)

\mathbb{P}(\bar{M}_{j}\geq 2C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}s(a_{\rm{str}}))\leq e^{-\lambda^{-1}}.

Proof.

For
\theta=1/(2\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}}))
, Markov inequality and Proposition
8
(2)
yield

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bar{M}_{j}\geq 2C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}s(a_{\rm{str}})\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta s(a_{\rm{str}})}\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{M}_{j}}]\leq e^{-2C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta s(a_{\rm{str}})}e^{C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta s_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}\theta s(a_{\rm{str}})}=e^{-C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}/2\lambda},

using also that the score
s_{j}
associated to the weak vertex
j
is smaller than
s(a_{\rm{str}})
.
∎

Corollary 6
.

Assume that the additional technical condition
(H2)
holds, yielding some
\alpha\in(0,1]
, and
c_{\eqref{cond:H2}}>0
, not depending on
\lambda
, such that

\displaystyle s(a_{\rm{str}})

\displaystyle\leq c_{\eqref{cond:H2}}\lambda^{-3+\alpha},

\displaystyle s(a_{\rm{str}})

\displaystyle\leq c_{\eqref{cond:H2}}\lambda^{-1+\alpha}\inf\big\{\tfrac{s(x)}{T^{\rm{loc}}(x)}\colon x<a_{\rm{str}}\big\}.

For any
c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}>0
and large
r>0
, taking
\mathfrak{c}
sufficiently large, there exists a constant
c_{\ref{LDBoundsFixedTime}}
such that for small
\lambda
, for any strong vertex
i
and weak vertex
j
, we have

(113)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bar{M}_{j}\geq\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}s_{j})

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c_{\ref{LDBoundsFixedTime}}\lambda^{-\alpha}},

(114)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bar{J}_{i}\geq c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}\tfrac{s_{i}}{T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}})

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c_{\ref{LDBoundsFixedTime}}\lambda^{-\alpha}},

(115)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bar{K}_{i}\geq\tfrac{r}{10}\pi_{i})

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c_{\ref{LDBoundsFixedTime}}\lambda^{-\alpha}}.

Proof.

To obtain
(
113
)
, proceed as in the proof of (
112
) to get

\mathbb{P}\left(\bar{M}_{j}\geq\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}s_{j}\right)\leq e^{-\frac{\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}-C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}})}},

and conclude by observing that
\lambda^{2}\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}
and
\lambda^{\alpha-3}/s(a_{\rm{str}})
are both lower bounded by positive constants.
To obtain (
114
), take
\theta=1/(\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}}))
to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bar{J}_{i}\geq c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}\tfrac{s_{i}}{T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}\theta\frac{s_{i}}{T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}}\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta\bar{J}_{i}}]\leq e^{(-c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}+C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}/\mathfrak{c})\frac{s_{i}}{\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}})T^{\rm{loc}}_{i}}}\leq e^{\frac{-c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}+c/\mathfrak{c}}{c_{\eqref{cond:H2}}}\lambda^{-\alpha}},

using
(H2)
, provided the constant
-c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}+C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}/\mathfrak{c}
is negative, which we can ensure by choosing
\mathfrak{c}
large.
Finally, to obtain (
115
), take
\theta=\lambda^{2-\alpha}/2\leq\min(1,1/2\lambda s(a_{\rm{str}}))
and
r/10
larger than
C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}
given by Proposition
8
(4)
. Then,

\mathbb{P}\left(\bar{K}_{i}\geq\frac{r}{10}\pi_{x}\right)\leq e^{-(\frac{r}{10}-C_{\ref{exponential_moments}})\theta\pi_{i}}\leq e^{-c\lambda^{2-\alpha}\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}}

for some small constant
c
. We conclude again by observing that
\lambda^{2}\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}
is lower bounded by a positive constant.
∎

Exploring and bounding the connectivities of the visited strong vertices

We now turn to the true environment encountered by the infection. From now on, we assume that condition
(H2)
holds, so in particular we can apply Corollary
6
. For a strong vertex
i\in G_{t}
, recall the definition of
J_{i}(t)
and decompose it as
J_{i}^{\rm{out}}(t)+J_{i}^{\rm{in}}(t)
with

J_{i}^{\rm{out}}(t):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\text{ weak}\\
(i,j)\text{ present}\end{subarray}}\lambda M_{j}(t),\qquad J_{i}^{\rm{in}}(t):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\text{ weak}\\
(j,i)\text{ present}\end{subarray}}\lambda M_{j}(t).

Recalling that there is (at most) one edge
(j,i)
in
G_{t}
pointing to
i
, the sum in the definition of
J_{i}^{\rm{in}}
contains only one or zero term. Similarly, we write
K_{i}(t)=K_{i}^{\rm{out}}(t)+K_{i}^{\rm{in}}(t)
with

K_{i}^{\rm{out}}(t):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\text{ weak}\\
(i,j)\text{ present}\end{subarray}}\frac{M_{j}(t)}{s_{j}},\quad K_{i}^{\rm{in}}(t):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\text{ weak}\\
(j,i)\text{ present}\end{subarray}}\frac{M_{j}(t)}{s_{j}}.

For a weak vertex
i
, we also decompose
M_{i}(t)=M_{i}^{\rm{out}}(t)+M_{i}^{\rm{in}}(t)
with

\displaystyle M_{i}^{\rm{out}}(t)

\displaystyle:=\tilde{s}_{i}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\text{ weak}\\
(i,j)\text{ present}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathfrak{t}\\
d(i,k)=d(j,k)+1\end{subarray}}(2\lambda)^{d(i,k)}\tilde{s}_{k},

\displaystyle M_{i}^{\rm{in}}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\text{ weak}\\
(j,i)\text{ present}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathfrak{t}\\
d(i,k)=d(j,k)+1\end{subarray}}(2\lambda)^{d(i,k)}\tilde{s}_{k}.

By the following lemma, the idealized environment provides an upper bound for the quantities
M_{i}^{\rm{out}}
,
J_{i}^{\rm{out}}
and
K_{i}^{\rm{out}}
.

Lemma 4
.

•

If
j
is a weak vertex and
t\geq 0
, then, conditionally on
j
being visited, the random variable
M_{j}^{\rm{out}}(t){\mathsf{1}}_{t<T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}}
is stochastically bounded by
\bar{M}_{j}
.

•

If
i
is a strong vertex and
t\geq 0
, then, conditionally on
i
being visited, the random variables
J_{i}^{\rm{out}}(t){\mathsf{1}}_{t<T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}}
and
K_{i}^{\rm{out}}(t){\mathsf{1}}_{t<T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}}
are stochastically bounded by
\bar{J}_{i}
and
\bar{K}_{i}
respectively.

,

Proof.

For a visited weak vertex, we can write

M_{j}^{\rm{out}}(t)
on the event
\{t<T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}\}
as

\tilde{s}_{j}+\sum_{n\geq 1}(2\lambda)^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\text{ distinct}\\
\text{weak vertices}\end{subarray}}{\mathsf{1}}_{\{\text{the edges }(j,j_{1}),\ldots,(j_{n-1},j_{n})\text{ are in }G_{t}\}}\tilde{s}_{j_{n}}.

Recalling the bound
\tilde{s}_{k}\leq 2s_{k}
, it remains to couple the graph and the random variables
C
in such a way that for any directed path
(j,j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})
in

G_{t}
, we have
C_{j,\ldots,j_{n}}=1
. We proceed by induction on
n
. If
j_{n-1}
is first visited at time
s\leq t
, then for every weak vertex
j_{n}
which is not in
G_{s-}
, the edge
(j_{n-1},j_{n})
is present in
G_{t}
with the stationary probability
p_{j_{n-1},j_{n}}
. We then couple the connection at time
t
with
C_{j,\ldots,j_{n}}
. Note that we consider times smaller than
T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}
so the connection of an edge
(j_{n-1},j_{n})
is coupled to at most one variable
C_{j,\ldots,j_{n}}
, thus maintaining the independence between the
C
variables. The second part of the lemma with strong vertices is similar.
∎

In the rest of this proof specifically, we write
\tilde{T}
for
T_{\ell}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}\wedge T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{deg}}\wedge\tilde{T}_{\rm{deg}}\wedge T_{\rm{hit}}\wedge t_{0}
.

Corollary 7
.

Considering the same settings as in Corollary
6
, say a weak vertex
j
is
bad
at time
t
if it is visited and satisfies

M_{j}^{\rm{out}}(t)\geq 2C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}s(a_{\rm{str}})\wedge\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}s_{j}.

Similarly, say a strong vertex
i
is bad at time
t
if it is visited and satisfies any of the following inequalities:

\displaystyle J_{i}^{\rm{out}}(t)

\displaystyle\geq c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}\frac{s_{i}}{T_{i}^{\rm{loc}}},

\displaystyle K_{i}^{\rm{out}}(t)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{r}{10}\pi_{i}.

Finally, say a vertex is bad if it is bad at some time
t<\tilde{T}
.
Then the probability of finding any bad vertex is bounded by
C_{\ref{LD_outcontributions}}\ell^{3}a^{-2\gamma}t_{0}e^{-c_{\ref{LDBoundsFixedTime}}\lambda^{-\alpha}}
, for some finite constant
C_{\ref{LD_outcontributions}}
.

We stress that the definition of bad vertices in this corollary actually depends on the parameters
c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}
and
r
(that we have still not fixed), and also requires a constraint of large
\mathfrak{c}
to apply Corollary
6
. We discuss later how all these parameters are chosen.

Proof.

Note that at times
t<\tilde{T}
, the number of visited vertices is bounded by
\ell
, while the number of nonvisited weak vertices in
G_{t}
is bounded by
(2c_{2}a^{-\gamma}\vee 1/8\lambda^{2})\ell
. Further, let
\ell^{\prime}=\ell+(2c_{2}a^{-\gamma}\vee 1/8\lambda^{2})\ell
, and choose a vertex
i
conditioned to be in
G_{\tilde{T}}
. Suppose that the vertex turns bad at some time
t<\tilde{T}
. Then, with probability at least
1/e
, the vertex
i
and the weak vertices in
G_{t}
do not update until time
t+\tau
, with
\tau=1/\kappa_{0}\ell^{\prime 2}
. Therefore, with probability at least
e^{-1}
, we have
t+\tau<T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}
and the value of
M_{i}
(if
i
is weak) or of
J_{i}
and
K_{i}
(if
i
is strong) do not decrease on this time interval. In particular, there exists a time
s\in\tau\mathbb{N}\cap [0,\tilde{T}+\tau)
which is less than
T_{\rm{bad}}^{\rm{tree}}
and at which we observe a large value for
M_{i}(s)
(or
J_{i}(s)
or
K_{i}(s)
). By Lemma
4
and Corollaries
5
and
6
, the probability that such a time
s
exists, is bounded by
(t_{0}/\tau+1)e^{-c_{\ref{LDBoundsFixedTime}}\lambda^{-\alpha}}.
We deduce successively a bound of

e(t_{0}/\tau+1)e^{-c_{\ref{LDBoundsFixedTime}}\lambda^{-\alpha}}
for the probability that a visited vertex turns bad, and a bound of

\ell e(t_{0}/\tau+1)e^{-c_{\ref{LDBoundsFixedTime}}\lambda^{-\alpha}}
for the probability of finding any bad vertex.
∎

We now suppose that we do not encounter any bad vertex. We will then prove recursively
that all the visited weak vertices
j
satisfy

(116)

M_{j}(t)<3C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}s(a_{\rm{str}})\wedge 2\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}s_{j},

at all times
t<\tilde{T}
.
For the first visited weak vertex, we have
M_{i}(t)=M_{i}^{\rm{out}}(t)
, so the bound follows from the fact that this vertex is good. Now, for a newly visited weak vertex
i
, we can bound

M_{i}^{\rm{out}}
by
2C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}s(a_{\rm{str}})\wedge\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}s_{i}
, so it suffices to bound
M_{i}^{\rm{in}}
by
C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}s(a_{\rm{str}})\wedge\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}s_{i}
. But the sum on
j
in the definition of
M_{i}^{\rm{in}}
contains at most one term, which corresponds to a previously visited weak vertex
j
and can be bounded by
2\lambda M_{j}
. By the recurrence hypothesis, we can further bound this term by
6\lambda C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}s(a_{\rm{str}})
, which is smaller than
C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}s(a_{\rm{str}})
if
\lambda\leq 1/6
. For small
\lambda
, we also obtain the bound
2\lambda M_{j}\leq\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}\leq\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}s_{i}
by observing that we have
\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}\geq 1/20\kappa_{0}\lambda^{-2}
and recalling that by condition
(H2)
we have
s(a_{\rm{str}})\leq c_{\eqref{cond:H2}}\lambda^{-3+\alpha}
.

Having proved that all visited weak vertices satisfy (
116
), we deduce the following bound for any visited strong vertex
i
.

\displaystyle J_{i}^{\rm{in}}

\displaystyle\leq 3\lambda C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}s(a_{\rm{str}})\leq 3\lambda^{\alpha}C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}c_{\eqref{cond:H2}}\frac{s_{i}}{T_{i}^{\rm{loc}}},

where we used condition
(H2)
to obtain the second inequality. Taking
\lambda
small, we can further bound this by
c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}s_{i}/T_{i}^{\rm{loc}}
. For the term
K_{i}^{\rm{in}}
we obtain similarly a simple bound, namely
2\pi_{a_{\rm{str}}}\leq 2\pi_{i}
. Combining this with the fact that all visited strong vertices are good, we obtain that they all satisfy, at all times
t<\tilde{T}
,

(117)

\displaystyle J_{i}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}s_{i}}{T_{i}^{\rm{loc}}}=2c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}r\kappa_{i}t_{i},

(118)

\displaystyle K_{i}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\left(2+\frac{r}{10}\right)\pi_{i}.

We thus finally obtained bounds that resemble (
102
) and (
103
), and it only remains to explain in detail how we fix the values of
r
,
\mathfrak{c}
,
\varepsilon
,
c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}
.

First, recall we have to fix the values
\mathfrak{c}
,
r
and
\varepsilon
, so that if the scoring function
s
is assumed to satisfy Inequalities (
48
), (
49
) and (
50
) with this value of
\mathfrak{c}
, and if we use this value of
r
in the definition the vertex scores
t_{i}
(and thus of the configuration score
M(t)
), then we can ensure that Inequalities (
102
) and (
103
) are likely to be satisfied up until time
\tilde{T}
. In order to do so, we first request
\mathfrak{c}>8r
and fix a small value of
\varepsilon
so as to have
4r/3\mathfrak{c}+\varepsilon\leq 1/6.
Then, choosing
r
large, we can ensure that we have
(4r-6-\tfrac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}-\varepsilon)/32\geq 2+r/10
, so that (
102
) is implied by (
117
). Now, choosing
c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}=1/12r
, we also have
(\tfrac{1}{3}-\tfrac{4r}{3\mathfrak{c}}-\varepsilon)\geq\tfrac{1}{6}=2c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}r
, so that (
103
) is implied by (
118
).

At this point, we are still free to increase the value of
r
if we keep the request
\mathfrak{c}\geq 8r
and
c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}=1/12r
. We choose the (large) value of
r
and
c_{\eqref{LD_coro_J}}=1/12r
, so as to apply Corollary
6
. Eventually, we fix the large value of
\mathfrak{c}
necessary to apply Corollaries
5
and
6
, fixing at the same time the value
C_{\ref{exponential_moments}}
. We then have a proper definition of bad vertices and can apply Corollary
7
to deduce that each visited strong vertex satisfies (
117
) and (
118
) and thus (
102
) and (
103
), with failure probability bounded by
C_{\ref{LD_outcontributions}}\ell^{3}a^{-2\gamma}t_{0}e^{-c_{\ref{LDBoundsFixedTime}}\lambda^{-\alpha}}
. This bound on
P^{\rm{conn}}
is stretched exponentially small in
\lambda
, and hence negligible compared to
a
as
\lambda\to 0
.
